Applying the polynomial relative theorem to \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:pure-quartic} proves \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:quartic-main}. A harmonic perturbation changes the subleading problem, so neither the baseline's first correction nor the comparison's exponential remainder is carried unchanged into that general family.

\section{OEIS applications and a matching interpretation}\label{pwd:hm:sec:applications}
All identifications in this section use the current displayed definitions or programs as well as exact displayed-prefix checks. Finite prefix agreement alone would not prove a weight identity. Entries were inspected on 4 October 2026; no OEIS or repository record was changed.

\subsection{A216966 and common-opener matching triples}
The continued fraction for A216966 explicitly uses $\lambda_h=h^3$. Its first terms are
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 9,\ 297,\ 24273,\ 3976209,\ 1145032281,\ 530050022073.
\]
A perfect matching on $[2n]$ has an opener at the smaller endpoint of each pair and a closer at the larger. Consider ordered triples of matchings having the same opener set. Scanning the vertices gives a common Dyck path: an opener increments the unmatched-opener count, a closer decrements it, and the count never becomes negative. At a closer of current height $h$, each of the three matchings independently chooses one of its $h$ available earlier openers. Thus the number of decorations of a fixed path is $\prod_hh^{3N_h}$. Conversely these choices produce exactly one ordered triple. Therefore A216966 counts these common-opener matching triples. This elementary interpretation is the cubic analogue of the matching quadruples for pure quartic moments; it does not require any connectedness condition.

Write $D_3=r_3^3=4/I_3^3$ and $C_3=\sqrt{3D_3/(2\pi)}$. Equation~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:pure-cubic} reads
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:A216966}
 \mathrm{A216966}(n)\sim C_3D_3^n(n!)^3n^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
In gamma notation $D_3=192\sqrt3\pi^3/\Gamma(1/3)^9$. These are exactly the already-posted Kot\v e\v sovec constants described in Section~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:results}.

\subsection{A218221 and tetrahedral descent weights}
A218221's displayed finite-fraction program gives
$\lambda_h=\binom{h+2}{3}=h(h+1)(h+2)/6$. Thus $\kappa=1/6$, $a=3$, and the gamma product is $\Gamma(1)\Gamma(2)\Gamma(3)=2$. Its first terms are
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 5,\ 65,\ 1725,\ 81225,\ 6181125,\ 710984625.
\]
The exact leading consequences are
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:A218221-ratio}
 \frac{6^n\mathrm{A218221}(n)}{\mathrm{A216966}(n)}\sim\frac{(r_3n)^3}{2},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:A218221}
 \mathrm{A218221}(n)\sim
 \sqrt{\frac3{8\pi}}\,r_3^{9/2}
 \left(\frac{r_3^3}{6}\right)^n(n!)^3n^{5/2}.
\end{equation}
The amplitude is equivalently
$2^{15/2}3^{11/4}\pi^4/\Gamma(1/3)^{27/2}=0.603824587006556\ldots$, matching the posted formula. This is an application of a rigorous cubic baseline plus polynomial transfer. The Dixon weights begin $4,36,100,252,\ldots$; after scaling the first to one they begin $1,9,25,\ldots$, whereas A218221's weights begin $1,4,10,\ldots$. The parity theorem is a substantive bridge, not an identification of these two S-fractions.

\subsection{Lemniscatic and tree examples in degree four}
The pure quartic moment sequence is A227887, with $\lambda_h=h^4$. Its first terms are
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 17,\ 1585,\ 485729,\ 372281761.
\]
The following three examples are classical elliptic/tree families, used as applications and normalization tests \cite{oeisquartic}.

A144853 identifies its S-fraction coefficients with A187756, namely
\[
 \lambda_h=\frac{h^2(4h^2-1)}3
 =\frac43h^2(h-1/2)(h+1/2).
\]
Here $a=0$ and the gamma product is $\pi/2$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:A144853}
 \mathrm{A144853}(n)\sim\frac2\pi\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,r^2
 \left(\frac43r^4\right)^n(n!)^4n^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
Its initial values are $1,1,21,2541,1023561,1036809081$. A261000 has weights $h^2(4h^2-1)$ and is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:A261000}
 \mathrm{A261000}(n)=3^n\mathrm{A144853}(n).
\end{equation}
This also follows from the entries' identical JacobiSD coefficient formulas with their displayed scale factors. Its interpretation is in unordered even-degree bilabeled increasing trees on $2n+1$ nodes, and its first terms are $1,3,189,68607,82908441$.

A144849 explicitly uses $h\,\mathrm{A139757}(h)/3$, where
$\mathrm{A139757}(h)=(h+1)(2h+1)^2$. Thus
\[
 \lambda_h=\frac{h(h+1)(2h+1)^2}{3}
 =\frac43h(h+1/2)^2(h+1).
\]
Its shift sum is $a=2$ and gamma product is $\pi/4$, giving
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:A144849}
 \mathrm{A144849}(n)\sim\frac4\pi\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,r^4
 \left(\frac43r^4\right)^n(n!)^4n^{3/2}.
\end{equation}
It begins $1,6,336,77616,50916096,76307083776$. The entry gives squared sine-lemniscate and increasing bilabeled strict binary tree interpretations, the latter with $4n+2$ labels. In particular the comparison within this classical family is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:tree-ratio}
 \frac{\mathrm{A144849}(n)}{\mathrm{A144853}(n)}\sim2r^2n^2.
\end{equation}
This explains the exponent and constant uniformly through the shifts. We do not claim that these classical elliptic asymptotics are new.

\subsection{Pure quartic moments are not A338634}
Let $M(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathrm{A227887}(n)z^n$. A338634 is the distinct formal transform $A(x)$ satisfying
\[
 A(x)=M(x/A(x)^2),\qquad M(z)=A(zM(z)^2).
\]
Its positive-index coefficients are the even free cumulants of the symmetric moment sequence with even moments $Z_n(h^4)$ and vanishing odd moments. For example it begins
$1,1,15,1490,472475,367254494$, rather than the raw-moment prefix above. Report 199 treats that transform and its connected noncrossing-closure interpretation. The transfer theorems in this article concern the raw weighted-Dyck moments $Z_n$, and must not be applied to A338634 by silently identifying it with $Z_n(h^4)$.

\section{Classical low-degree normalization checks}\label{pwd:hm:sec:classical}
The values $I_1=2$ and $I_2=\pi/2$ give $r_1=2$ and $r_2=4/\pi$. The following exact families independently test the semilength convention, the harmonic power, and the gamma constant. They are classical benchmarks rather than new targets.

\subsection{Associated Hermite moments}
For $c>-1$, the normalized associated-Hermite recurrence with coefficient $h+c$ has density
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:hermite-density}
 \varrho_c(y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(c+1)|D_{-c}(iy)|^2},\qquad
 Z_n(h+c)=\int_{\mathbb R}y^{2n}\varrho_c(y)\dd y.
\end{equation}
Here $D_\nu$ is the parabolic-cylinder function. The normalization follows by rescaling Askey--Wimp's recurrence
$H_{m+1}=2xH_m-2(m+c)H_{m-1}$ using
$P_m(y)=2^{-m/2}H_m(y/\sqrt2;c)$. The displayed recurrence and orthogonality measure were checked in DLMF 18.30.13--15, which attributes them to Askey--Wimp \cite{dlmf,askeywimp}; the original publisher record, but not its full paper, was inspected. Drake gives the associated-Hermite matching context \cite{drake}.

DLMF 12.9.1 applies along the imaginary axis and gives
\[
 \varrho_c(y)\sim\frac{|y|^{2c}\ee^{-y^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(c+1)}\quad(|y|\to\infty).
\]
For any fixed tail cutoff the compact part of the moment integral is negligible compared with the Gaussian-tail moment. Comparing tails and then evaluating the gamma integral yields
\[
 Z_n(h+c)\sim\frac{2^{n+c}\Gamma(n+c+1/2)}{\sqrt\pi\Gamma(c+1)}.
\]
Since $Z_n(h)=(2n-1)!!=2^n\Gamma(n+1/2)/\sqrt\pi$ exactly,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:hermite-ratio}
 \frac{Z_n(h+c)}{Z_n(h)}\sim\frac{(2n)^c}{\Gamma(1+c)},
\end{equation}
in agreement with \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:one-shift}. There is no uniformity claim as $c\downarrow-1$ or as $c$ grows with $n$.

\subsection{The secant-power family}
For $\beta=1+\alpha>0$, the exact EGF is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:secant-EGF}
 \sum_{n\ge0}Z_n(h(h+\alpha))\frac{t^{2n}}{(2n)!}=(\sec t)^\beta.
\end{equation}
Deb--Sokal \cite[Section 6.3, Eq. (6.24a) at $k=0$]{debsokal} give the continued-fraction normalization, with attribution to Stieltjes and Rogers. Flajolet's weighted-path treatment supplies the general correspondence \cite{flajolet}. The exact identity can also be checked from the addition law
\[
 (\sec(t+u))^\beta=(\sec t)^\beta(\sec u)^\beta
 (1-\tan t\tan u)^{-\beta},
\]
which yields the Jacobi coefficients $h(h+\beta-1)$.

At $R=\pi/2$ the local expansion is
\[
 (\sec t)^\beta=R^{-\beta}(1-t/R)^{-\beta}
 \bigl(1+O((1-t/R)^2)\bigr).
\]
The pole or algebraic singularity at $-R$ contributes equally to an even coefficient; no other singularity is on $|t|=R$. Standard coefficient transfer gives
\[
 [t^{2n}](\sec t)^\beta\sim
 \frac{2R^{-2n-\beta}(2n)^{\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\beta)}.
\]
Dividing by the $\alpha=0$ case proves
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:secant-ratio}
 \frac{Z_n(h(h+\alpha))}{Z_n(h^2)}\sim
 \frac{(4n/\pi)^\alpha}{\Gamma(1+\alpha)}.
\end{equation}
The two nearest singularities and the EGF index $2n$ account for the powers of two. The distinct weights $(h+c)^2$ are not identified with this EGF: $h(h+2c)$ differs from $(h+c)^2$ by $c^2$.

The package includes displayed-prefix checks for the associated-Hermite sequences A000698 with its one-term offset, A167872 and A321963, and the appropriate secant-power sequences A000182, A002105, A326328, A395382 and A395383. These identifications are used only as finite normalization checks, with their sign and scale conventions stored explicitly.

\section{A safeguarded inverse for factorial-power growth}\label{pwd:hm:sec:inverse}
The absolute harmonic and polynomial formulas are useful for locating a large threshold, but their relative $o(1)$ errors do not justify an unconditional rounding rule. The following lemma states exactly what a leading equivalent supplies.

\begin{theorem}[Inclusive factorial-power inverse]\label{pwd:hm:thm:inverse}
Let $(z_n)_{n\ge0}$ be positive and suppose, for fixed $C>0$, $B>0$, $p>0$, and $q\in\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-template}
 z_n\sim CB^n(n!)^pn^q.
\end{equation}
Define $N(Y)=\min\{n\ge0:z_n\ge Y\}$. Set
\[
 R=B^{1/p},\qquad b=q+p/2,\qquad C_{\log}=\log[C(2\pi)^{p/2}].
\]
For sufficiently large $Y$, write $L=\log Y$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-center}
 t=\frac{L}{pW_0(RL/(p\ee))},\qquad
 \xi=t-\frac{b\log t+C_{\log}}{p\log(Rt)},
\end{equation}
where $W_0$ is the principal Lambert branch. There exists a nonnegative radius
$\eta(Y)=o(1/\log t)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:two-ceilings}
 \lceil\xi-\eta(Y)\rceil\le N(Y)\le\lceil\xi+\eta(Y)\rceil.
\end{equation}
The two bounding integers are equal or consecutive for all sufficiently large $Y$. Equivalently, for each fixed $\varepsilon>0$, eventually
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:epsilon-ceilings}
 \left\lceil\xi-\frac\varepsilon{\log t}\right\rceil
 \le N(Y)\le
 \left\lceil\xi+\frac\varepsilon{\log t}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
The onset may depend on the sequence and $\varepsilon$ and is not effective from \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-template} alone.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $z_n=CB^n(n!)^pn^q(1+e_n)$ with $e_n\to0$. Then
\[
 \frac{z_{n+1}}{z_n}=B(n+1)^p(1+1/n)^q\frac{1+e_{n+1}}{1+e_n}
 \sim Bn^p\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Thus for some $n_*$ the sequence is strictly increasing on $n\ge n_*$ and tends to infinity. No global monotonicity assumption is needed. To ensure that the first crossing belongs to this tail, impose the finite-prefix safeguard
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:prefix-safeguard}
 Y>\max_{0\le n\le n_*}z_n.
\end{equation}
It holds automatically for all sufficiently large $Y$, but must be checked or otherwise certified for a concrete threshold.

Stirling's formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:G}
 \log z_n=G(n)+o(1),\qquad
 G(x)=px\log(Rx/\ee)+b\log x+C_{\log}.
\end{equation}
The $O(1/n)$ Stirling term is absorbed into $o(1)$; the unknown $e_n$ need not have a power rate. Let $F$ be piecewise-linear interpolation of $\log z_n$ on $[n_*,\infty)$. It is continuous and strictly increasing. Since
$G''(x)=p/x-b/x^2=O(1/x)$, its own linear-interpolation error is $O(1/x)$. Interpolation of the discrete $o(1)$ term still tends to zero, so
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:interpolation}
 F(x)=G(x)+o(1)\quad(x\to\infty).
\end{equation}
For $Y$ satisfying \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:prefix-safeguard}, let $x_Y$ be the unique real solution of $F(x_Y)=L$. The inclusive convention gives the exact equality
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:exact-ceiling}
 N(Y)=\lceil x_Y\rceil.
\end{equation}
In particular if $Y=z_m$ then $x_Y=m$ and the first crossing is $m$.

The Lambert equation in \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-center} is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:t-L}
 pt\log(Rt/\ee)=L.
\end{equation}
As $Y\to\infty$, $t\to\infty$. Put $d=\xi-t$; the displayed formula gives $d=O(1)$. For $G_0(x)=px\log(Rx/\ee)$, one has $G_0'(x)=p\log(Rx)$ and $G_0''(x)=p/x$. Taylor expansion over the bounded displacement $d$ yields
\[
 G_0(t+d)=L+p\log(Rt)d+O(1/t),\qquad
 b\log(t+d)=b\log t+O(1/t).
\]
The chosen $d$ cancels the logarithmic and constant terms, proving
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:center-residual}
 G(\xi)-L=O(1/t).
\end{equation}
This is the residual of the explicit center, not an estimate for the unknown error in \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-template}.

Comparing $F((1\pm\varepsilon)t)$ with $L$ for fixed small $\varepsilon>0$ in \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:interpolation}--\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:t-L} proves $x_Y\sim t$. Since $F(x_Y)=L$, equations~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:interpolation} and \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:center-residual} give
$G(x_Y)-G(\xi)=o(1)$. On the interval between $x_Y$ and $\xi$,
\[
 G'(x)=p\log(Rx)+b/x\sim p\log t.
\]
The mean-value theorem consequently gives $x_Y-\xi=o(1/\log t)$. Take
$\eta(Y)=|x_Y-\xi|$, or any larger radius of the same order. Monotonicity of ceilings and \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:exact-ceiling} prove \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:two-ceilings} and \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:epsilon-ceilings}. Eventually $2\eta<1$, so the ceilings differ by at most one.
\end{proof}

The theorem is intentionally qualitative. Multiplying the template by
$\exp(1/\log(n+2))$ preserves \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-template}, while its logarithmic perturbation shifts the continuous inverse by order $1/(\log t)^2$. Thus an $o(1/(t\log t))$ pre-rounding radius cannot follow from a leading equivalent alone. Nor may one erase the two-ceiling safeguard near an integer boundary. A concrete certification requires an explicit remainder or exact evaluations near the candidate index, together with the finite-prefix check.

For any family in Corollary~\ref{pwd:hm:cor:broad-residual}, use $C=\mathcal{C}_pr_p^aP_f$, $B=\kappa d_p$, and $q=a-1/2$. For a degree-$d$ polynomial use $p=d$, $C=\mathcal{C}_dr_d^a/\prod_j\Gamma(1+c_j)$, and $B=\kappa r_d^d$. The generic finite-prefix safeguard remains part of the first-crossing statement.

For a cubic polynomial in Theorem~\ref{pwd:hm:thm:polynomial-main}, put
\[
 C=C_Q=\frac{\sqrt{6/\pi}\,I_3^{-3/2}r_3^a}{\prod_j\Gamma(1+c_j)},\quad
 B=r_3^3\kappa,\quad p=3,\quad q=a-1/2.
\]
Then $R=r_3\kappa^{1/3}$, $b=a+1$, and
$C_{\log}=\log[(2\pi)^{3/2}C_Q]$. For a quartic polynomial, use
\[
 C=C_Q=\frac{\sqrt{2/\pi}\,r^{2+a}}{\prod_j\Gamma(1+c_j)},\quad
 B=\kappa r^4,\quad p=4,\quad q=a-1/2,
\]
so $R=r\kappa^{1/4}$, $b=a+3/2$, and $C_{\log}=\log(4\pi^2C_Q)$.

\subsection{A sharper logarithmic envelope for pure powers}
For unscaled pure weights $h^p$, the quantitative baseline supplies a smaller asymptotic radius. It still does not supply effective numerical bounds.

\begin{corollary}[Pure-power logarithmic inverse]\label{pwd:hm:cor:pure-inverse}
Fix $p>0$, write $m_n=Z_n(h^p)$, and define
$N_p(Y)=\min\{n\ge0:m_n\ge Y\}$. In \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-center} take
\[
 R=r_p,\qquad b=(p-1)/2,\qquad C_{\log}=\log[\mathcal{C}_p(2\pi)^{p/2}].
\]
There are constants $C'_p>0$ and $Y_p$ such that for $Y\ge Y_p$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:hm:eq:pure-inverse}
 \left\lceil\xi-\frac{C'_p}{(\log t)^3}\right\rceil
 \le N_p(Y)\le
 \left\lceil\xi+\frac{C'_p}{(\log t)^3}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
The two integers are equal or consecutive eventually. The constants and onset are not supplied effectively.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Appending $UD$ at height zero injects $\Dyck_n$ into $\Dyck_{n+1}$ without changing a weight, since $\lambda_1=1$. For $n\ge1$, the mountain of semilength $n+1$ is a positive-weight path outside that image. Therefore $m_0=m_1=1$ and $m_{n+1}>m_n$ for $n\ge1$. For $Y>1$ there is consequently no finite-prefix ambiguity.

Let $F$ linearly interpolate $\log m_n$ on integers $n\ge1$. Theorem~\ref{pwd:hm:thm:absolute} and Stirling give
\[
 F(x)=G(x)+O_p((\log x)^{-2}),\qquad
 G(x)=px\log(r_px/\ee)+\frac{p-1}{2}\log x+C_{\log}.
\]
At nonintegers the interpolation error of $G$ is $O_p(1/x)$ because $G''(x)=O_p(1/x)$, so the displayed error is unchanged. If $F(x_Y)=\log Y$, strict increase and the inclusive convention give $N_p(Y)=\lceil x_Y\rceil$, including $Y=m_k$.

The same Lambert equation and bounded correction as in Theorem~\ref{pwd:hm:thm:inverse} give $G(\xi)-\log Y=O_p(1/t)$ and $x_Y\sim t$. Since $G'$ is asymptotic to $p\log t$ between $x_Y$ and $\xi$, the mean-value theorem yields
\[
 |x_Y-\xi|=O_p((\log t)^{-3}).
\]
Choose a sufficiently large fixed implied constant and use monotonicity of the ceiling. Once $2C'_p/(\log t)^3<1$, the bounding integers differ by at most one.
\end{proof}
This corollary is stated for $h^p$ itself. Scaling by $\kappa<1$ need not preserve the immediate monotonicity argument, and a concrete scaled-family inverse must retain a finite-prefix safeguard. More importantly, the pure-power logarithmic rate has not been proved for a general harmonic or polynomial perturbation. Those families retain Theorem~\ref{pwd:hm:thm:inverse}'s $o(1/\log t)$ radius. Neither enclosure is an unconditional single-ceiling formula.

\begin{remark}[{[write]} The inverse and the transseries volume, 7~October 2026]\label{pwd:hm:rem:transseries}
Statement by statement, against the volume named in the Guide (its symbols
carry the subscript ``vol'').
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Instance.} The identity~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:exact-ceiling},
$N(Y)=\lceil x_Y\rceil$ with $x_Y$ the root of the piecewise-linear
interpolation $F$ of $\log z_n$ on the increasing tail, is
\file{p0:thm:staircase}(1) for the admissible interpolation $F$ of
\file{p0:def:three-inverses} (with $A_n=\log z_n$); the brackets
\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:two-ceilings}, \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:epsilon-ceilings}
and~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:pure-inverse} then follow from $|x_Y-\xi|\le\eta$ by
monotonicity of the ceiling, the setting of \file{p0:thm:staircase}(2). The
same holds in Corollary~\ref{pwd:hm:cor:pure-inverse}.
\item \emph{Instance.} The centre $t$ of~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-center}
solves~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:t-L}, $pt\log(Rt/\ee)=L$, which is
\file{p0:prop:factorial-core} with $\kappa_\vol=p$, $d_\vol=p\log R-p$:
there $X=L/(\kappa_\vol v)$ with $v=W_0((L/\kappa_\vol)\ee^{d_\vol/\kappa_\vol})
=W_0(RL/(p\ee))$, exactly $t$.
\item \emph{Formal instance, first coefficient.} With $x=t(1+E)$ the core
part of $G$ becomes $pt[\Lambda E+(1+E)\log(1+E)-E]$ with
$\Lambda=\log(Rt)$, the form of \file{p0:thm:core-reversion}
($\Lambda_\vol=\log(Rt)$, $h_\vol(E)=(1+E)\log(1+E)-E$, the remaining terms
of $G$ in $f_\vol$, with $\log t$ in the coefficient ring); its first
coefficient is the displacement $\xi-t=-(b\log t+C_{\log})/(p\log(Rt))$
of~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:inverse-center}. Only formally, with the source's own
residual~\eqref{pwd:hm:eq:center-residual}. The final mean-value step on the
smooth $G$, between $x_Y$ and $\xi$, is \file{p0:thm:backward-error}.
\end{enumerate}
No novelty is claimed for any of these inversions.
\end{remark}

\section{Exact checks and reproducibility}\label{pwd:hm:sec:repro}
The companion archive contains the complete TeX source and PDF, portable Python programs, exact integer and rational data, displayed-source fixtures with provenance, numerical diagnostics, a README, and deterministic file manifests. It redistributes no third-party paper PDF. The mathematical proofs are the preceding arguments; finite checks are independent consistency tests, not substitutes for limiting estimates.

The principal moment computation is a height-state walk recurrence. With $v_s(h)$ the total weight of nonnegative prefixes of length $s$ ending at $h$, start from $v_0(0)=1$ and apply
\[
 v_{s+1}(h+1)\mathrel{+}=v_s(h),\qquad
 v_{s+1}(h-1)\mathrel{+}=\lambda_hv_s(h)\quad(h\ge1).
\]
Then $Z_n=v_{2n}(0)$. A separate first-return recurrence uses elevated moments $M_{a,n}$ for paths based at height $a$:
\[
 M_{a,0}=1,\qquad M_{a,n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{a+1}M_{a+1,k}M_{a,n-1-k}.
\]
An independently truncated formal S-fraction checks the same prefixes. Integer scaling is explicit for rational-weight examples; floating-point arithmetic is not used in these equality tests.

The public exact suite includes:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.6em]
 \item moment prefixes through $n=256$ for A216966, A218221, A227887, A144849, A144853, A261000, and the Dixon comparison, together with a separately computed A338634 transform;
 \item all inspected displayed moment terms, with offsets and signs explicit, and the displayed A187756 weight values; unexamined b-files are not claimed as validated;
 \item independent walk, first-return, and S-fraction prefix comparisons through $n=24$;
 \item exact total-occupation and primitive negative-excursion deletion identities for varied positive rational laws, including irregular laws and positive polynomials with complex or negative nonintegral shifts;
 \item exact two-step conditional-pair identities, retaining the boundary case where $DU$ is infeasible;
 \item Dixon ODE-versus-Dyck coefficients through $n=48$, exact finite product recurrences and gamma-factor algebra, and Gaussian/secant-power formal coefficient checks.
\end{itemize}
A separate Freud companion constructs exact rational gamma moments for integer parameters $p=1,\ldots,6$, obtains monic norms through degree $16$ by rational Hankel factorization, and compares the resulting recurrence S-fraction with the moments through $n=16$. It checks the telescoping products, probability and scale normalizations, Gaussian recurrence, and prefactor algebra. Its default receipt records $760$ finite instances, including $60$ independently checked inclusive thresholds for pure integer powers. Separate high-precision output samples eight fixed powers, including $p=1/4,1/2,3/2$, and records the scaled logarithmic residuals and inverse errors. These diagnostics do not estimate universal constants in the $O_p$ bounds, and the exact algebra does not independently prove the cited Freud coefficient theorem.

The code uses explicit exception checks rather than Python assertions, so its validations remain active under \code{python -O}. Code, public fixtures, and output files use relative package paths; no private research paths are needed. The README records exact instance counts and the finite ranges, rather than equating a count of tests with a proof.

Numerical files are explicitly labeled diagnostics. They evaluate arch constants, pure-power and one-shift occupation means, gamma products, cubic and quartic amplitudes, and inclusive inverse thresholds. Such computations can expose normalization mistakes and slow finite convergence, but they are not interval-certified bounds, effective asymptotic onsets, or proofs of the claimed limits. The low-power tests deliberately include $p=1/2$, where finite convergence can be slow.

The rounding warning is visible in a finite example: at the exact integer threshold $Y=\mathrm{A218221}(128)$, the numerical center is $\xi\approx128.000375119743$, so its unguarded ceiling is $129$, whereas exact comparison against all earlier generated terms gives the inclusive crossing $128$. This demonstrates why the two-ceiling convention matters; it does not determine the theorem's unknown asymptotic radius.

The build validates source and package manifests, regenerates the data and PDF in fresh directories, and makes a deterministic ZIP. A normal replay compares every public member with its recorded release value. The release procedure additionally extracts the actual ZIP and runs the documented builds in both ordinary and optimized Python modes, with the caller's bytecode environment unset, then compares every public member and the complete ZIP bytes. PDF identity is scoped to the documented matching TeX toolchain and font files; arbitrary TeX versions are not asserted to be byte-identical. Guard tests cover the listed malformed inputs, manifests, symlinks, and unsafe destinations; they are a finite rejection suite, not a general security certification.

\section{Source inspection and attribution boundaries}\label{pwd:hm:sec:sources}
The preceding external analytic inputs are specific and checkable. For the general-power baseline, Claeys--Krasovsky--Minakov \cite{ckm} was inspected directly at the fixed-weight definitions and equation (2.3), including the explicit all-$\beta>0$ range. The full March 2023 UCLouvain manuscript, September 2022 arXiv v2, and SISSA postprint agree on the needed formula. The publisher record confirms the 2023 publication; its typeset full body was not the inspected copy. The original Kriecherbauer--McLaughlin full body was not retrieved, so its Theorem 1.5 is cited only as explicitly restated in CKM. The varying-weight ensemble coefficient, a later local estimate restricted to $\beta\ge1$, and the source's separate Hankel-determinant constant problem are not substituted for the fixed-weight leading-coefficient theorem used here.

 The Conrad--Flajolet full PDF was available and its Theorem 2, signs, formal-Laplace convention, and equation (19) were inspected. The \emph{Analytic urns} full PDF was inspected at Theorem 1 (PDF pages 8--9), Theorem 2 and displayed expansion (23) (PDF page 13), and Corollary 1 and its coefficient argument (PDF page 16). Journal and PDF pagination differ. Section~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:dixon} checks the urn parameters, hypotheses, local identification, all dominant singularities, and all residue phases explicitly.

The quartic comparison uses the Berg--Valent full-text formulas (2.31), (3.1), the definition of $K_0$, and Proposition 3.6.1 on pages 194--195, already inspected for Report 199. Only its stated leading normalization and classical moment theorem are used here. Report 199 is a companion result, not a replacement citation for Berg--Valent.

For low-degree checks, the DLMF formulas for associated Hermite and cylinder functions were inspected; the original Askey--Wimp publisher metadata and abstract were available, but its complete original paper was not audited. Drake's arXiv paper and the relevant Deb--Sokal and Flajolet full-text formulas were inspected. Stieltjes and Rogers are historical attributions as recorded in those sources, not unexamined theorem statements invoked to justify a general-$p$ amplitude.

Avdoshkin--Dymarsky's Appendix C already formulates the weighted entropy saddle and equation
$-f''/(1-f'^2)=p/(2f)$ for height scaling $h\approx2nf(t)$. Its parabola for $p=1$ and sine for $p=2$ translate into the arch here by $y(s)=2f(s/2)$. That is prior for the formal geometry; the singular fixed-height occupation regularization and the precise transfer hypothesis in this article are separate steps. Dymarsky--Smolkin's supplement studies the logarithmic moment correction from a constant shift of asymptotically linear Lanczos coefficients. Its $n$-power is consistent with $a=2c$ for $\lambda_h=(h+c)^2$, but the inspected discussion does not supply the exact $\Gamma(1+c)^{-2}$ amplitude under the present hypotheses. These are bounded statements about the inspected portions, not literature-wide absence claims.

For OEIS, A216966 and A218221 already post the equivalents and authorship dates stated earlier, and A216966 also posts \eqref{pwd:hm:eq:posted-general}. The A144849 weight formula is explicit in its continued-fraction definition; A144853 uses A187756's coefficients. Peter Bala's associated A144853 note was available as indexed text, while its direct PDF fetch did not yield a usable file in this inspection. Its contents are corroborative, not needed in place of the explicit entry definitions. The elliptic and tree interpretations are known applications, not a claim of new sequence discovery.

Focused literature searches and a bounded read-only screen of the public ProveIt repository sought the sequence identifiers and weighted-Dyck/harmonic topics. Searches also checked the most relevant existing reports; the nearby peak-weight Newton-edge problem concerns a different model. These checks did not establish an exact duplicate of the present full theorem package, but they cannot prove that no equivalent result exists elsewhere. No first-ever proof or priority claim is made.

\section{What is reusable and what remains open}\label{pwd:hm:sec:questions}
The uniform occupation estimate applies to an entire nonsmooth power class, before any arch is known. Its combination with a concrete Freud moment measure and an exact telescoping norm product proves the already-posted absolute formula for all fixed positive powers, without a new saddle fluctuation determinant. It provides a direct way to separate low-height product constants from the macroscopic contribution of a harmonic tail. The direct interpolated concentration avoids the need for a bounded change of measure. The parity argument isolates the cancellation needed by the cubic comparison without imposing independence. These components can be reused separately, with their stated hypotheses.

Several questions remain outside the results proved here:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em]
 \item Can the pure-power logarithmic remainder be sharpened to a proved inverse-power correction for every fixed $p>0$? The current argument controls the published norm error without differentiating it; an asserted smooth recurrence expansion would require additional justification.
 \item Can harmonic transfer be given an effective rate, including a quantitative shrinking-cutoff occupation estimate? The present fixed-cutoff limit does not determine a universal rate or a numerical onset.
 \item Which residual classes beyond ordinarily convergent series with $r_h=o(1/h)$ admit amplitude transfer, and which oscillatory $1/h$ terms admit cancellation beyond parity? Arbitrary $\ell^1$ residuals and general periodic terms require further arguments.
 \item Can fluctuation theory for the arch yield controlled subleading amplitudes for genuinely shifted cubic and quartic families? A complete raw inverse-power expansion would require substantially more than the leading transfer result.
 \item Can explicit error constants make the two-ceiling inverse a certified finite-threshold method?
\end{enumerate}
\begin{writenote}[7 October 2026]
Under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026 (unproved claims are moved
to questions, wrong ones are refuted on the record), the write found no claim
of this Part that is unproved as stated and none that is wrong. The one
external dependence, the Freud coefficient theorem read only as restated, is
disclosed at the end of Section~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:freud}; to read the original
Kriecherbauer--McLaughlin Theorem~1.5 and confirm the all-$\beta>0$ statement
remains an open task of the same kind as Part~III's Question~1. On
Question~1, Part~III records a conditional first correction
(\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:conditional}); at $p=3$ the write finds it numerically
consistent (note at the end of Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:limits}), which is not
a proof.
\end{writenote}


\part{Quartic Dyck moments and noncrossing cumulants: leading amplitude and first correction for OEIS A338634}\label{pwd:qt:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~199, archive \file{Report199.zip}, delivered as \file{Report199.tex} (22 pages). Delivered title block ``Quartic Dyck moments and noncrossing cumulants / Leading amplitude and first correction for OEIS A338634 / Report 199 / 4 October 2026''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} remarks and notes; Report~199's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+18$ here, with its statements and equations. Its labels carry \file{pwd:qt:}; its citation key \file{oeis} is \file{oeisA338634}. This Part is self-contained.}
\begin{partabstract}
The implicit continued fraction defining OEIS A338634 is the noncrossing cumulant transform of Dyck paths whose descents from height $h$ carry weight $h^4$. We give a concrete interpretation by ordered matching quadruples with a common opener set, and prove the leading equivalent already posted by V\'aclav Kot\v e\v sovec together with its first relative correction $-(1+\pi)/(16n)$. The amplitude comes from a uniform elementary occupation estimate and the exactly solved quartic comparison of Berg and Valent. To identify the correction, we prove a singular-potential limit shape by block counting, an endpoint-safe lower bound, and a calibration argument; a subtracted occupation integral then evaluates to $-\pi/16$. Independently, a cyclic-interval Bonferroni argument gives an expansion to every fixed order in shifted raw moments, with an explicit uniform remainder. This is not an all-orders inverse-power expansion of the raw moments. We also prove the parity criterion and obtain a second explicit Lambert-$W$ inverse center with a little-$o$ two-ceiling threshold envelope. Exact computations and a self-contained reproducibility package accompany the proofs. No effective asymptotic onset, $O(n^{-2})$ raw-moment remainder, unconditional single-ceiling inverse, or absolute priority claim is made.
\end{partabstract}

\section{Definitions and main results}\label{pwd:qt:sec:main}
A Dyck path of semilength $n$ starts at height zero, has $n$ upward and $n$ downward unit steps, stays nonnegative, and ends at zero. Let $N_h(D)$ count its descents from $h$ to $h-1$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:moments}
 m_n=\sum_{D:\,|D|=2n}\prod_{h\ge1}h^{4N_h(D)},\qquad m_0=1,
 \qquad M(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}m_nz^n.
\end{equation}
The first-return decomposition gives the formal Stieltjes fraction
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:sfraction}
 M(z)=\cfrac{1}{1-\cfrac{1^4z}{1-\cfrac{2^4z}{1-\cfrac{3^4z}{\ddots}}}}.
\end{equation}
Let $A(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nx^n$, $a_0=1$, be determined by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:transform}
 A(x)=M\bigl(x/A(x)^2\bigr),\qquad M(z)=A\bigl(zM(z)^2\bigr).
\end{equation}
These equivalent identities define the sequence A338634 \cite{oeisA338634}. All power series here are formal; their coefficients grow too rapidly for positive analytic radii of convergence. In the usual symmetric moment notation, the even moments are $m_n$, the odd moments vanish, and the even free cumulants are $a_n$.

The first coefficients fix the normalization:
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrr}
 n&0&1&2&3&4&5\\ \hline
 m_n&1&1&17&1585&485729&372281761\\
 a_n&1&1&15&1490&472475&367254494
\end{array}
\]
Put
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:constants}
 I=\int_0^1\frac{\dd u}{\sqrt{1-u^4}}
   =\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^2}{4\sqrt{2\pi}},\quad
 r=\frac{\sqrt2}{I}=\frac{8\sqrt\pi}{\Gamma(1/4)^2},\quad
 F(x)=\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,r^{4x+2}\frac{\Gamma(x+1)^4}{\sqrt x}.
\end{equation}
Numerically $r=1.0787052023767587\ldots$.

\begin{theorem}[Amplitude and first correction]\label{pwd:qt:thm:main}
As $n\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:main}
 \frac{m_n}{F(n)}=1-\frac{1+\pi}{16n}+o(n^{-1}),\qquad
 \frac{a_n}{F(n)}=1-\frac{1+\pi}{16n}+o(n^{-1}).
\end{equation}
More precisely, if $b_n$ is the Dyck moment with descent weights
$\gamma_h=h^2(h^2-1/4)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:comparison-main}
 \frac{m_n}{b_n}=\frac\pi2\left(1-\frac\pi{16n}+o(n^{-1})\right).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
The leading equivalent $a_n\sim F(n)$ is already posted on OEIS by Kot\v e\v sovec, dated 12 November 2020. It is a posted formula, not a formula labeled conjectural on the inspected entry. The exact comparison model and its gamma-factor asymptotic are classical results of Berg and Valent \cite{bergvalent}. The work needed here is the amplitude transfer from $\gamma_h$ to $h^4$, the signed correction, and the passage to this cumulant sequence. We make no claim that a bounded literature search establishes the first proof of the equivalent.

\begin{theorem}[Every fixed order in shifted moments]\label{pwd:qt:thm:shifted}
For each fixed integer $K\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:shifted}
 a_n=\sum_{j=0}^K Q_j(n)m_{n-j}
       +O_K\bigl(n^{K+1}m_{n-K-1}\bigr),\qquad
 Q_j(n)=[z^j]M(z)^{-2n}.
\end{equation}
The remainder divided by $m_n$ is $O_K(n^{-3K-3})$. In particular,
\begin{align}\label{pwd:qt:eq:qpolys}
 Q_0(n)&=1,&Q_1(n)&=-2n,\notag\\
 Q_2(n)&=2n^2-33n,&
 Q_3(n)&=-\tfrac43n^3+66n^2-\tfrac{9410}3n.
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
The distinction between \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:main} and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:shifted} is essential. The latter is a finite expansion using the exact shifted values $m_{n-j}$, available for every fixed $K$. It does not assert that $m_n/F(n)$ has an all-orders expansion in powers of $1/n$. It is a sequence-specific rapidly-growing-series calculation of the Bender--Richmond type, with a self-contained tail proof, rather than a new general inversion principle \cite{bender,benderrichmond,borinsky}.

Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:combinatorics} identifies $a_n$ as the number of common-opener matching quadruples whose least noncrossing closure has one block. Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:parity} proves that $a_n$ is odd precisely for $n=0$ or a positive power of two. Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:inverse} gives an explicit refined inverse: its pre-rounding uncertainty is $o(1/(t\log t))$ on the Lambert-$W$ scale $t$. The two ceilings are necessary because no separation from integer boundaries is assumed.

\begin{remark}[{[write]} The OEIS entry, quoted, and its conjecture proved, 7~October 2026]\label{pwd:qt:rem:oeis}
OEIS A338634, read on 7~October 2026 in the internal format, is at revision
\#9 (12~November 2020), author Paul D.~Hanna (4~November 2020), offset~0; its
name is ``G.f. A(x) satisfies: 1 = A(x) - x/(A(x) - 2\^{}4*x/(A(x) -
3\^{}4*x/(A(x) - 4\^{}4*x/(A(x) - 5\^{}4*x/(A(x) - 6\^{}4*x/(A(x) - ...
)))))), a continued fraction relation.'' Its formula lines include
the unattributed line ``For n > 0, a(n) is odd iff n is a power of 2
(conjecture).'' and Kot\v e\v sovec's ``a(n) \textasciitilde{} sqrt(2/Pi) *
(8*sqrt(Pi) / Gamma(1/4)\^{}2)\^{}(4*n + 2) * (n!)\^{}4 / sqrt(n).'' (12~November
2020), which is the leading term of Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:main}. \emph{The
parity conjecture is proved} by Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:parity}: modulo~2 the
Stieltjes fraction collapses to $M=(1-z)^{-1}$, and $A^2+A=x$ over
$\mathbb F_2$ forces $A=1+\sum_{j\ge0}x^{2^j}$; this is the exact posted
statement ($n>0$, with $1=2^0$ a power of two). The source did not retrieve
the b-file ($0\le n\le130$, Hanna); the write did, computed $a_n$ for
$n\le130$ by the Lagrange formula~\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:lagrange} from its own
$m_n$, found all 131 terms equal, and found the odd terms for $1\le n\le130$
exactly at $n=1,2,4,8,16,32,64,128$. The moments $m_n$ are A227887, whose
b-file also agrees. Nothing was submitted to the OEIS.
\end{remark}

\section{Matching quadruples and noncrossing closure}\label{pwd:qt:sec:combinatorics}
A perfect matching on $[2n]=\{1,\ldots,2n\}$ has an \emph{opener} at the smaller endpoint of each edge and a closer at the larger endpoint. Consider ordered quadruples of perfect matchings with the same opener set. Scanning the vertices gives a common Dyck path: an opener raises the number of unmatched openers and a closer lowers it. At a closer of current height $h$, each matching independently chooses one of its $h$ unmatched earlier openers. Thus a fixed path has exactly $\prod_hh^{4N_h}$ decorations. This proves that $m_n$ counts all such quadruples.

A partition of an ordered set is noncrossing if there are no two distinct blocks containing $a<c$ and $b<d$, respectively, with $a<b<c<d$. Take the least noncrossing partition of $[2n]$ that contains every edge of all four matchings within a block. It can be constructed by first joining the endpoints of every edge and then repeatedly merging any two crossing blocks. This process terminates at the unique least such partition: every merge is forced in any noncrossing coarsening.

This is different from ordinary connectivity of the multigraph formed by the four matchings. For example, four copies of the matching $\{(1,3),(2,4)\}$ have two graph components, but their noncrossing closure has a single block. Counting graph-connected quadruples would give the wrong sequence.

\begin{proposition}\label{pwd:qt:prop:count}
For $n\ge1$, $a_n$ counts the ordered common-opener matching quadruples on $[2n]$ whose least noncrossing closure has one block. In particular $0\le a_n\le m_n$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every closure block has even size, since no matching edge leaves it. Restrict the four matchings to one block and standardize the inherited linear order. Their opener sets remain equal, and their noncrossing closure on the block is connected; otherwise the original closure could be refined. Conversely, decorate each block of an even noncrossing partition by such a connected quadruple. The union has the common opener property, and its least noncrossing closure is the chosen partition.

Let $c_k$ count connected decorations of size $2k$. In the block containing vertex $1$, the $2k$ vertices leave $2k$ ordered gaps, each independently filled by an arbitrary object. Thus
\[
 M(z)=1+\sum_{k\ge1}c_kz^kM(z)^{2k}.
\]
The formal recursion uniquely determines the $c_k$ and agrees with \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:transform}; hence $c_k=a_k$.
\end{proof}
The same elementary interpretation works for $d$ ordered common-opener matchings with weights $h^d$, for any positive integer $d$. This observation is combinatorial only; it does not extend the quartic asymptotic theorem to a general family of weights. The moment--cumulant decomposition belongs to the established noncrossing framework; broad relations with Jacobi parameters and tree enumerations are discussed in \cite{mlotkowski,defantlee}.

Lagrange inversion in $u=zA(u)^2$ gives the exact coefficient formula
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:lagrange}
 a_n=\frac1{1-2n}[z^n]M(z)^{1-2n},\qquad n\ge1.
\end{equation}
Indeed $x=zM(z)^2$, so the standard change-of-variable coefficient identity applied to $A(x)=M(z)$ gives \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:lagrange}. This identity is also useful for exact computation, but by itself does not justify truncating the asymptotic expansion: a uniform bound for the discarded configurations is still needed.

\section{Elementary moment bounds and convolution tails}\label{pwd:qt:sec:bounds}
\begin{lemma}\label{pwd:qt:lem:ratios}
For $R_n=m_n/m_{n-1}$ and $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:ratios}
 (n/\ee)^4\le R_n\le4n^4,\qquad
 (n!)^4\le m_n\le C_n(n!)^4\le4^n(n!)^4,
\end{equation}
where $C_n$ is the $n$th Catalan number.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The mountain $U^nD^n$ has weight $(n!)^4$. The $j$th descent of any path has height at most $n-j+1$, so no path has larger weight. This proves the bounds on $m_n$.

Use the symmetric tridiagonal matrix $J$ with zero diagonal and edge entries $J_{h-1,h}=J_{h,h-1}=h^2$. A closed walk of length $2n$ has an upward and a downward traversal for each edge pair, so
$m_n=\langle e_0,J^{2n}e_0\rangle=\|J^ne_0\|^2$.
All statements use finite truncations large enough to contain the paths in question. Cauchy--Schwarz gives $m_n^2\le m_{n-1}m_{n+1}$; consequently $R_n\ge m_n^{1/n}\ge(n/\ee)^4$. Truncate to heights $0,\ldots,n$. Its maximum row sum is at most $2n^2$, so its operator norm is at most $2n^2$. Applying this to $J^{n-1}e_0$ gives $m_n\le4n^4m_{n-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform convolution tail]\label{pwd:qt:lem:tail}
For every fixed positive integer $q$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:tail}
 \sum_{k=q}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}m_km_{n-k}=O_q(m_{n-q}).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $f_k=m_km_{n-k}$. For $k\le n/10$, \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:ratios} yields, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\[
 \frac{f_{k+1}}{f_k}\le4\ee^4\left(\frac{k+1}{n-k}\right)^4\le\frac12.
\]
This part sums to at most $2m_qm_{n-q}$. In the central range $n/10\le k\le n/2$, the factorial upper bound for $m_k$ and the ratio lower bound for $m_n/m_{n-k}$ give
\[
 \frac{f_k}{m_n}\le\frac{(4\ee^4)^k}{\binom nk^4}
 \le(n+1)^4\exp\{n[\alpha\log(4\ee^4)-4\mathcal H(\alpha)]\},
\]
where $\alpha=k/n$ and $\mathcal H(\alpha)=-\alpha\log\alpha-(1-\alpha)\log(1-\alpha)$. We used $\binom nk\ge(n+1)^{-1}\exp(n\mathcal H(k/n))$. The exponent as a function of $\alpha$ is convex, and is negative at both endpoints $1/10$ and $1/2$; at $1/2$ it equals $2-3\log2<0$. Thus the entire central sum is exponentially small compared with $m_n$. Finally $m_{n-q}\ge m_n/(4^qn^{4q})$ by the upper ratio bound, absorbing that tail into \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:tail}.
\end{proof}

\section{The shifted moment expansion with a uniform remainder}\label{pwd:qt:sec:shifted}
We prove Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:shifted} by inclusion--exclusion over small interval blocks. This supplies the global error control that a formal ``one large index'' calculation would omit.

View the $2n$ vertices cyclically. Fix $K\ge1$. For each cyclic interval $I$ of $2k$ vertices, $1\le k\le K$, let $E_I$ be the event that $I$ is exactly one block of the least noncrossing closure. Its internal decoration has $a_k$ choices. Distinct such events can coexist only for disjoint intervals. Conversely, connected decorations on disjoint cyclic intervals coexist with an arbitrary object on the remaining vertices. The standardization on every subset always uses its inherited \emph{linear} order. This argument does not require a matching law invariant under cyclic rotation.

Every nontrivial noncrossing partition has at least two cyclic interval blocks, often called ears. To see this, inspect the cyclic gaps of any block. If only one gap is nonempty, that block is an ear and an innermost block in the remaining gap is another. If at least two gaps are nonempty, each contains an innermost interval block. Hence there is an ear containing at most half the vertices. A disconnected object with none of the $E_I$ for $k\le K$ therefore has an ear of semilength between $K+1$ and $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$. Its total count is at most
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:noear}
 2n\sum_{k=K+1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}a_km_{n-k}
 \le2n\sum_{k=K+1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}m_km_{n-k}
 =O_K(nm_{n-K-1}).
\end{equation}
The same estimate with $K=0$ already gives $a_n=m_n+O(nm_{n-1})$, proving Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:shifted} for $K=0$.

For $\ell$ disjoint small intervals of total semilength $j<n$, the intersection sum is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:placement}
 m_{n-j}\frac{2n}{\ell}
 \binom{2n-2j+\ell-1}{\ell-1}
 [u^j]\bigl(A_{\le K}(u)-1\bigr)^\ell,
 \quad A_{\le K}(u)=1+\sum_{k=1}^Ka_ku^k.
\end{equation}
Choose the starting vertex in $2n$ ways, order the intervals cyclically, and distribute $2n-2j$ unused vertices among $\ell$ possibly empty gaps. Divide by $\ell$ for the interval chosen to start the cyclic list. Ordered sizes and decorations give the coefficient in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:placement}. Adjacent interval blocks are allowed. For $j\le K$ the truncation of $A$ is irrelevant; for larger $j$, replacing it by full $A$ is only an upper bound.

Apply Bonferroni through intersection order $K$. The remainder is bounded by the sum of $(K+1)$-fold intersections. Their total semilength lies in the fixed range $K+1\le j\le K(K+1)$. Formula~\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:placement} is $O_K(n^{K+1}m_{n-j})$, and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:ratios} makes the sum $O_K(n^{K+1}m_{n-K-1})$. Among retained intersections $\ell\le K$, the terms with $j>K$ satisfy the same bound. The event of avoiding all small interval blocks differs from connectedness only by \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:noear}. Thus the coefficient of $m_{n-j}$ for $1\le j\le K$ is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:pj}
 P_j(n)=\sum_{\ell=1}^j(-1)^\ell\frac{2n}{\ell}
 \binom{2n-2j+\ell-1}{\ell-1}[u^j](A(u)-1)^\ell.
\end{equation}

For $n>j$, Lagrange inversion in $u=zA(u)^2$ gives
\[
 [z^j]M(z)^{-2n}
 =\frac{n}{n-j}[u^j]A(u)^{-2(n-j)}.
\]
Expanding the last power gives exactly \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:pj}. This proves \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:shifted}, including its uniform remainder. Applying the lower ratio bound $K+1$ times shows
$n^{K+1}m_{n-K-1}/m_n=O_K(n^{-3K-3})$. The displayed polynomials \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:qpolys} follow by finite expansion of $M(z)^{-2n}$, using $m_1=1,m_2=17,m_3=1585$.

In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:cumulant-transfer}
 \frac{a_n}{m_n}=1+O(n^{-3}),\qquad
 \frac{a_n}{m_n}=1-2n\frac{m_{n-1}}{m_n}+O(n^{-6}).
\end{equation}
After the moment asymptotic is known, $m_{n-1}/m_n\sim r^{-4}n^{-4}$, so $a_n/m_n=1-2r^{-4}n^{-3}+o(n^{-3})$. This last consequence concerns the cumulant-to-moment ratio; it does not add higher raw-moment corrections to \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:main}.

\section{The classical quartic comparison and its normalization}\label{pwd:qt:sec:classical}
Let $b_n$ be the Dyck moments with weights $\gamma_h=h^2(h^2-1/4)$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:k0}
 K_0=\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^2}{4\sqrt\pi}=\sqrt2 I.
\end{equation}
Berg and Valent \cite[Eq.~(3.1), Prop.~3.6.1]{bergvalent} study the birth and death rates
\[
 \lambda_j=(4j+1)(4j+2)^2(4j+3),\qquad
 \mu_j=(4j-1)(4j)^2(4j+1),\quad \mu_0=0.
\]
Their moment sequence $s_n$ has contracted Jacobi coefficients
$\lambda_j+\mu_j$ and $\lambda_j\mu_{j+1}$, in their notation (2.31).
To track the normalization directly, the alternating Stieltjes coefficients are
\[
 \alpha_{2j+1}=\lambda_j,\quad \alpha_{2j}=\mu_j,
 \qquad \alpha_h=4h^2(4h^2-1)=16\gamma_h.
\]
The standard contraction of an S-fraction has diagonal entries
$\alpha_{2j}+\alpha_{2j+1}$ (with $\alpha_0=0$) and squared off-diagonal entries
$\alpha_{2j+1}\alpha_{2j+2}$. These agree term by term with the birth--death coefficients above. Scaling every descent weight by $16$ multiplies every moment of semilength $n$ by $16^n$, so $b_n=16^{-n}s_n$.

Their Proposition~3.6.1, on pages 194--195, gives directly
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:bv}
 s_n=\frac{4\Gamma(4n+2)}{K_0^{4n+2}}
        \bigl(1+O(25^{-n})\bigr),\qquad
 b_n=\frac{4\Gamma(4n+2)}{16^nK_0^{4n+2}}
        \bigl(1+O(25^{-n})\bigr).
\end{equation}
This is the classical source input in our proof. It avoids any dependence on a separately normalized integral density. Chen--Ismail's model \cite{chenismail}, reproduced in \cite{dai}, supplies a corroborating equivalent comparison recurrence, not a replacement of $\gamma_h$ by $h^4$.

Stirling expansion in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:bv} yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:stirling-b}
 \frac\pi2b_n=F(n)\left(1-\frac1{16n}+O(n^{-2})\right).
\end{equation}
For clarity, the $1/n$ term in $\log\Gamma(4n+2)$ after its leading terms is $13/48$, while that in $4\log\Gamma(n+1)$ is $1/3=16/48$. Their difference is $-1/16$; the remaining constants give precisely \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:constants}. The $O(n^{-2})$ in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:stirling-b} pertains to the solved comparison $b_n$, not to the target raw moments $m_n$.

\section{Uniform occupation near the lower boundary}\label{pwd:qt:sec:occupation}
The amplitude comparison requires more than an exponential growth rate. We prove an occupation estimate that is uniform over a whole bounded class of quartic weights.

For any positive weights $w=(w_h)_{h\ge1}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:weight-class}
 \tfrac34h^4\le w_h\le h^4,
\end{equation}
write $Z_n=\sum_D\prod_hw_h^{N_h(D)}$, and use its normalized weighted law $\Prob_{n,w}$ and expectation $\E_{n,w}$. Let $H(D)$ be the maximum height. Define
\[
 u_n(h)=\E_{n,w}N_h,\qquad p_n(h)=\Prob_{n,w}(H\ge h),
 \qquad u_N(j)=0\quad(j>N).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Uniform lower-edge occupation]\label{pwd:qt:thm:occupation}
Uniformly over all weights in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:weight-class},
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:occupation}
 \E_{n,w}|N_h-1|\le15552(h/n)^4,
 \qquad n\ge64,\quad 1\le h\le n/64.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\subsection{An all-length deletion ratio}
Finite symmetric Jacobi matrices with off-diagonal entries $\sqrt{w_h}$ give log-convexity of $Z_n$, just as in Lemma~\ref{pwd:qt:lem:ratios}. Convexity of $\log Z_n$ and $Z_0=1$ imply, for all $0\le k\le n$,
\[
 Z_{n-k}\le Z_n^{(n-k)/n}.
\]
The mountain and $n!\ge(n/\ee)^n$, $\ee<3$, give
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:allk}
 Z_n\ge(\tfrac34)^n(n!)^4\ge(n^4/108)^n,
 \qquad \frac{Z_{n-k}}{Z_n}\le(108/n^4)^k.
\end{equation}
This is a fixed-$n$ convexity chord bound. Multiplying less well-adapted one-step bounds would introduce a factorial loss that we do not need.

\subsection{Exact negative excursion deletion}
Let $e_{h,k}$ be the total weight of paths of length $2k$ that start at $h$, immediately descend, stay in $\{0,\ldots,h-1\}$ until their final step, and finally rise to $h$. These are primitive negative excursions at height $h$. Every descent has height at most $h$, and there are at most $C_{k-1}$ such paths, whence
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:exc-bound}
 0\le e_{h,k}\le4^kh^{4k}.
\end{equation}
Every descent across edge $h$ except the last is followed by a first return to $h$. Delete this entire negative excursion. Conversely, insert any such excursion at any vertex of height $h$ in a shorter Dyck path. All other descent heights are unchanged. For $h>0$, the number of height-$h$ vertices equals $N_h+N_{h+1}$: arrivals at height $h$ are either upward traversals of edge $h$ or downward traversals of edge $h+1$. Thus the marked bijection gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:exc-identity}
 u_n(h)-p_n(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-h}e_{h,k}\frac{Z_{n-k}}{Z_n}
             \bigl(u_{n-k}(h)+u_{n-k}(h+1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
The remaining path must reach $h$, explaining the cutoff $n-h$. Empty sums are allowed. With $a(n,h)=432(h/n)^4$, \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:allk}--\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:exc-bound} imply
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:recursion}
 u_n(h)\le1+\sum_{k=1}^{n-h}a(n,h)^k
                   \bigl(u_{n-k}(h)+u_{n-k}(h+1)\bigr).
\end{equation}

\subsection{A bounded occupation cone}
We first show
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:cone}
 u_n(h)\le12\qquad(1\le h\le n/16).
\end{equation}
Expand \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:recursion} recursively while the current pair $(N,j)$ lies in the outer cone $j\le N/8$. Stop a branch on leaving this cone and bound its terminal value $u_N(j)$ by $N\le n$. The expansion is finite since $N$ decreases at every recursion. Inside the cone,
\[
 a(N,j)\le\eta:=\frac{432}{8^4}=\frac{27}{256},\qquad
 \theta:=3\eta=\frac{81}{256}<\frac13.
\]
For a chain with $q\ge1$ steps and total size decrease $s=k_1+\cdots+k_q$, its coefficient is at most $\eta^s$. There are at most $2^q\binom{s-1}{q-1}$ choices: a composition of $s$, and at each step either retaining or increasing the height index. Since
\[
 \sum_{q=1}^s2^q\binom{s-1}{q-1}=2\cdot3^{s-1},
\]
the total contribution of the constant terms at internal nodes is at most
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:internal}
 1+\sum_{s\ge1}2\cdot3^{s-1}\eta^s
   =1+\frac{2\eta}{1-3\eta}=\frac{229}{175}<\frac43.
\end{equation}
An exit chain starting with $h\le n/16$ terminates at $(n-s,h+t)$ for $0\le t\le q\le s$. The exit condition $8(h+t)>n-s$ and $8h\le n/2$ imply $9s>n/2$. Thus the sum of all terminal contributions is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:exit}
 \frac{2n}{3}\frac{\theta^{\lfloor n/18\rfloor+1}}{1-\theta}
 \le\frac{2}{3(1-\theta)}n\theta^{n/18}<9.
\end{equation}
For the final inequality, $2/[3(1-\theta)]<1$ and the maximum of $x\theta^{x/18}$ for real $x\ge0$ is $18/[\ee\log(1/\theta)]<9$. A child with $j>N$ contributes zero. Counting unrestricted compositions overcounts only nonnegative terms, so is harmless. Equations~\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:internal}--\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:exit} imply $u_n(h)<11$, proving the safe bound \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:cone}.

\subsection{Proof of the occupation theorem}
Assume $n\ge64$, $h\le n/64$. For $k\le n/2$,
\[
 h+1\le n/32\le(n-k)/16,
\]
so both child occupations in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:exc-identity} are at most $12$. For $k>n/2$ use the bound $n$ for each. With $a=432(h/n)^4<1/4$,
\begin{align}\label{pwd:qt:eq:occupation-excess}
 0\le u_n(h)-p_n(h)
 &\le\frac{24a}{1-a}+\frac{2na^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}}{1-a}\notag\\
 &\le\frac{26a}{1-a}<35a.
\end{align}
Here $n4^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\le1$ for $n\ge2$. Paths with maximum below $h$ have at most $4^n$ possible shapes and every descent weight at most $h^4$, giving by \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:allk}
\[
 1-p_n(h)\le\bigl(432(h/n)^4\bigr)^n=a^n\le a.
\]
Pathwise,
$|N_h-1|=(N_h-\mathbf1_{\{H\ge h\}})+\mathbf1_{\{H<h\}}$.
Combining these estimates proves \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:occupation} with $36\cdot432=15552$.

\begin{writenote}[Part~I's counterpart, 7 October 2026]
This section is the case $p=4$, with the class $\frac34h^4\le w_h\le h^4$ and
the explicit constant $15552$, of Part~I's uniform endpoint estimate
(Theorem~\ref{pwd:hm:thm:endpoint}, proved in
Sections~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:ratios}--\ref{pwd:hm:sec:cone} by the same deletion
identity and cone expansion). It is kept because its explicit constants and
range are cited by the later sections of this Part.
\end{writenote}

\section{Amplitude transfer by logarithmic interpolation}\label{pwd:qt:sec:amplitude}
For $0\le t\le1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:interpolation}
 f_h=-\log\left(1-\frac1{4h^2}\right),\qquad
 w_h(t)=\gamma_h\ee^{tf_h},\qquad Z_n(t)=Z_n(w(t)).
\end{equation}
Then $Z_n(0)=b_n$, $Z_n(1)=m_n$, and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:weight-class} holds uniformly in $t$. Since each partition function is a finite positive sum, differentiation is exact:
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:logderivative}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log Z_n(t)=\sum_{h=1}^nf_hu_{n,t}(h),
 \qquad 0<f_h\le\frac1{3h^2}.
\end{equation}
Put $L=\lfloor n/64\rfloor$. The occupation theorem gives
\[
 \left|\sum_{h\le L}f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1)\right|
 \le\frac{15552}{3n^4}\sum_{h\le L}h^2=O(n^{-1}).
\]
Since $\sum_hu_{n,t}(h)=n$, the two tails satisfy
\[
 \sum_{h>L}f_hu_{n,t}(h)\le\frac{n}{3(L+1)^2}=O(n^{-1}),
 \qquad \sum_{h>L}f_h\le\frac1{3L}=O(n^{-1}).
\]
In the second sum the deterministic baseline extends over \emph{all} $h>L$, including $h>n$. Hence, uniformly in $t$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:rough-derivative}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log Z_n(t)=\sum_{h\ge1}f_h+O(n^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Euler's sine product at $1/2$ evaluates the convergent sum:
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:euler}
 \prod_{h\ge1}\left(1-\frac1{4h^2}\right)=\frac2\pi,
 \qquad \sum_{h\ge1}f_h=\log(\pi/2).
\end{equation}
Integrating \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:rough-derivative} gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:rough-comparison}
 \log\frac{m_n}{b_n}=\log(\pi/2)+O(n^{-1}),\qquad
 \log\frac{m_n}{Z_n(t)}=(1-t)\log(\pi/2)+O(n^{-1}),
\end{equation}
with a uniform error. In particular \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:stirling-b} already proves $m_n/F(n)=1+O(n^{-1})$. Identifying its coefficient requires signed occupation information at macroscopic heights.

A useful consequence of the same uniform estimate is a change-of-measure bound. Since $w_h(t)\le h^4$ pathwise, for every event $B$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:RN}
 \Prob_{n,t}(B)\le\frac{m_n}{Z_n(t)}\Prob_{n,1}(B)
 \le C_0\Prob_{n,1}(B)
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $n$, with a constant $C_0$ independent of $t$ and $B$. Thus concentration for the quartic law transfers uniformly along the entire interpolation. A pointwise limit in $t$ alone would not justify the integration used later.

\section{The quartic limit shape with singular potential}\label{pwd:qt:sec:shape}
For a Dyck path of length $2n$, linearly interpolate its heights and set
\[
 y_n(s)=\frac{\text{height at step }ns}{n},\qquad 0\le s\le2.
\]
The path belongs to the compact set $\K$ of nonnegative $1$-Lipschitz functions on $[0,2]$ that vanish at both endpoints. Every $y\in\K$ satisfies $y(s)\le\min(s,2-s)\le1$. Define the binary slope entropy
\[
 E(v)=-\frac{1+v}{2}\log\frac{1+v}{2}
       -\frac{1-v}{2}\log\frac{1-v}{2},\qquad -1\le v\le1,
\]
with $0\log0=0$, and the functionals
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:functional}
 S(y)=\int_0^2E(y'(s))\dd s,\qquad
 P(y)=2\int_0^2\log y(s)\dd s,\qquad \Phi(y)=S(y)+P(y).
\end{equation}
The logarithmic integral is understood in the extended Lebesgue sense and may be $-\infty$. Its coefficient follows from an exact identity: if $H_j$ is the larger endpoint height of step $j$, upward and downward crossings of each edge balance, so
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:edge-weight}
 \log\frac{\prod_hh^{4N_h}}{n^{4n}}
 =2\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\log(H_j/n).
\end{equation}
The following argument is an elementary sample-path entropy proof, specialized to a singular logarithmic potential and exact Dyck endpoints. It sits within the classical sample-path large-deviation tradition \cite{mogulskii}; no external theorem with unchecked singular-potential hypotheses is invoked.

\subsection{The weighted upper bound}
For a finite partition $Q=(0=s_0<\cdots<s_M=2)$ put
\[
 S_Q(y)=\sum_i(s_i-s_{i-1})E\left(\frac{y(s_i)-y(s_{i-1})}{s_i-s_{i-1}}\right).
\]
These functionals are continuous in the uniform topology. For successive dyadic refinements $Q_j$, concavity and differentiation of Lipschitz functions imply $S_{Q_j}\downarrow S$: the averages of $y'$ converge almost everywhere to $y'$, and $E$ is bounded and continuous. For $0<\varepsilon\le1$, put
\[
 P_\varepsilon(y)=2\int_0^2\log\max(y(s),\varepsilon)\dd s.
\]
These are continuous and decrease to $P$ as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Thus $\Phi$ is upper semicontinuous on $\K$.

Let $B\subseteq\K$ be closed. Round the partition times $ns_i$ to integer step indices. There are at most $(n+1)^{M-1}$ possible vectors of block-end heights. A block of length $L$ and displacement $d$ has $\binom{L}{(L+d)/2}$ unrestricted bridges when parity is feasible; their number is at most $\exp(LE(d/L))$. Restricting to nonnegative paths or to $B$ can only reduce this number. For fixed $Q$, time rounding changes the normalized block entropy by $o(1)$ uniformly, using uniform continuity of $E$.

On each step interval, $H_j/n$ differs from the interpolated height by at most $1/n$. Since $x\mapsto\log\max(x,\varepsilon)$ is $1/\varepsilon$-Lipschitz, \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:edge-weight} is at most $nP_\varepsilon(y_n)+O(1/\varepsilon)$. Within each feasible endpoint vector, take the largest cutoff potential among paths actually in $B$. The common entropy bound and this supremum give
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:upper-fixed}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\left(n^{-4n}
    \operatorname{weight}_{\beta}\{y_n\in B\}\right)
 \le\sup_{y\in B}\bigl(S_Q(y)+P_\varepsilon(y)\bigr),
 \quad\beta_h=h^4.
\end{equation}
The polynomial number of vectors contributes only $O(M\log n)=o(n)$ to the logarithm.

Choose nested dyadic $Q_j$ and $\varepsilon_j\downarrow0$. On the compact set $B$, a decreasing sequence of continuous functions has maxima converging to the maximum of its pointwise upper-semicontinuous limit. Indeed, choose maximizing points, pass to a convergent subsequence, and bound by each fixed earlier function before sending its index to infinity. This argument also covers a limit of $-\infty$; the empty case is immediate. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:upper}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\left(n^{-4n}
    \operatorname{weight}_{\beta}\{y_n\in B\}\right)
 \le\sup_{y\in B}\Phi(y).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Calibration of the unique maximizer}
Let $H=1/I$. Define $y_*$ on $[0,1]$ by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:arch}
 s=\int_0^{y_*(s)}\frac{\dd v}{\sqrt{1-(v/H)^4}},
\end{equation}
and reflect about $s=1$. Since $HI=1$, the arch reaches $H$ at time $1$. It belongs to $\K$, is positive on $(0,2)$, and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:shape-ode}
 (y_*')^2=1-(y_*/H)^4,\qquad y_*''=-2y_*^3/H^4.
\end{equation}
The second equation extends continuously across the top. At the endpoints, $y_*(s)/s\to1$ and $y_*(s)/(2-s)\to1$, respectively.

Set
\[
 V(s)=E'(y_*'(s))=\frac12\log\frac{1-y_*'(s)}{1+y_*'(s)},\qquad0<s<2.
\]
Direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:calibration}
 V'(s)=\frac2{y_*(s)},\qquad
 V(s)=O\bigl(1+|\log s|+|\log(2-s)|\bigr).
\end{equation}
For every $y\in\K$, concavity gives the tangent inequalities
\[
 E(y')-E(y_*')\le V(y'-y_*'),\qquad
 2(\log y-\log y_*)\le\frac{2(y-y_*)}{y_*}.
\]
Integrate first on $[\delta,2-\delta]$ and integrate by parts. Equation~\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:calibration} cancels the interior terms. The remaining boundary expression
$[V(y-y_*)]_{\delta}^{2-\delta}$ tends to zero, because both paths are bounded by $\min(s,2-s)$ and $\delta|\log\delta|\to0$. All tangent terms are integrable: $V$ is integrable, slopes are bounded, and $|y-y_*|/y_*$ is bounded near each endpoint. If $P(y)=-\infty$ the conclusion is automatic; otherwise passage to the endpoints is justified and gives $\Phi(y)\le\Phi(y_*)$.

The logarithmic tangent deficit is
\[
 2\int_0^2\left[\frac{y}{y_*}-1-\log\frac{y}{y_*}\right]\dd s.
\]
It is strictly positive (or infinite) if the continuous functions differ, since then they differ on an interval of positive measure. The entropy deficit is also nonnegative. Thus $y_*$ is the unique maximizer. Compactness and upper semicontinuity imply, for every fixed $\eta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:gap}
 \sup_{\|y-y_*\|_\infty\ge\eta}\Phi(y)<\Phi(y_*).
\end{equation}
As a normalization check, the identity
$E(v)-vE'(v)=\log2-\tfrac12\log(1-v^2)$ gives
$E(y_*')+2\log y_*=y_*'V+\log(2H^2)$. Since $y_*V$ vanishes at the endpoints, integration and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:calibration} give
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:phi-value}
 \Phi(y_*)=-4+2\log(2H^2)=4\log r-4.
\end{equation}
This agrees with the exponential rate of the classical factorial template.

\subsection{A lower bound that retains the endpoints}
We now prove the matching lower bound without assuming an unbounded-potential form of a general large-deviation theorem. Approximate $y_*$ by positive polygonal paths $z\in\K$ that equal $s$ on $[0,\varepsilon]$ and $2-s$ on $[2-\varepsilon,2]$, and have all interior slopes strictly between $-1$ and $1$.

One construction replaces the initial segment by the line to $(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$, joins it to $(2\varepsilon,y_*(2\varepsilon))$, then uses fine chords of $y_*$, with a reflected end modification. For small $\varepsilon$,
$\varepsilon<y_*(2\varepsilon)<2\varepsilon$, so the joining slope is strictly feasible. Interior chords are also strictly feasible. These approximations converge uniformly, their derivatives converge almost everywhere, and their heights are bounded below by $c\min(s,2-s)$ with a common $c>0$ along sufficiently fine approximations. Boundedness of entropy and an integrable logarithmic envelope give
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:polygon-limit}
 \Phi(z)\longrightarrow\Phi(y_*).
\end{equation}

Fix such a polygon $z$. Refine its interior linear pieces into blocks of maximum width $\delta$, leaving $z$ and $S(z)$ unchanged. Round block times to lattice times and choose endpoint heights within $O(1)$ of $nz(s_i)$ with the required parity. These choices are feasible for every sufficiently large $n$, since the fixed interior slopes are separated from $\pm1$. On the initial and final pieces choose the deterministic all-up and all-down paths, taking rounded integer lengths and their exact joining heights. Interior endpoint rounding stays $O(1)$.

In each interior block take all bridges with the chosen endpoints. Each lies within $2\delta+O(1/n)$ of $z$, by the unit Lipschitz bound. Choose $\delta$ small compared with the positive minimum of $z$ on $[\varepsilon,2-\varepsilon]$; then all these bridges stay positive, and their concatenations are valid Dyck paths. Stirling's formula gives logarithm of their number
$nS(z)+o(n)$, because there are a fixed finite number of blocks and the endpoint pieces have zero entropy. On the interior, normalized log weights differ from the integral contribution to $nP(z)$ by $o_\delta(n)+o(n)$, uniformly over all bridges.

The deterministic endpoint contributions in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:edge-weight} are exactly
\[
 4\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor}\log(j/n)+O(\log n).
\]
Dividing by $n$ gives $4\int_0^\varepsilon\log s\dd s$ in the limit, precisely the missing endpoint potential. Consequently
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log(n^{-4n}m_n)
 \ge\Phi(z)-o_\delta(1).
\]
First refine $\delta\downarrow0$, then let the polygon approach $y_*$ as in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:polygon-limit}. We obtain
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:lower}
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log(n^{-4n}m_n)\ge\Phi(y_*).
\end{equation}
All limits in the lattice construction were taken with the polygon and finite partition fixed before refinement. No favorable subsequence of $n$ is required.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-neighborhood concentration]\label{pwd:qt:thm:shape}
For every fixed $\eta>0$ there is $c_\eta>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:concentration}
 \Prob_{n,1}(\|y_n-y_*\|_\infty\ge\eta)\le\ee^{-c_\eta n}.
\end{equation}
Convergence in probability to $y_*$ is uniform for the interpolation laws $\Prob_{n,t}$, $0\le t\le1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:upper} to the closed complement of the uniform $\eta$-ball, use the strict gap \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:gap}, and divide by the lower bound \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:lower}. The uniform change-of-measure estimate \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:RN} proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}
This theorem concerns each fixed neighborhood. It does not supply a rate for neighborhoods shrinking with $n$, a fluctuation law, or quantitative concentration constants.

\begin{writenote}[Part~I's counterpart, 7 October 2026]
This limit shape is the case $p=4$ of Part~I's arch concentration
(Section~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:shape}: closed-set upper bound, calibration and
endpoint-safe lower bound), written here for the interpolation of this Part.
It is kept because the correction of Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:correction}
cites it by its own labels.

\section{The subtracted occupation limit and first correction}\label{pwd:qt:sec:correction}
Fix $0<\varepsilon<H$ and put $L_n=\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor$. For every bounded height function $g$, equal upward and downward edge crossings give the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:occupation-functional}
 \frac1n\sum_hN_hg(h/n)=\frac1{2n}\sum_{j=1}^{2n}g(H_j/n).
\end{equation}
Take $g_\varepsilon(x)=x^{-2}\mathbf1_{\{x>\varepsilon\}}$. Uniform convergence to $y_*$ implies that the right side tends in probability to
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:Teps}
 T_\varepsilon=\frac12\int_0^2y_*(s)^{-2}
                   \mathbf1_{\{y_*(s)>\varepsilon\}}\dd s
 =\int_\varepsilon^H\frac{\dd y}{y^2\sqrt{1-(y/H)^4}}.
\end{equation}
The cutoff discontinuity is harmless: $y_* =\varepsilon$ at exactly two times, a set of measure zero, and edge heights differ from the interpolated path by at most $1/n$. Since $g_\varepsilon\le\varepsilon^{-2}$, the same limit holds in expectation. The convergence is uniform in $t$ by \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:RN} and Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:shape}. Replacing the cutoff by $L_n/n$ does not affect the limit, by the same bounded convergence argument.

Now $f_h=1/(4h^2)+O(h^{-4})$ and $\sum_hu_{n,t}(h)=n$, so for fixed $\varepsilon$, uniformly in $t$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:high-limits}
 n\sum_{h>L_n}f_hu_{n,t}(h)\longrightarrow\tfrac14T_\varepsilon,
 \qquad n\sum_{h>L_n}f_h\longrightarrow\frac1{4\varepsilon}.
\end{equation}
The first expansion error is $O_\varepsilon(n^{-2})$. The second limit follows from the integral test. Its sum is over \emph{all} heights $h>L_n$, not just those attainable by a path of semilength $n$. Omitting the deterministic tail above $n$ would alter the correction constant.

For $\varepsilon<1/64$, Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:occupation} gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:low-subtract}
 n\left|\sum_{h\le L_n}f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1)\right|
 \le Cn^{-3}\sum_{h\le L_n}h^2\le C'\varepsilon^3,
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$ and sufficiently large $n$. This is the endpoint estimate needed for the singular statistic: it controls occupation \emph{after subtracting one crossing of each low edge}.

Combining \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:logderivative}, \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:high-limits}, and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:low-subtract}, we first let $n\to\infty$ with $\varepsilon$ fixed and then let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. It follows uniformly in $t$ that
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:signedlimit}
 n\left(\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log Z_n(t)-\sum_{h\ge1}f_h\right)
 \longrightarrow C_*,\qquad
 C_*:=\frac14\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
               (T_\varepsilon-1/\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
More explicitly, the supremum over $t$ of the error has limsup at most $C'\varepsilon^3$ plus the error in the limiting regularized integral; this tends to zero. Thus no interchange of a singular cutoff limit with an uncontrolled expectation is involved.

To evaluate the integral, put $y=Hu$:
\[
 C_* =\frac1{4H}\lim_{a\downarrow0}
 \left(\int_a^1\frac{\dd u}{u^2\sqrt{1-u^4}}-\frac1a\right).
\]
The elementary identity
\[
 \frac{\dd}{\dd u}\frac{\sqrt{1-u^4}}u
 =-\frac1{u^2\sqrt{1-u^4}}-\frac{u^2}{\sqrt{1-u^4}}
\]
shows that the regularized bracket equals $-J$, where
\[
 J=\int_0^1\frac{u^2\dd u}{\sqrt{1-u^4}}
   =\frac{\Gamma(3/4)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(1/4)},\qquad
 I=\frac{\Gamma(1/4)\Gamma(1/2)}{4\Gamma(3/4)},\qquad IJ=\frac\pi4.
\]
Since $H=1/I$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:cstar}
 C_*=-\frac{IJ}4=-\frac\pi{16}.
\end{equation}
Integrating the uniform limit \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:signedlimit} over $0\le t\le1$ and using \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:euler} yields
\[
 \log(m_n/b_n)=\log(\pi/2)-\frac\pi{16n}+o(n^{-1}).
\]
Exponentiation proves \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:comparison-main}. Combining it with \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:stirling-b} proves the raw-moment formula in \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:main}, and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:cumulant-transfer} proves the formula for $a_n$. This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:main}.

\section{Parity}\label{pwd:qt:sec:parity}
\begin{theorem}\label{pwd:qt:thm:parity}
For $n>0$, $a_n$ is odd if and only if $n$ is a power of two. Also $a_0=1$ is odd.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Modulo $2$, $h^4$ equals $1$ for odd $h$ and $0$ for even $h$. Every Dyck path reaching height $2$ has zero weight, so the only surviving path is $(UD)^n$. Hence $M(z)=(1-z)^{-1}$ in $\mathbb F_2[[z]]$. Since $x=zM(z)^2$ and $A(x)=M(z)$, we obtain
\[
 A(x)^2+A(x)=x.
\]
The unique solution with constant term $1$ is
\[
 A(x)=1+\sum_{j\ge0}x^{2^j}.
\]
For example, uniqueness follows recursively from the coefficient equation: the coefficient at odd degree greater than one is zero, the coefficient at degree one is one, and an even-degree coefficient equals that at half its degree. This proves the assertion.
\end{proof}
This is the parity criterion labeled conjectural on the inspected OEIS entry. It is a side consequence of the exact transform, independent of the asymptotic proof.

\section{A second Lambert W center for threshold inversion}\label{pwd:qt:sec:inverse}
For real $Y>0$, let
\[
 N(Y)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge Y\}.
\]
Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:main} implies $a_{n+1}/a_n\sim r^4n^4$, so the sequence tends to infinity and is eventually strictly increasing. No monotonicity assertion about an unspecified finite initial segment is needed below.

For large $Y$ define
\begin{align}\label{pwd:qt:eq:inverse-defs}
 L&=\log Y,&
 t&=\frac{L}{4W_0(rL/(4\ee))},&D&=4\log(rt),\notag\\
 C&=\log(4\sqrt2\,\pi^{3/2}r^2),&
 B&=\tfrac32\log t+C,&d&=\frac{13-3\pi}{48},\\
 \delta_0&=-\frac BD,&
 \delta_1&=-\frac{2\delta_0^2+\tfrac32\delta_0+d}{tD},&
 \xi(Y)&=t+\delta_0+\delta_1.\notag
\end{align}
Here $W_0$ is the principal real Lambert function, satisfying $W_0(u)\ee^{W_0(u)}=u$ for $u>0$.

\begin{theorem}[Shrinking two-ceiling envelope]\label{pwd:qt:thm:inverse}
There is a positive function $\omega(Y)\to0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $Y$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:inverse}
 \left\lceil\xi(Y)-\frac{\omega(Y)}{t\log t}\right\rceil
 \le N(Y)\le
 \left\lceil\xi(Y)+\frac{\omega(Y)}{t\log t}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for every $\varepsilon>0$ the same bounds hold with $\varepsilon$ in place of $\omega(Y)$ once $Y$ is sufficiently large depending on $\varepsilon$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $c=(1+\pi)/16$. Stirling's formula and Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:main} give
\begin{align}\label{pwd:qt:eq:log-model}
 \log F(x)&=4x\log(rx/\ee)+\tfrac32\log x+C+\frac1{3x}+O(x^{-3}),\notag\\
 \log a_n&=g(n)+o(n^{-1}),\qquad
 g(x)=h(x)+\tfrac32\log x+C+d/x,\quad h(x)=4x\log(rx/\ee),
\end{align}
where $d=1/3-c$. By the definition of $t$, $h(t)=L$, and
\[
 h'(t)=D,\qquad h''(t)=4/t,\qquad h'''(t)=-4/t^2.
\]
Also $g'(x)=4\log(rx)+3/(2x)-d/x^2>0$ for large $x$.

We have $\delta_0\to-3/8$ and $\delta_1=O(1/(t\log t))$. For bounded $s$, Taylor expansion yields uniformly
\[
 g(t+s)=L+Ds+B+\frac{2s^2+\tfrac32s+d}{t}+O(t^{-2}).
\]
Take $s=\delta_0+\delta_1$. The terms $D\delta_0+B$ cancel. Replacing $s$ by $\delta_0$ in the quadratic bracket changes its contribution by $O(|\delta_1|/t)=O(t^{-2})$, and $D\delta_1$ cancels the bracket at $\delta_0$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:center-error}
 g(\xi(Y))=L+O(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
The large smooth root $x_Y$ of $g(x_Y)=L$ therefore satisfies
$x_Y-\xi(Y)=O(1/(t^2\log t))$.

To preserve integer boundary cases, write $r_n=\log a_n-g(n)$ and set
\[
 E_0(t)=\sup_{n\ge\lceil t/2\rceil}|nr_n|.
\]
This tends to zero, and $|r_n|\le2E_0(t)/t$ in that range. Choose fixed $K_0'>0$ with $|g(\xi)-L|\le K_0'/t^2$ for large $Y$, and set
\[
 \eta(t)=E_0(t)+(K_0'+1)/t,\qquad q(Y)=\frac{8\eta(t)}{tD}.
\]
Then $q=o(1/(t\log t))$. On $[\xi-q,\xi+q]$ we have $g'\ge D/2$ for large $Y$. Hence
\[
 g(\xi+q)-L\ge\frac{4\eta}{t}-\frac{K_0'}{t^2}>\frac{2E_0(t)}t,
 \qquad
 g(\xi-q)-L\le-\frac{4\eta}{t}+\frac{K_0'}{t^2}<-\frac{2E_0(t)}t.
\]
Monotonicity of $g$ shows that every integer $n\ge\xi+q$ has $a_n\ge Y$, while every $\lceil t/2\rceil\le n<\xi-q$ has $a_n<Y$. Smaller integers are also below $Y$ for large $Y$: eventual monotonicity deals with the growing interval below $\lceil t/2\rceil$, and the finitely many earlier values are eventually dominated by $Y$. It follows that
$\lceil\xi-q\rceil\le N(Y)\le\lceil\xi+q\rceil$.
Taking $\omega(Y)=q(Y)t\log t$ proves \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:inverse}.
\end{proof}
The uncertainty shrinks, but its onset and the function $\omega$ are not made effective. If both ceilings agree, their common value is the threshold; near an integer boundary they may differ by one. An unconditional formula $N(Y)=\lceil\xi(Y)\rceil$ is not a consequence of the theorem.

\begin{remark}[{[write]} The inverse and the transseries volume, 7~October 2026]\label{pwd:qt:rem:transseries}
Statement by statement, against the volume named in the Guide (its symbols
carry the subscript ``vol'').
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Instance.} For $Y$ beyond the early values, $N(Y)$ is the integer
staircase of \file{p0:def:three-inverses} with $A_n=a_n$ and $n_1$ the onset
of eventual increase, so \file{p0:thm:staircase}(1) applies.
\item \emph{Instance.} The centre $t$ of~\eqref{pwd:qt:eq:inverse-defs},
with $h(t)=4t\log(rt/\ee)=L$, is \file{p0:prop:factorial-core} with
$\kappa_\vol=4$, $d_\vol=4\log r-4$: there $X=L/(4v)$,
$v=W_0((L/4)\ee^{\log r-1})=W_0(rL/(4\ee))$.
\item \emph{Formal instance, first coefficient checked.} With $x=t(1+E)$,
$h(t(1+E))-h(t)=4t[\Lambda E+(1+E)\log(1+E)-E]$ with $\Lambda=\log(rt)=D/4$;
dividing the rest of $g$ by $4t$ gives an equation of the form of
\file{p0:thm:core-reversion} ($\Lambda_\vol=\log(rt)$,
$h_\vol(E)=(1+E)\log(1+E)-E$, coefficients in a ring containing $\log t$).
Its first coefficient $e_1=-B/(4\Lambda)=-B/D$ is the source's $\delta_0$;
the write did not compare $\delta_1$ with the volume's second coefficient.
\item \emph{Analogue.} Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:inverse} is, as stated and
