proved, an analogue of \file{p0:thm:staircase}(2): a two-ceiling bracket
proved directly at the integers from the smooth model $g$, not deduced from
an admissible interpolation.
\end{enumerate}
No novelty is claimed for any of these inversions.
\end{remark}

\section{Exact computations and reproducibility}\label{pwd:qt:sec:computation}
The companion archive contains this TeX source and PDF, exact integer data, portable Python code, a README, and file manifests. It contains no third-party paper PDFs. Exact checks use explicit exceptions rather than removable Python assertions; running under \code{python -O} does not suppress them.

The raw moments are computed by a height-state dynamic program. If $v_s(h)$ is the total weight of nonnegative prefixes of length $s$ ending at height $h$, start with $v_0(0)=1$ and apply
\[
 v_{s+1}(h+1)\mathrel{+}=v_s(h),\qquad
 v_{s+1}(h-1)\mathrel{+}=h^4v_s(h)\quad(h\ge1).
\]
Then $m_n=v_{2n}(0)$. The comparison weights are stored in the integer scaling $4\gamma_h=h^2(4h^2-1)$, so their moments are $4^nb_n$ and all stored values remain integers.

For exact powers $G(z)=M(z)^e$, $G(0)=1$, the identity $MG'=eM'G$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:qt:eq:power-code}
 g_k=\frac1k\sum_{j=1}^k\bigl((e+1)j-k\bigr)m_jg_{k-j}.
\end{equation}
Together with \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:lagrange}, this computes cumulants with exact integer divisibility checks. Separate first-block and finite continued-fraction computations check the defining transform on a finite range. In particular the archive records:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.6em]
 \item exact quartic raw moments through $n=500$, integer-scaled comparison moments through $500$, and cumulants through $160$;
 \item equality to the fourteen displayed OEIS coefficients, at indices $0$ through $13$, and the parity pattern through $160$;
 \item independent enumeration of common-opener matching quadruples and their least noncrossing closures through $n=3$;
 \item exact negative-excursion deletion identities for several rational weight laws, and finite occupation checks within the theorem's range;
 \item symbolic shifted-moment polynomials through degree $8$, cyclic-placement identities, and exact algebraic checks of the Stirling and inverse cancellations.
\end{itemize}
The OEIS b-file was not successfully retrieved in the source inspection. The claim is comparison to the fourteen displayed terms, not a comparison to an unexamined b-file.

Finite matching enumeration gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|rr}
 n&\text{all common-opener quadruples}&\text{one noncrossing-closure block}\\ \hline
 1&1&1\\2&17&15\\3&1585&1490
\end{array}
\]
The enumeration treats closure explicitly, rather than substituting ordinary graph connectivity. Exact finite checks are independent consistency tests; they do not prove an asymptotic remainder, establish its numerical onset, or replace any argument in the preceding sections.

A separate optional numerical program evaluates ratios, regularized integrals, and the inverse model at high precision. These outputs are labeled diagnostics. For example, the predicted limits are
\[
 n\left(\frac{m_n}{(\pi/2)b_n}-1\right)\longrightarrow-\frac\pi{16}
     =-0.196349540849\ldots,
\]
\[
 n\left(\frac{m_n}{F(n)}-1\right)\longrightarrow-\frac{1+\pi}{16}
     =-0.258849540849\ldots.
\]
For a few exact raw moments, the numerical values are:
\[
\begin{array}{r|rr}
 n&n\bigl(m_n/((\pi/2)b_n)-1\bigr)&n\bigl(m_n/F(n)-1\bigr)\\ \hline
20&-0.192995705&-0.256298484\\
80&-0.195499050&-0.258208800\\
160&-0.195923282&-0.258528922\\
500&-0.196212917&-0.258746889
\end{array}
\]
These rounded values are diagnostics, not certified asymptotic error bounds. A visually small finite residual would not justify replacing the little-$o$ term by $O(n^{-2})$.

The build procedure validates the manifest, replays the exact outputs, and rebuilds the PDF with a fixed date and deterministic metadata. The release archive is extracted and rebuilt in fresh directories in ordinary and optimized Python modes, comparing the public output members byte for byte. PDF reproducibility is scoped to the documented matching TeX toolchain; it is not a claim of identical bytes across arbitrary TeX installations. Input and manifest rejection tests document the malformed cases actually checked, rather than a general security guarantee. Full commands and file roles are in the archive README.

\section{Scope of the source and overlap review}\label{pwd:qt:sec:sources}
The source review underlying this article was bounded. OEIS A338634 and the sibling entries A158119 and A338635 were inspected on 4 October 2026. The latter concern different weight systems and are not additional asymptotic theorems of this article. The current A338634 display attributes its leading equivalent to Kot\v e\v sovec and labels its parity criterion conjectural. The absence of a proof link in that display does not establish absence of a published proof.

The full Berg--Valent text was available; the inspected portions include (2.31), (3.1), the definition of $K_0$, and Proposition~3.6.1 on pages 194--195. Those are the exact external theorem and normalization used in Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:classical}. Dai--Ismail--Wang \cite{dai} was inspected at its reproduced recurrence and weight, especially (1.5), (1.6), (2.15), and (2.21). The direct Chen--Ismail PDF request did not return a usable full text, so that article is cited as historical context corroborated by the later primary paper, not as a full-text audit here.

For cumulant prior, the Bo\.{z}ejko--Hasebe full text \cite{bozejko} was available, and its relevant recurrence, Remark~3.5, and final open problems were inspected. The arXiv text of Defant--Lee \cite{defantlee} was inspected at its introduction, moment--cumulant formulas, theorem scope, and distribution applications. The historical square-weight question in the former is not represented as still open in 2026. M\l otkowski's abstract and search-visible introduction \cite{mlotkowski} were inspected, but its complete theorem suite was not audited. These sources establish broader prior context, not an $h^4$ amplitude theorem used without proof.

Bender's and Bender--Richmond's abstracts \cite{bender,benderrichmond} and the relevant definitions, theorem scope, and Remark~52 of Borinsky \cite{borinsky} were inspected. The original Bender--Richmond full hypotheses were not independently checked; the explicit proof in Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:shifted} removes the need to invoke them. Borinsky identifies the faster regime $nf_{n-1}=o(f_n)$ with Bender--Richmond-type methods, and \eqref{pwd:qt:eq:ratios} puts these moments in that regime. The related Anti-SEQ discussion \cite{antiseq} was also inspected, including its distinction between set/sequence/cycle decompositions and implicit noncrossing settings.

For the limit-shape method, the primary publisher and Math-Net records for Mogul'skii \cite{mogulskii} were inspected. The complete specialized entropy proof is supplied here. An institutional abstract of Disanto--Munarini \cite{disanto} covers related local-height questions for weighted Dyck models with exponents in $\{0,1,2\}$; its full text was not audited, and that observation does not exclude earlier quartic work.

A bounded public-repository screen of \href{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt}{Vladimir Reshetnikov's ProveIt repository} checked the current root tree, relevant complete filename trees in Analysis, Combinatorics, Oeis, SetTheory, and the changed Algebra subtree. A saved 64-report README corpus and a broader local collection of 585 README files were searched for the three sequence identifiers. This was not a full-body search of every repository file. Filename or identifier absence is not a novelty test, and earlier cumulant and inversion frameworks remain relevant prior work.

\section{What the argument makes reusable}\label{pwd:qt:sec:utility}
The results have several uses beyond recognizing one integer sequence. First, the matching model turns an implicit divergent series into a nonnegative enumerative class. It makes the distinction between noncrossing closure and graph connectivity concrete and gives independently enumerable finite objects.

Second, the occupation theorem is uniform over all weights between $(3/4)h^4$ and $h^4$. It supplies an elementary boundary estimate before a limit shape is known. Its combination with a summable logarithmic weight perturbation explains why the leading amplitude is the Euler product $\pi/2$, while the first finite-size correction is governed by a regularized macroscopic occupation integral. The theorem and interpolation proof have precise stated ranges; they are not promoted here to a general perturbation theorem.

Third, the Bonferroni proof separates a cumulant transform from the asymptotic analysis of the raw moments. For a fixed precision goal it uses finitely many low moments and exact shifted high moments, with a proved remainder. This is useful even when the raw moments have no known inverse-power expansion. Finally, the threshold theorem translates a little-$o$ coefficient estimate into a rounding-safe statement, preserving the exceptional behavior near integers rather than hiding it in a single ceiling.

\section{Further questions}\label{pwd:qt:sec:questions}
Several natural questions remain genuinely outside the proved scope.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em]
 \item Does $m_n/F(n)$ have a complete inverse-power expansion? In particular, can the next coefficient be obtained with a proved $O(n^{-2})$ or sharper remainder? This would require more than the fixed-cutoff convergence used here.
 \item Can one prove quantitative concentration for shrinking neighborhoods of $y_*$, a fluctuation law, or a controlled correction to the macroscopic occupation measure? The present theorem gives exponential concentration only for each fixed neighborhood.
 \item How far can the elementary occupation argument and amplitude transfer be extended to other polynomial weights or more general summable perturbations? Any such extension needs its own endpoint estimates and uniform change-of-measure argument; no general parameter family is asserted here.
 \item Can the asymptotic constants and threshold onset be made numerically effective? The current two-ceiling envelope has an existential little-$o$ radius, not a certified numerical error bound for a given $Y$.
 \item Can alternative combinatorial descriptions make the coefficients or congruences more transparent, beyond the parity result and common-opener interpretation?
\end{enumerate}
\begin{writenote}[7 October 2026]
Under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026, the write found no claim of
this Part that is unproved as stated and none that is wrong. One recorded
conjecture is settled: A338634's parity criterion, proved by
Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:parity} (Remark~\ref{pwd:qt:rem:oeis}). On Question~1,
Part~III's relative theorem gives a second route to the first correction
(Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:quartic-proof}), but no $O(n^{-2})$ remainder; the
question stands. The write recomputed the diagnostic table of
Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:computation} from its own exact $m_n$ and $b_n$: all
printed entries agree.
\end{writenote}
The package's exact and numerical checks provide test cases for these questions, while the proved statements remain those explicitly formulated above.


\part{First relative corrections for power weighted Dyck moments: three regimes and cumulative perturbations}\label{pwd:rc:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~202, archive \file{Report202.zip}, delivered as \file{Report202.tex} (27 pages). Delivered title block ``First relative corrections for power weighted Dyck moments / Three regimes and cumulative perturbations / Report 202 / 4 October 2026''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} remarks and notes; Report~202's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+33$ here, with its statements and equations, and its appendices keep the letters A and~B. Its labels carry \file{pwd:rc:}. It cites Reports~201 and~199, which are Parts~I and~II here; it is self-contained through its appendices.}
\begin{partabstract}
For positive descent weights asymptotic to $\kappa h^p$, we prove first relative corrections under summable logarithmic changes. A tail $b h^{-q}$ has three regimes: a regularized occupation integral when $1<q<p+1$, a logarithmically enhanced endpoint term when $q=p+1$, and an absolutely summable edge correction when $q>p+1$. We derive uniform one-step ratios from free energy and convexity, sharpen the endpoint occupation estimate, and prove local occupation convergence on compact height intervals strictly below the limiting arch top. This local result yields a cumulative-tail transfer theorem which covers genuinely oscillatory perturbations without a pointwise asymptotic coefficient. The consequences include exact-base relative inverse displacements with safeguarded integer ceilings and unconditional first corrections for all positive zero-harmonic quartic and quadratic polynomial weights. Complete endpoint and concentration foundations are included. Classical comparison models and prior general-power formulas are credited separately from the arguments developed here. A deterministic TeX, PDF, and code package separates exact finite checks from numerical diagnostics. We make no first-proof claim, effective onset claim, all-orders claim, or new unconditional general-power absolute coefficient claim.
\end{partabstract}

\section{Results and notation}\label{pwd:rc:sec:results}
For a Dyck path $D$ of semilength $n$, let $N_h(D)$ count descents from $h$ to $h-1$. The path has $2n$ nearest-neighbor steps, stays nonnegative, and begins and ends at zero. For positive weights $\lambda_h$ define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:definition}
 W_\lambda(D)=\prod_{h\ge1}\lambda_h^{N_h(D)},\qquad
 Z_n(\lambda)=\sum_{D\in\Dyck_n}W_\lambda(D),\qquad Z_0=1.
\end{equation}
Thus $\sum_hN_h=n$ and $Z_n(\kappa\lambda)=\kappa^nZ_n(\lambda)$. First-return decomposition gives the classical formal Stieltjes fraction \cite{flajolet}
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}Z_n(\lambda)z^n
 =\cfrac1{1-\cfrac{\lambda_1z}{1-\cfrac{\lambda_2z}{1-\cfrac{\lambda_3z}{\ddots}}}}.
\]
No analytic convergence of this ordinary generating series is assumed.

Fix $p,\kappa>0$. Throughout the main comparison results,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:asymptotic}
 \lambda_h/(\kappa h^p)\longrightarrow1,\qquad
 \widetilde\lambda_h=\lambda_h\ee^{f_h}.
\end{equation}
Write
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:constants}
 I_p=\int_0^1\frac{\dd u}{\sqrt{1-u^p}}
 =\frac1p B(1/p,1/2),\qquad H_p=I_p^{-1},\qquad d_p=4H_p^p.
\end{equation}
All limits fix $p$; no uniformity as $p$ approaches zero or varies is asserted. If $\sum_h|f_h|<\infty$, then
\[
 P=\exp\Bigl(\sum_{h\ge1}f_h\Bigr)
  =\prod_{h\ge1}\frac{\widetilde\lambda_h}{\lambda_h}>0.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Three relative correction regimes]\label{pwd:rc:thm:three}
Under \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:asymptotic} the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item If $1<q<p+1$ and $f_h=b h^{-q}+o(h^{-q})$, then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:bulk}
 \frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =P\left[1+bJ_{p,q}n^{1-q}+o(n^{1-q})\right],
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 J_{p,q}&=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 \left[\int_\varepsilon^{H_p}
 \frac{y^{-q}\dd y}{\sqrt{1-(y/H_p)^p}}
 -\frac{\varepsilon^{1-q}}{q-1}\right],\label{pwd:rc:eq:Jreg}\\
 &=H_p^{1-q}\frac{q-1-p/2}{p(q-1)}
 B\left(1-\frac{q-1}{p},\frac12\right).\label{pwd:rc:eq:Jpositive}
\end{align}
Both arguments of the beta function in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Jpositive} are positive.
\item If $f_h=b h^{-p-1}+o(h^{-p-1})$, then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:critical}
 \frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =P\left[1+\frac{2b}{d_p}n^{-p}\log n+o(n^{-p}\log n)\right].
\end{equation}
\item If $|f_h|\le C_f h^{-q}$ for some $q>p+1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:endpoint-regime}
 \frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =P\left[1+D n^{-p}+o(n^{-p})\right],\qquad
 D=\frac2{\kappa d_p}\sum_{h\ge1}(\widetilde\lambda_h-\lambda_h).
\end{equation}
The series defining $D$ is absolutely convergent.
\end{enumerate}
A zero displayed coefficient yields the corresponding little-$o$ conclusion only; it does not identify a next nonzero term.
\end{theorem}
For $p>1$ put $J_p=J_{p,2}$. In particular
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:Jp}
 J_p=\frac{I_p(1-p/2)}p B(1-1/p,1/2),\qquad
 J_2=0,\qquad J_4=-\frac\pi4.
\end{equation}
The zero occurs more generally at $q=1+p/2$ within the first regime. The sign of $J_{p,q}$ changes there. This cancellation makes the theorem useful even when a naive tail estimate would suggest a nonzero correction.

\begin{theorem}[Cumulative rather than pointwise transfer]\label{pwd:rc:thm:cumulative}
Let $p>1$. Suppose \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:asymptotic} holds and
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cumulative-hyp}
 |f_h|\le C_fh^{-2},\qquad
 S_N:=\sum_{h=1}^Nf_h=S+\frac A N+o(N^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cumulative}
 \frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =\ee^S\left[1-\frac{A J_p}{n}+o(n^{-1})\right].
\end{equation}
It is unnecessary that $h^2f_h$ converge. The envelope in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cumulative-hyp}, however, is an essential stated hypothesis of this theorem.
\end{theorem}
Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:oscillatory} provides an explicit oscillatory example. Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:inverse} gives relative inverse displacements for these laws. Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:polynomials} obtains absolute first corrections for quadratic and quartic weights with vanishing coefficient of the next-to-leading power.

\subsection{Relation to the companion reports and prior formulas}
Report 201 treats harmonic amplitude changes and the general positive-power leading equivalent. The endpoint deletion proof and uniform arch foundations from that companion are reproduced, with the two-sequence uniformity made explicit, in Appendices~\ref{pwd:rc:app:endpoint}--\ref{pwd:rc:app:shape}. This makes the present proofs independent of access to unpublished working notes. Report 199 established the pure-quartic correction. Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:quartic-proof} recovers it directly from the present relative theorem and the correctly normalized Berg--Valent comparison \cite{bergvalent}.

The general leading formula
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:Fp}
 Z_n(h^p)\sim F_p(n),\qquad
 F_p(n)=\sqrt{\frac{p d_p}{2\pi}}\,d_p^n(n!)^p n^{-1/2}
\end{equation}
was posted by V. Kot\v e\v sovec in A216966 on 24 September 2020 \cite{oeiscubic}. Report 201 derives it using the precise coefficient estimate restated by Claeys, Krasovsky, and Minakov \cite[Eq.~(2.3)]{ckm}, attributed there to Kriecherbauer and McLaughlin \cite{km}. The original KM theorem text was not directly inspected. Formula~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Fp} is context, not an input to our relative theorems: their weaker free energy is proved in Appendix~\ref{pwd:rc:app:shape}. No general absolute first correction is inferred by differentiating a norm remainder.

Avdoshkin and Dymarsky already derived the formal power-weight entropy saddle and arch equation \cite[Appendix C]{avdoshkin}. Their height convention $h\approx2nf(t)$ translates to ours by $y(s)=2f(s/2)$. The appendices here supply the endpoint occupation control and uniform concentration needed for the correction theorems; the arch geometry itself is not claimed as a new discovery.

\section{Uniform prerequisites and one step ratios}\label{pwd:rc:sec:prerequisites}
Interpolate geometrically,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:interpolation}
 \lambda_h(t)=\lambda_h\ee^{t f_h},\qquad 0\le t\le1.
\end{equation}
Whenever $f_h\to0$, the family belongs to one fixed two-sided power class
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:powerclass}
 c h^p\le\lambda_h(t)\le C h^p\quad(h\ge1,\ 0\le t\le1),
\end{equation}
and $\lambda_h(t)/(\kappa h^p)\to1$ uniformly in $t$. Define
\[
 u_{n,t}(h)=\E_{n,t}N_h,\qquad
 v_{n,t}(h)=\Prob_{n,t}(\max D\ge h),
\]
and extend $u_{n,t}(h)=0$ for $h>n$.

The two prerequisites needed below are stated here for reference; their complete proofs are in the appendices.
\begin{lemma}[Endpoint and arch foundations]\label{pwd:rc:lem:foundations}
For a whole fixed class \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:powerclass}, there exist $C_1,\rho,n_0>0$, depending only on $p,c,C$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:endpoint-bound}
 \E_{n,t}|N_h-1|\le C_1(h/n)^p\quad(n\ge n_0,\ 1\le h\le\rho n).
\end{equation}
For any family satisfying \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:powerclass} and uniform asymptotic equivalence to $\kappa h^p$, its linearly interpolated path $y_n(s)$, with time and height divided by $n$, concentrates uniformly exponentially in every fixed uniform neighborhood of a unique arch $y_*$ on $[0,2]$. The arch is symmetric about $s=1$, reaches $H_p$, and on its increasing half satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:arch-ode}
 y_*'(s)=\sqrt{1-(y_*(s)/H_p)^p}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:free-energy-main}
 \log Z_n(\lambda(t))
 =pn\log n+n\{\log(\kappa d_p)-p\}+o(n)
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$.
\end{lemma}
This statement applies without monotonicity of the weights or continuity of the family parameter. For the interpolation the differentiability used later follows simply because each partition function is a finite positive sum.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform one step ratios]\label{pwd:rc:lem:ratios}
Under the family hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:foundations},
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:ratio}
 R_n(t):=\frac{Z_{n-1}(\lambda(t))}{Z_n(\lambda(t))}
 =\frac{1+o(1)}{\kappa d_p n^p}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $L_n(t)=\log Z_n(\lambda(t))$. Finite Jacobi spectral positivity, proved in Appendix~\ref{pwd:rc:app:endpoint}, implies discrete convexity of $L_n$. For fixed $0<\varepsilon<1$ and $r=\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor$,
\[
 \frac{L_n-L_{n-r}}r\le L_n-L_{n-1}
 \le\frac{L_{n+r}-L_n}r.
\]
Insert \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:free-energy-main}. Subtract $p\log n+\log(\kappa d_p)$. The two limiting errors are, respectively,
\[
 p\left[-\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\log(1-\varepsilon)-1\right],\qquad
 p\left[\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\log(1+\varepsilon)-1\right].
\]
Both tend to zero as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. At each fixed $\varepsilon$, the $o(n)/r$ errors tend to zero uniformly in $t$, and rounding $r$ is harmless. Exponentiating proves \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ratio}. Convexity, rather than differentiation of an unknown asymptotic error, is what allows the one-step conclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Strengthened endpoint cone]\label{pwd:rc:prop:cone}
After decreasing the fixed $\rho$ if necessary,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cone-second}
 u_{n,t}(h)-1=2\lambda_h(t)R_n(t)+O((h/n)^{2p})
 \quad(1\le h\le\rho n)
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$ and sufficiently large $n$. In particular, for every fixed $h$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:fixed-height}
 n^p(u_{n,t}(h)-1)\longrightarrow
 \frac{2\lambda_h(t)}{\kappa d_p}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $e_{h,k}(t)$ be the weight sum of primitive negative excursions of length $2k$ at height $h$, staying nonnegative. Exact deletion gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:deletion-main}
 u_{n,t}(h)-v_{n,t}(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-h}e_{h,k}(t)
 \frac{Z_{n-k}(\lambda(t))}{Z_n(\lambda(t))}
 [u_{n-k,t}(h)+u_{n-k,t}(h+1)].
\end{equation}
The bijection and the uniform bound
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:geometric-k}
 e_{h,k}(t)Z_{n-k}/Z_n\le a^k,\qquad a=A_0(h/n)^p
\end{equation}
are proved in Appendix~\ref{pwd:rc:app:endpoint}. Choose $\rho$ so small that $a\le1/4$ and for $k\le n/2$ both child heights lie in the rough bounded-occupation cone of that appendix. Then their occupations are bounded by a fixed $B_0$.

The $k=1$ weight is exactly $e_{h,1}=\lambda_h(t)$. The endpoint bound at size $n-1$ gives
\[
 u_{n-1,t}(h)+u_{n-1,t}(h+1)=2+O((h/n)^p),
\]
using $h+1\le2h$. Since $R_n\lambda_h(t)=O((h/n)^p)$ by \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ratio}, replacing this bracket by $2$ costs $O((h/n)^{2p})$.

For $2\le k\le n/2$ use $B_0$, and for larger $k$ use the trivial bound $n$. The remaining sum is at most
\[
 2B_0\sum_{k\ge2}a^k+2n\sum_{k>n/2}a^k=O(a^2).
\]
Indeed $n a^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}=O(a^2)$ uniformly for $a\le1/4$ and $n\ge2$. The mountain/counting bound in the appendix gives $1-v_{n,t}(h)\le a^n\le a^2$. This proves \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cone-second}; multiply by $n^p$ at fixed $h$ and apply \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ratio} for \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:fixed-height}.
\end{proof}

\section{The bulk correction and its regularization}\label{pwd:rc:sec:bulk}
Along \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:interpolation}, finite differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:derivative}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log Z_n(\lambda(t))=\sum_{h=1}^nf_hu_{n,t}(h).
\end{equation}
Subtracting $\log P=\sum_{h\ge1}f_h$, and extending $u$ by zero, centers the occupation at one descent per edge. To prove Theorem~\ref{pwd:rc:thm:three}(i), it suffices to show
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:bulk-target}
 n^{q-1}\sum_{h\ge1}f_h[u_{n,t}(h)-1]\longrightarrow bJ_{p,q}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$.

Choose $0<\varepsilon<\min(\rho,H_p)$ and $L=\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor$. Since $|f_h|\le C_fh^{-q}$ for all $h$, the endpoint bound implies
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:bulk-low}
 n^{q-1}\left|\sum_{h\le L}f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1)\right|
 \le C n^{q-1-p}\sum_{h\le L}h^{p-q}
 \le C'\varepsilon^{p-q+1}.
\end{equation}
The exponent is positive precisely in the stated regime $q<p+1$.

Write $f_h=bh^{-q}+r_h$ and $\omega(L)=\sup_{h>L}h^q|r_h|\to0$. Because $\sum_hu_{n,t}(h)=n$,
\begin{align*}
 n^{q-1}\sum_{h>L}|r_h|u_{n,t}(h)&\le\omega(L)\frac{n^q}{(L+1)^q}\longrightarrow0,\\
 n^{q-1}\sum_{h>L}|r_h|&\le C_q\omega(L)n^{q-1}L^{1-q}\longrightarrow0
\end{align*}
for each fixed $\varepsilon$, uniformly in $t$. No regularity of the remainder sequence is used.

For every bounded height test $g$, up/down edge balance gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:occupation-identity}
 \frac1n\sum_hN_hg(h/n)=\frac1{2n}\sum_{j=1}^{2n}g(H_j/n),
\end{equation}
where $H_j$ is the upper endpoint of step $j$. Apply this with $g(y)=y^{-q}\mathbf1_{y>L/n}$. Uniform arch concentration yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cutoff-limit}
 n^{q-1}\sum_{h>L}h^{-q}u_{n,t}(h)
 \longrightarrow\frac12\int_0^2 y_*(s)^{-q}\mathbf1_{y_*(s)>\varepsilon}\dd s
 =\int_\varepsilon^{H_p}\frac{y^{-q}\dd y}{\sqrt{1-(y/H_p)^p}}.
\end{equation}
Here is the cutoff justification. The test is bounded for fixed $\varepsilon$; its discontinuity has two time preimages on the arch, a null set. For any uniformly convergent deterministic sequence of paths, the edge height and interpolated path differ by at most $1/n$, so dominated convergence gives the displayed limit. Compactness makes this convergence uniform in a sufficiently small fixed neighborhood of $y_*$. Uniform concentration and boundedness then give uniform convergence of expectations. The same argument accommodates $L/n\to\varepsilon$.

The integral test also gives
\[
 n^{q-1}\sum_{h>L}h^{-q}\longrightarrow\frac{\varepsilon^{1-q}}{q-1}.
\]
Thus the limiting error in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:bulk-target} is bounded by \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:bulk-low} plus the error in replacing the regularized integral at $\varepsilon$ by its limit.

To evaluate that limit using ordinary convergent integrals, set $a=\varepsilon/H_p$ and $G(u)=u^{1-q}\sqrt{1-u^p}$. Direct differentiation gives
\[
 G'(u)=\frac{(1-q)u^{-q}+(q-1-p/2)u^{p-q}}{\sqrt{1-u^p}}.
\]
Since $G(1)=0$, integration from $a$ to $1$ gives
\[
 \int_a^1\frac{u^{-q}\dd u}{\sqrt{1-u^p}}
 =\frac{G(a)}{q-1}+\frac{q-1-p/2}{q-1}
 \int_a^1\frac{u^{p-q}\dd u}{\sqrt{1-u^p}}.
\]
Now $G(a)-a^{1-q}=O(a^{p-q+1})\to0$, and the remaining integral equals $p^{-1}B(1-(q-1)/p,1/2)$ in the limit. This proves \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Jreg}--\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Jpositive} and \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:bulk-target}.

Integrating \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:derivative} in $t$ therefore gives
\[
 \log\frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =\log P+bJ_{p,q}n^{1-q}+o(n^{1-q}).
\]
Since $q>1$, the square of the vanishing correction is smaller order, so exponentiation proves \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:bulk}. A beta function with first argument $(1-q)/p$ could alternatively describe this coefficient by analytic continuation; it would not be a convergent improper beta integral. Formula~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Jpositive} avoids that ambiguity.

For $p=4,q=2$, ordinary positive-argument beta integrals give
\[
 I_4=\frac{\Gamma(1/4)\sqrt\pi}{4\Gamma(3/4)},\qquad
 \int_0^1\frac{u^2\dd u}{\sqrt{1-u^4}}
 =\frac{\Gamma(3/4)\sqrt\pi}{\Gamma(1/4)}.
\]
Their product is $\pi/4$, proving $J_4=-\pi/4$. At $p=2$ the factor $1-p/2$ in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Jp} vanishes directly.

\section{Critical and endpoint dominated corrections}\label{pwd:rc:sec:phases}
We now prove the remaining two regimes. The common centered derivative is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:centered}
 T_n(t)=\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log Z_n(\lambda(t))-\log P
       =\sum_{h\ge1}f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1).
\end{equation}
Take $L=\lfloor\rho n\rfloor$ for the strengthened cone.

\subsection{The critical power}
Suppose $f_h=b h^{-p-1}+o(h^{-p-1})$. Then
\[
 \sum_{h>L}|f_h|u_{n,t}(h)+\sum_{h>L}|f_h|=O(n^{-p})
\]
uniformly in $t$, using $\sum_hu_{n,t}(h)=n$. By \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cone-second},
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{h\le L}f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1)
 &=2R_n(t)\sum_{h\le L}f_h\lambda_h(t)
   +O\left(n^{-2p}\sum_{h\le L}h^{2p}|f_h|\right)\\
 &=2R_n(t)\sum_{h\le L}f_h\lambda_h(t)+O(n^{-p}).
\end{align*}
For every fixed $p>0$, $\sum_{h\le L}h^{p-1}=O(n^p)$, which justifies the last error. The hypotheses imply $h f_h\lambda_h(t)\to\kappa b$ uniformly in $t$. A split at fixed large $M$, followed by the harmonic sum estimate, gives
\[
 \sum_{h\le L}f_h\lambda_h(t)=\kappa b\log n+o(\log n)
\]
uniformly in $t$, including $b=0$. Consequently \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ratio} yields
\[
 T_n(t)=\frac{2b}{d_p}n^{-p}\log n+o(n^{-p}\log n)
\]
uniformly. Integrate and exponentiate to obtain \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:critical}.

\subsection{Summable edge differences}
Suppose $|f_h|\le C_fh^{-q}$ for $q>p+1$. The high-height terms in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:centered} are now $O(n^{1-q})=o(n^{-p})$. For $h\le L$, the endpoint bound gives
\[
 n^p|f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1)|\le C|f_h|h^p,
 \qquad\sum_{h\ge1}|f_h|h^p<\infty.
\]
Combine this summable majorant with \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:fixed-height}. First truncate to $h\le M$, where convergence is uniform in $t$, and then let $M\to\infty$. It follows that
\[
 n^pT_n(t)\longrightarrow\frac2{\kappa d_p}
 \sum_{h\ge1}f_h\lambda_h(t)
\]
uniformly in $t$. The series on the right converges absolutely and uniformly because $\lambda_h(t)\le Ch^p$. Hence termwise integration is justified, and
\[
 \int_0^1 f_h\lambda_h\ee^{tf_h}\dd t
 =\widetilde\lambda_h-\lambda_h,
\]
with the same identity at $f_h=0$. Also
$|\widetilde\lambda_h-\lambda_h|\le C h^p|f_h|=O(h^{p-q})$,
so its series converges absolutely. Integration and exponentiation prove \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:endpoint-regime}.

For example, changing only $\lambda_1$ gives
\[
 \frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =\frac{\widetilde\lambda_1}{\lambda_1}
 \left[1+\frac{2(\widetilde\lambda_1-\lambda_1)}{\kappa d_p}n^{-p}
 +o(n^{-p})\right].
\]
The factor two is fixed by the two vertex-arrival types in the deletion identity; it is not obtained from a fitted numerical coefficient.

\section{Local occupation below the arch top}\label{pwd:rc:sec:local}
Weak occupation convergence sufficed for the pointwise-tail theorem. The cumulative theorem requires more: a uniform local limit for single-height expected occupation.

\begin{theorem}[Local occupation density]\label{pwd:rc:thm:local}
Let a family of positive weights satisfy \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:powerclass} and $\lambda_h(t)/(\kappa h^p)\to1$ uniformly in $t$. For every compact $K\subset(0,H_p)$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:local}
 \sup_{\substack{t\in[0,1]\\h/n\in K}}
 \left|u_{n,t}(h)-U_p(h/n)\right|\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad U_p(x)=\frac1{\sqrt{1-(x/H_p)^p}}.
\end{equation}
There is no claim at the arch top, no rate claim, and no monotonicity assumption on the weights.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Long negative excursions}
Fix $b<H_p$ and $\delta>0$. We show that a primitive negative excursion from $h\le bn$ of semilength $k>\delta n$ forces a fixed-neighborhood concentration failure. Choose $\zeta>0$ so that $b+\zeta<H_p$ and
\[
 \frac\zeta{v_0}<\delta,\qquad
 v_0=\sqrt{1-((b+\zeta)/H_p)^p}>0.
\]
If a rescaled path is within $\zeta$ of $y_*$, its excursion interval $[s_1,s_2]$ lies in the sublevel set $y_*\le h/n+\zeta$. The interval is connected and therefore lies within one of the two disjoint components of that sublevel set. The arch is monotone there, with absolute slope at least $v_0$. At both endpoints its height differs from $h/n$ by at most $\zeta$. Thus $s_2-s_1\le2\zeta/v_0<2\delta$, contradicting the excursion length $2k/n>2\delta$. This argument also covers $h/n<\zeta$.

There are at most $n$ marked descents in a path. Uniform exponential concentration therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:long-excursions}
 \E_{n,t}\{\text{non-last descents at edge $h$ with excursion }k>\delta n\}
 \le n\ee^{-c_\delta n},\quad h\le bn,
\end{equation}
for large $n$, uniformly in $t$. By exact deletion, this is precisely the long-$k$ part of \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:deletion-main}. Bounding the event avoids separately overestimating the factors of a long excursion.

\subsection{Catalan contraction on a compact cone}
Fix $0<a\le b<H_p$ and choose $b_1$ strictly between $b$ and $H_p$. Put $M_h(t)=\max_{j\le h}\lambda_j(t)$. Uniform asymptotic equivalence and the fixed power class give
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:max-weight}
 M_h(t)/(\kappa h^p)\longrightarrow1
\end{equation}
uniformly: large $j$ obey the asymptotic bound and $j^p\le h^p$, the finitely many small $j$ contribute $o(h^p)$, and $j=h$ gives the lower bound.

Choose $\tau>0$ and $0<\delta<1/2$ sufficiently small that
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:r-local}
 r=\frac{1+\tau}{1-\tau}\frac{b_1^p}{d_p(1-\delta)^p}<\frac14.
\end{equation}
This is possible since $d_p=4H_p^p$. Let $C_j=(j+1)^{-1}\binom{2j}{j}$ be the Catalan numbers. There are $C_{k-1}$ unrestricted primitive negative excursions of semilength $k$; the floor at zero can only remove some. Equations~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ratio} and \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:max-weight} imply, for sufficiently large $h,N$, $h\le b_1N$ and $k\le\delta N$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:catalan-bound}
 e_{h,k}(t)\frac{Z_{N-k}}{Z_N}
 \le C_{k-1}M_h(t)^k
 [\kappa d_p(1-\tau)(N-k)^p]^{-k}
 \le C_{k-1}r^k=:c_k.
\end{equation}

Starting at $(n,h)$ with $an\le h\le bn$, recursively expand the inequality from \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:deletion-main} at nodes $(N,j)$ with $N\ge n/2$ and $j\le b_1N$. Retain only $k\le\delta N$ in the recursion, using \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:long-excursions} to bound the omitted part. Throughout the expanded domain, $j\ge h\ge an$ and $N\ge n/2$, so the large-height and large-size bounds are uniform. The forcing at each expanded node is at most $1+n\ee^{-cn/2}$. Each retained edge decreases $N$ by $k$ and either leaves $j$ fixed or increases it by one. Stop at the first exit and bound terminal occupations by $n$.

For a branch with total decrease $s$, height increases by at most $s$. An exit through $j>b_1N$ requires $bn+s>b_1(n-s)$ and hence $s>(b_1-b)n/(1+b_1)$. An exit through $N<n/2$ requires $s>n/2$. Thus every exit has $s\ge c_0n$ for some fixed $c_0>0$.

Let $C(z)=\sum_{k\ge1}c_kz^k=(1-\sqrt{1-4rz})/2$. The two child choices at every recursive step give a total branch coefficient bounded by
\[
 \sum_{\ell\ge0}[2C(1)]^\ell=\frac1{\sqrt{1-4r}}.
\]
Choose $\theta>0$ with $r\ee^\theta<1/4$. Branches whose total decrease is at least $c_0n$ have coefficient sum at most
\[
 \frac{\ee^{-\theta c_0n}}{1-2C(\ee^\theta)}.
\]
All coefficients are nonnegative; enlarging to all formal branches is legitimate. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:local-upper}
 u_{n,t}(h)\le\frac{1+o(1)}{\sqrt{1-4r}}+O(n\ee^{-\theta c_0n}),
 \qquad an\le h\le bn,
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$.

\subsection{Identification of the limit}
Fix $x\in(0,H_p)$ and any sequence $n\to\infty$, $h/n\to x$, with arbitrary $t\in[0,1]$. First fix $x<b<b_1<H_p$ and the small parameters above, take the upper limit in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:local-upper}, and then let $b,b_1\downarrow x$ and $\tau,\delta\downarrow0$. This yields
\[
 \limsup u_{n,t}(h)\le[1-4x^p/d_p]^{-1/2}=U_p(x).
\]
Let $U_-(x)$ be the liminf over all such sequences. It is finite and nonnegative by \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:local-upper}. Concentration gives $v_{n,t}(h)\to1$. For each fixed $k$, every primitive excursion is feasible for large $h$ and stays within $k$ levels of $h$. Uniform asymptotic equivalence gives
\[
 e_{h,k}(t)=(1+o(1))C_{k-1}(\kappa h^p)^k,\qquad
 Z_{n-k}/Z_n=(1+o(1))(\kappa d_p n^p)^{-k}.
\]
Keep only $1\le k\le K_0$ in the nonnegative deletion identity. Both child sequences still have scaled height tending to $x$, so taking the liminf gives
\[
 U_-(x)\ge1+2U_-(x)\sum_{k=1}^{K_0}C_{k-1}(x^p/d_p)^k.
\]
The coefficient on the right is less than one after multiplication by two. Rearrange, then let $K_0\to\infty$ to obtain $U_-(x)\ge U_p(x)$. This proves sequential convergence. If convergence in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:local} failed on a compact set, a failing sequence would have a subsequence with $h/n\to x\in K$, contradicting this result and continuity of $U_p$. The theorem follows.

\section{Cumulative tail transfer and an oscillatory example}\label{pwd:rc:sec:cumulative}
We prove Theorem~\ref{pwd:rc:thm:cumulative}. Along the interpolation, let $0<\varepsilon<\min(\rho,H_p)$ be fixed. The endpoint bound gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cum-low}
 n\left|\sum_{h\le\varepsilon n}f_h(u_{n,t}(h)-1)\right|
 \le Cn^{1-p}\sum_{h\le\varepsilon n}h^{p-2}
 \le C'\varepsilon^{p-1}.
\end{equation}
This tends to zero as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ because $p>1$.

For the interior heights introduce the signed discrete measures
\[
 \mu_n=n\sum_{h\ge1}f_h\delta_{h/n}.
\]
On every compact interval $[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)$ their total variations are uniformly bounded: $n\sum_{an\le h\le bn}|f_h|=O(1)$. For fixed $a<x<y<b$, the cumulative hypothesis gives
\[
 \mu_n((x,y])=n(S_{\lfloor ny\rfloor}-S_{\lfloor nx\rfloor})
 \longrightarrow A(1/y-1/x)=-A\int_x^y z^{-2}\dd z.
\]
Endpoint atoms are $O(n^{-1})$. Step-function approximation and the common variation bound therefore imply convergence against every continuous test on compact intervals away from zero:
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:signed-limit}
 \mu_n\Longrightarrow -A x^{-2}\dd x.
\end{equation}
For $\delta>0$ with $\varepsilon<H_p-\delta$, Theorem~\ref{pwd:rc:thm:local} and \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:signed-limit} yield
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cum-interior}
 n\sum_{\varepsilon n<h<(H_p-\delta)n}f_hu_{n,t}(h)
 \longrightarrow-A\int_\varepsilon^{H_p-\delta}x^{-2}U_p(x)\dd x
\end{equation}
uniformly in $t$. Indeed the integral of the fixed continuous function $U_p$ converges by \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:signed-limit}, and the varying-test error $u_{n,t}-U_p$ is bounded by its uniform supremum times the common total variation.

The upper region is controlled without a local theorem at the top. Above $H_p+\delta$,
\[
 n\sum_{h>(H_p+\delta)n}|f_h|u_{n,t}(h)
 \le\frac C{(H_p+\delta)^2}\frac1n
 \sum_{h>(H_p+\delta)n}u_{n,t}(h)\longrightarrow0.
\]
This follows from weak occupation convergence \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:occupation-identity} and uniform concentration. In the band $|h/n-H_p|\le\delta$, the same argument bounds the absolute contribution by $C/(H_p-\delta)^2$ times its normalized occupation mass. The limiting mass is
\[
 \frac12\operatorname{meas}\{s\in[0,2]:|y_*(s)-H_p|\le\delta\},
\]
which tends to zero as $\delta\downarrow0$. The boundary levels have null time preimages, so the indicator convergence is justified as in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cutoff-limit}. The density $U_p$ has an integrable square-root singularity at its top. First take $n\to\infty$ and then $\delta\downarrow0$ in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cum-interior} to obtain
\[
 n\sum_{h>\varepsilon n}f_hu_{n,t}(h)
 \longrightarrow-A\int_\varepsilon^{H_p}x^{-2}U_p(x)\dd x
\]
uniformly in $t$. Separately,
\[
 n\sum_{h>\varepsilon n}f_h=n(S-S_{\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor})
 \longrightarrow-\frac A\varepsilon.
\]
Subtracting this baseline, using \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cum-low}, and then letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ gives
\[
 n\left[\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log Z_n(\lambda(t))-S\right]\longrightarrow-AJ_p
\]
uniformly. Integrating and exponentiating proves \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cumulative}. The top-band estimate, rather than a claim of local convergence at $H_p$, is the key remaining uniform-integrability step.

\subsection{A genuinely oscillatory telescoping perturbation}\label{pwd:rc:sec:oscillatory}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:oscillatory}
 a_0=1,\qquad a_h=1+\frac1{3h}+\frac{(-1)^h}{4h^2}\quad(h\ge1),\qquad
 f_h=\log\frac{a_h}{a_{h-1}}.
\end{equation}
Every $a_h$ is positive, since $a_h\ge1+1/(3h)-1/(4h^2)>0$. Telescoping gives $S_N=\log a_N=1/(3N)+O(N^{-2})$, so $S=0$ and $A=1/3$. For $h\ge2$,
\[
 \log a_h=\frac1{3h}+\frac{(-1)^h/4-1/18}{h^2}+O(h^{-3}).
\]
Subtracting the expression at $h-1$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:osc-limits}
 h^2f_h=-\frac13+\frac{(-1)^h}{2}+O(h^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Thus $h^2f_h$ tends to $1/6$ along even indices and $-5/6$ along odd indices. It has no single pointwise limiting coefficient, although $|f_h|\le Ch^{-2}$. For any $p>1$ and any base weights asymptotic to $\kappa h^p$,
\[
 \frac{Z_n(\lambda_h a_h/a_{h-1})}{Z_n(\lambda_h)}
 =1-\frac{J_p}{3n}+o(n^{-1}).
\]
At $p=4$ the correction is $\pi/(12n)$. At $p=2$ the conclusion is $1+o(n^{-1})$, with no next term claimed. This example is within the cumulative theorem and outside the pointwise $q=2$ hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{pwd:rc:thm:three}(i).

\section{Polynomial comparisons and absolute low degree corrections}\label{pwd:rc:sec:polynomials}
\subsection{Equal next to leading coefficients}
Let $Q,R$ be real polynomials of the same degree $d>1$, positive at every positive integer, with common leading coefficient $\kappa>0$. Suppose
\[
 \frac{Q(h)}{\kappa h^d}=1+\frac a h+\frac{c_Q}{h^2}+O(h^{-3}),\qquad
 \frac{R(h)}{\kappa h^d}=1+\frac a h+\frac{c_R}{h^2}+O(h^{-3}).
\]
Then $\log(Q(h)/R(h))=(c_Q-c_R)h^{-2}+O(h^{-3})$ and
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:poly-relative}
 \frac{Z_n(Q)}{Z_n(R)}=P_{Q,R}
 \left[1+\frac{(c_Q-c_R)J_d}{n}+o(n^{-1})\right].
\end{equation}
If
$Q(x)=\kappa\prod_{j=1}^d(x+\alpha_j)$ and
$R(x)=\kappa\prod_{j=1}^d(x+\beta_j)$, counted with multiplicity, then equality of the next-to-leading coefficients is $\sum_j\alpha_j=\sum_j\beta_j$, and
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:gamma-product}
 P_{Q,R}=\prod_{j=1}^d\frac{\Gamma(1+\beta_j)}{\Gamma(1+\alpha_j)}.
\end{equation}
Indeed the finite product through $N$ equals the displayed constant times
$\prod_j\Gamma(N+1+\alpha_j)/\Gamma(N+1+\beta_j)$; the latter tends to one by the fixed-shift gamma ratio and equal shift sums. Positivity excludes roots at positive integers and hence poles in the displayed gamma factors. Complex conjugate roots or individually negative real shifted factors cause no ambiguity: the full expression is the limit of positive real finite products.

For example, for $|a|<1$,
\[
 \frac{Z_n(h^p(1-a^2/h^2))}{Z_n(h^p)}
 =\frac{\sin(\pi a)}{\pi a}
 \left[1-\frac{a^2J_p}{n}+o(n^{-1})\right]\quad(p>1),
\]
with the prefactor interpreted continuously at $a=0$.

\subsection{The quartic baseline from Berg and Valent}\label{pwd:rc:sec:quartic-proof}
Let $m_n=Z_n(h^4)$ and $b_n=Z_n(h^2(h^2-1/4))$. The relative theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:quartic-comparison}
 \frac{b_n}{m_n}=\frac2\pi\left[1+\frac\pi{16n}+o(n^{-1})\right],
 \qquad
 \frac{m_n}{b_n}=\frac\pi2\left[1-\frac\pi{16n}+o(n^{-1})\right].
\end{equation}
Here $f_h=\log(1-1/(4h^2))=-1/(4h^2)+O(h^{-4})$, the product is $2/\pi$, and $J_4=-\pi/4$.

For the absolute normalization put
\[
 K_0=\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^2}{4\sqrt\pi}=\sqrt2 I_4,
 \qquad r=\frac2{K_0}=\frac{8\sqrt\pi}{\Gamma(1/4)^2}.
\]
Berg and Valent's equation (3.1) gives birth--death rates
\[
 \ell_j=(4j+1)(4j+2)^2(4j+3),\qquad
 m_j^{\rm BD}=(4j-1)(4j)^2(4j+1),\quad m_0^{\rm BD}=0.
\]
The alternating Stieltjes coefficients $\alpha_{2j+1}=\ell_j$, $\alpha_{2j}=m_j^{\rm BD}$ obey
$\alpha_h=4h^2(4h^2-1)=16h^2(h^2-1/4)$. S-to-J contraction gives diagonal $\alpha_{2j}+\alpha_{2j+1}$ and squared off-diagonal $\alpha_{2j+1}\alpha_{2j+2}$, matching their equation (2.31). Their moments are therefore $16^nb_n$. Proposition 3.6.1, pages 194--195, yields \cite{bergvalent}
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:BV}
 b_n=\frac{4\Gamma(4n+2)}{16^nK_0^{4n+2}}
 \left[1+O(25^{-n})\right].
\end{equation}
With
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:F4}
 F_4(n)=\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,r^{4n+2}(n!)^4n^{-1/2},
\end{equation}
ordinary shifted Stirling gives
\[
 \frac{\pi b_n}{2F_4(n)}=1-\frac1{16n}+O(n^{-2}).
\]
For explicit bookkeeping, the logarithmic $n^{-1}$ coefficient of $\Gamma(4n+2)$ is $13/48$, while that of $(n!)^4$ is $1/3=16/48$. Their difference is $-1/16$. Combining with \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:quartic-comparison} recovers the pure quartic result of Report 199,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:pure4}
 m_n=F_4(n)\left[1-\frac{1+\pi}{16n}+o(n^{-1})\right].
\end{equation}
The proof uses the classical comparison moment and a relative correction; it does not infer a recurrence correction from Freud norm errors.
\begin{writenote}[A second route, 7 October 2026]
Equation~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:pure4} is Part~II's Theorem~\ref{pwd:qt:thm:main}
for the moments, reached by a second route: Part~II integrates its own
subtracted occupation limit (Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:correction}), whereas
here the general relative theorem with $J_4=-\pi/4$ is applied. Both use the
same Berg--Valent normalization (Part~II's Section~\ref{pwd:qt:sec:classical},
Part~I's Section~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:quartic}).
\end{writenote}

\begin{corollary}[All positive zero harmonic quartics]\label{pwd:rc:cor:quartic}
Let $Q(h)=\kappa(h^4+b h^2+c h+d)$, with $\kappa>0$, be positive at all positive integers. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:quartic-poly}
 Z_n(Q)=\kappa^nP_QF_4(n)
 \left[1-\frac{(1+\pi)/16+\pi b/4}{n}+o(n^{-1})\right],
\end{equation}
where
\[
 P_Q=\prod_{h\ge1}\frac{Q(h)}{\kappa h^4}
 =\frac1{\prod_{j=1}^4\Gamma(1+\alpha_j)}>0,
 \qquad Q(x)/\kappa=\prod_{j=1}^4(x+\alpha_j).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The missing cubic coefficient means $\sum_j\alpha_j=0$ and
$\log(Q(h)/(\kappa h^4))=b/h^2+O(h^{-3})$.
Equations~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:poly-relative}, \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:Jp}, and \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:pure4} give \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:quartic-poly}. Formula~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:gamma-product} gives the product. The remainder is little-$o(n^{-1})$; no $O(n^{-2})$ remainder for general $Q$ follows.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The quadratic secant anchor}
For clarity the classical pure-quadratic identification is proved formally. On polynomials in $z$ set
\[
 L=(1+z^2)\frac{\dd}{\dd z}+z,
 \qquad Lz^h=h z^{h-1}+(h+1)z^{h+1}.
\]
The matrix in the monomial basis is symmetric with off-diagonal entry $h$ between levels $h-1$ and $h$. Therefore the constant coefficient of $L^{2n}1$ is $Z_n(h^2)$ by the finite weighted-walk identity. The formal function
\[
 \Psi(t,z)=\frac1{\cos t-z\sin t}
\]
satisfies $\partial_t\Psi=L\Psi$ and $\Psi(0,z)=1$. Formal uniqueness coefficient by coefficient in $t$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:secant}
 \sum_{n\ge0}\frac{Z_n(h^2)}{(2n)!}t^{2n}=\sec t.
\end{equation}
This is a classical continued-fraction correspondence, also given by Deb and Sokal \cite[Sec.~6.3, Eq.~(6.24a) at $\alpha=k=0$]{debsokal}, and by the earlier weighted-path theory \cite{flajolet}. No unbounded operator exponential is assumed.

Put $R=\pi/2$. After removing its singularities at $\pm R$,
\[
 \sec t-\frac1{R-t}-\frac1{R+t}
\]
is analytic in $|t|<3R$. Cauchy's estimate on $|t|=2R$ therefore gives
\[
 [t^{2n}]\sec t=2R^{-2n-1}\left[1+O(4^{-n})\right].
\]
Together with the central-binomial Stirling expansion,
\[
 \frac{(2n)!}{(n!)^2}=\frac{4^n}{\sqrt{\pi n}}
 \left[1-\frac1{8n}+O(n^{-2})\right],
\]
this yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:pure2}
 Z_n(h^2)=F_2(n)\left[1-\frac1{8n}+O(n^{-2})\right],\qquad
 F_2(n)=\frac4{\pi^{3/2}}\left(\frac{16}{\pi^2}\right)^n(n!)^2n^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[All positive zero harmonic quadratics]\label{pwd:rc:cor:quadratic}
Let $Q(h)=\kappa(h^2+b)$, where $\kappa>0$ and $b>-1$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:quadratic-poly}
 Z_n(Q)=\kappa^nP_bF_2(n)\left[1-\frac1{8n}+o(n^{-1})\right],
\end{equation}
where
\[
 P_b=\prod_{h\ge1}(1+b/h^2)=
 \begin{cases}
 \sinh(\pi\sqrt b)/(\pi\sqrt b),&b>0,\\
 1,&b=0,\\
 \sin(\pi\sqrt{-b})/(\pi\sqrt{-b}),&-1<b<0.
 \end{cases}
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The condition $b>-1$ is exactly positivity on positive integers. The logarithmic ratio is $b/h^2+O(h^{-4})$. Since $J_2=0$, the relative theorem gives $Z_n(Q)/(\kappa^nZ_n(h^2))=P_b[1+o(n^{-1})]$. Multiply by \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:pure2}. Euler's sine product, with its imaginary-argument version, evaluates $P_b$. The coefficient $-1/8$ is independent of $b$; the first nonzero $b$-dependent correction has not been established here.
\end{proof}

\subsection{OEIS normalization anchors}
A227887 is the raw pure-quartic moment sequence $Z_n(h^4)$, beginning
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 17,\ 1585,\ 485729,\ 372281761.
\]
A144853 has descent weights $h^2(4h^2-1)/3=(4/3)h^2(h^2-1/4)$, while A261000 has weights $h^2(4h^2-1)$ \cite{oeisquartic}. Their first terms are respectively
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 21,\ 2541,\ 1023561,\ 1036809081
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 1,\ 3,\ 189,\ 68607,\ 82908441.
\]
Thus A261000$(n)=3^n$A144853$(n)$ exactly, and
\begin{align*}
 \mathrm{A144853}(n)&=\frac2\pi\left(\frac43\right)^nF_4(n)
 \left[1-\frac1{16n}+o(n^{-1})\right],\\
 \mathrm{A261000}(n)&=\frac2\pi4^nF_4(n)
 \left[1-\frac1{16n}+o(n^{-1})\right].
\end{align*}
In these particular classical models \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:BV} even supplies an $O(n^{-2})$ remainder after the displayed first correction. These are normalization checks and consequences of classical elliptic formulas, not claims to new elliptic asymptotics.

A338634 is a distinct cumulant transform of the pure-quartic moments. Its prefix is
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 15,\ 1490,\ 472475,\ldots,
\]
which differs from the A227887 prefix. The theorems here concern the raw moments $Z_n$; no cumulant transfer for a general polynomial $Q$ is asserted. We assign no OEIS identifier to an unverified moment family.

\section{Relative inverse displacements and integer thresholds}\label{pwd:rc:sec:inverse}
Let $F,\widetilde F$ be the continuous piecewise-linear interpolations of $\log Z_n(\lambda)$ and $\log Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)$ on the nonnegative integers. By Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:ratios}, all one-step slopes are asymptotic to $p\log n$ and hence strictly positive on a tail. For sufficiently large $Y$, let $x,\widetilde x$ be the unique tail solutions
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:inverse-def}
 F(x)=\log(Y/P),\qquad\widetilde F(\widetilde x)=\log Y.
\end{equation}
The base inverse $x$ is exact for the base sequence's log-linear interpolation. It is not an explicit Lambert-$W$ approximation. The division by $P$ is part of the comparison and cannot be omitted.

\begin{theorem}[Exact base relative displacement]\label{pwd:rc:thm:inverse}
Suppose one of the proved logarithmic ratio laws has the form
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:ratio-scale}
 \log\frac{Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 =\log P+D s(n)+o(s(n)),
\end{equation}
where $s(n)$ is $n^{1-q}$, $n^{-p}\log n$, or $n^{-p}$ in its respective regime. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:inverse-shift}
 \widetilde x-x=-\frac{D s(x)}{p\log x}
 +o\left(\frac{s(x)}{\log x}\right).
\end{equation}
The cumulative theorem also has this conclusion with $s(x)=x^{-1}$, $P=\ee^S$, and $D=-AJ_p$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Each scale is positive on a tail, tends to zero, and obeys $s(n+\theta)/s(n)\to1$ uniformly for $0\le\theta\le1$. Linear interpolation of the endpoint values in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ratio-scale} therefore gives
\[
 \widetilde F(z)-F(z)-\log P=D s(z)+o(s(z))
\]
uniformly as real $z\to\infty$. The maximum of the two normalized endpoint errors tends to zero, and both endpoint scales are asymptotic to the scale within that interval.

At $z=x$ the residual is $\widetilde F(x)-\log Y=D s(x)+o(s(x))=o(1)$. Every slope on $[x-1,x+1]$ is $(p+o(1))\log x$, so first $\widetilde x\in(x-1,x+1)$, and then the lower slope bound gives $|\widetilde x-x|=O(s(x)/\log x)=o(1)$. If $D=0$, the same argument gives little-$o$ directly. A secant crossing one or more interpolation breakpoints is a convex combination of neighboring slopes. Hence
\[
 \widetilde F(\widetilde x)-\widetilde F(x)
 =(\widetilde x-x)(p+o(1))\log x
 =-D s(x)+o(s(x)),
\]
which proves \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:inverse-shift}. No derivative of the unknown remainder or differentiability at an integer breakpoint is assumed.
\end{proof}
The three explicit displacements are
\begin{align*}
 1<q<p+1:\quad
 \widetilde x-x&=-\frac{bJ_{p,q}}p\frac{x^{1-q}}{\log x}
 +o(x^{1-q}/\log x),\\
 q=p+1:\quad
 \widetilde x-x&=-\frac{2b}{p d_p}x^{-p}+o(x^{-p}),\\
 q>p+1:\quad
 \widetilde x-x&=-\frac2{p\kappa d_p}
 \left[\sum_{h\ge1}(\widetilde\lambda_h-\lambda_h)\right]
 \frac{x^{-p}}{\log x}+o(x^{-p}/\log x).
\end{align*}
In the critical case the logarithm in the correction cancels the logarithm in the inverse slope. In every case a zero coefficient gives only the stated smaller-order displacement.

Define the inclusive integer threshold
\[
 \widetilde N(Y)=\min\{n\ge0:Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)\ge Y\}.
\]
For sufficiently large $Y$, its value lies in the increasing tail and the finite earlier values are all below $Y$. Therefore $\widetilde N(Y)=\lceil\widetilde x\rceil$, including equality at an exact integer-indexed moment. Formula~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:inverse-shift} implies existence of a positive function $\eta(x)\to0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:ceilings}
 \left\lceil x-\frac{D s(x)}{p\log x}-\eta(x)\frac{s(x)}{\log x}\right\rceil
 \le\widetilde N(Y)\le
 \left\lceil x-\frac{D s(x)}{p\log x}+\eta(x)\frac{s(x)}{\log x}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
The ceilings eventually coincide or are consecutive. Neither $\eta$ nor the onset is effective here. A tiny real displacement does not force equality of the two integer thresholds near a boundary, and it does not justify an unconditional single ceiling.

\begin{remark}[{[write]} The inverse and the transseries volume, 7~October 2026]\label{pwd:rc:rem:transseries}
Against the volume named in the Guide (its symbols carry the subscript
``vol'').
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Instance.} The identity $\widetilde N(Y)=\lceil\widetilde x\rceil$
used for~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ceilings} is \file{p0:thm:staircase}(1) for the
piecewise-linear interpolation $\widetilde F$ of $\log Z_n(\widetilde\lambda)$,
an admissible interpolation in the sense of \file{p0:def:three-inverses} on
the increasing tail; the bracket~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:ceilings} then follows from
the displacement estimate by monotonicity of the ceiling, the setting of
\file{p0:thm:staircase}(2).
\item \emph{Analogue.} Theorem~\ref{pwd:rc:thm:inverse} divides a residual by
a slope, as \file{p0:thm:backward-error} does, but with secant slopes of a
piecewise-linear function rather than a derivative; it is an analogue of that
theorem, not an instance.
\item \emph{Not compared.} The base inverse $x$ is exact for the base
sequence's interpolation and is not an explicit Lambert or core solution, so
the volume's core theorems do not apply to it as stated.
\end{enumerate}
No novelty is claimed for any of these inversions.
\end{remark}

\section{What the present results do not yet establish}\label{pwd:rc:sec:limits}
The cumulative theorem can use information inaccessible to a pointwise coefficient theorem, but it still requires both parts of \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cumulative-hyp}. This distinction matters in the Freud comparison underlying Report 201. The directly inspected CKM restatement \cite[Eq.~(2.3)]{ckm} supplies, after the established norm telescoping and Stirling calculation, a cumulative coefficient $(p-1)/12$, but its common recurrence envelope is only $O(1/(h\log^2h))$. That does not imply $O(h^{-2})$. Differentiating an unspecified norm error is not legitimate, and qualitative local occupation convergence does not repair the missing envelope.

A sharper normalized leading-coefficient bound reported in Mastroianni and Milovanovi\'c \cite[Theorem 2.4.10]{mastroianni} suggests a route for $p\ge4$, but original-primary verification and source-image verification of that stronger statement are outside the cleared source basis of this report. We therefore record only the following conditional implication, without presenting it as a proved general-power asymptotic: if the Freud comparison has both
\[
 |f_h|=O(h^{-2}),\qquad
 \sum_{h\le N}f_h=\log P+\frac{p-1}{12N}+o(N^{-1}),
\]
then cumulative transfer and the explicit gamma moment would yield
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:conditional}
 \frac{Z_n(h^p)}{F_p(n)}=1+\frac{C_p}{n}+o(n^{-1}),\qquad
 C_p=\frac{p^2-6p+2}{24p}+\frac{p-1}{12}J_p.
\end{equation}
For the bookkeeping, the comparison moment is
\[
 V_n=\left(\frac4{s^2}\right)^n\frac{\Gamma(pn+p/2)}{\Gamma(p/2)},
 \qquad s=(pI_p)^{p/2}.
\]
Its Stirling coefficient relative to the factorial template is
$B_2(p/2)/(2p)-p/12=(p^2-6p+2)/(24p)$, where $B_2(z)=z^2-z+1/6$.
Dividing by the hypothetical cumulative comparison ratio adds $(p-1)J_p/12$, with the sign in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:conditional}. The formula is a conditional algebraic target. For $p=4$ it agrees with the independently proved coefficient $-(1+\pi)/16$. Such agreement and numerical fits do not verify the missing source premise for other $p$. No unconditional new $p\ge4$ or cubic absolute coefficient is claimed.

Several concrete questions remain.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item Can the required Freud envelope be verified directly in primary sources, with every endpoint of its exponent range checked?
\item When $J_{p,q}=0$, what additional tail information determines the next nonzero correction? The present theorem only gives a little-$o$ improvement.
\item What is the occupation scale and limiting law in a shrinking neighborhood of $H_p$? Our local theorem deliberately stops at compact intervals below it.
\item Can the cumulative theorem be extended under weaker pointwise envelopes by proving a quantitative discrete-variation bound for occupation?
\item Which higher corrections involve only tail coefficients and which involve finite-height data? The endpoint regime shows that finite modifications already matter at order $n^{-p}$.
\item Can explicit error bounds and an effective onset make the two-ceiling inverse enclosure into a practical certified threshold algorithm? Direct integer comparisons are required when refining a smooth absolute inverse beyond the curvature error of log-linear interpolation.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{writenote}[The conditional coefficient as an open question, 7 October 2026]
Under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026, the coefficient $C_p$
of~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:conditional} is not a theorem: it is conditional on a
Freud envelope $|f_h|=O(h^{-2})$ with the stated cumulative expansion, which
neither the source nor the write has verified in a primary source
(Mastroianni--Milovanovi\'c is a secondary lead only). It stands as an open
question, Question~1 above. What is known: at $p=2$ it gives $-1/8$, the
proved coefficient~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:pure2}, and at $p=4$ it gives
$-(1+\pi)/16$, the proved coefficient~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:pure4}; both checked by
the write ($J_2=0$, $J_4=-\pi/4$). At $p=3$ it gives
$C_3=-\tfrac7{72}+\tfrac16J_3=-0.19798885356\ldots$, $J_3=-0.6045997880\ldots$;
the write computed $Z_n(h^3)$ exactly for $n\le400$ and found
$n(Z_n(h^3)/F_3(n)-1)=-0.19776\ldots$, $-0.19787\ldots$, $-0.19793\ldots$ at
$n=100$, $200$, $400$, with Richardson values $2e(2n)-e(n)$ equal to
$-0.1979892\ldots$ ($n=100$) and $-0.1979889\ldots$ ($n=200$). This agreement
to about six digits is numerical evidence for the conditional formula at
$p=3$, not a proof, and it says nothing about the envelope itself.
\end{writenote}
\section{Reproduction and source boundaries}\label{pwd:rc:sec:repro}
The accompanying package contains the article's TeX and PDF, deterministic build instructions, exact rational/integer fixtures, executable finite identities, and separately labeled multiprecision diagnostics. Exact checks test finite mechanisms such as Dyck recurrences, marked-excursion deletion, Catalan branch coefficients, telescoping products, and the secant operator. Numerical checks illustrate convergence and inversion scales. Neither type replaces the proofs or supplies an effective asymptotic error enclosure.

Runtime validation uses explicit exceptions and remains active under optimized Python. The package records complete member hashes and distinguishes source files from generated results. Rebuilds from actual ZIP extraction are required to agree member by member and as a whole archive under the documented toolchain; PDF byte identity across different TeX distributions is not asserted. The README specifies commands, dependencies, and the final finite-check counts. No private paths, third-party PDFs, or remote fetches are needed for a normal reproduction.

The mathematical dependence on public sources is narrow. The formal Dyck/continued-fraction interpretation is classical \cite{flajolet}; the quadratic anchor is reproved here, with its prior secant identification explicitly acknowledged \cite{debsokal}. The quartic absolute normalization uses Berg--Valent's stated moment asymptotic with the factor $16^n$ checked explicitly \cite{bergvalent}. The general-power leading formula and Freud sources are cited for context and for the scope limitation, not smuggled into a stronger absolute theorem. The companion Report 201 supplies the origin of the appendix arguments, which are reproduced here so they can be checked without access to the companion. Report 199 supplies the prior report-level quartic result, recovered here as an application rather than claimed anew.

The OEIS examples are normalization anchors. A144853's S-fraction coefficients are identified through A187756, and the exact scaling to A261000 is consistent with their displayed JacobiSD coefficient formulas. A227887 is also identified as column four of the power-weight array A290569. No exhaustive literature search, priority certification, or claim that the classical elliptic formulas are new is made.

% [write] Part III's appendices keep their letters; the section counter is restored at Part IV.
\setcounter{pwdsavesec}{\value{section}}\setcounter{section}{0}\renewcommand{\thesection}{\Alph{section}}\renewcommand{\theHsection}{pwdrcapp.\Alph{section}}
\section{Exact deletion and the uniform endpoint bound}\label{pwd:rc:app:endpoint}
\begin{writenote}[Part~I's counterpart, 7 October 2026]
This appendix is Part~I's Sections~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:ratios}--\ref{pwd:hm:sec:cone}
almost word for word (Lemmas~\ref{pwd:hm:lem:allk}--\ref{pwd:hm:lem:deletion}
and the proof of Theorem~\ref{pwd:hm:thm:endpoint}), with the constant $A$
renamed $A_0$, the interpolation parameter suppressed, and one addition: the
Cauchy--Schwarz consequence $Z_j^2\le Z_{j-1}Z_{j+1}$ (discrete log-convexity),
which Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:ratios} uses. The batch-111 placement proposed
printing it once; it is printed again because that addition and the
references of Section~\ref{pwd:rc:sec:prerequisites} rest on it.
\end{writenote}
This appendix reproduces the endpoint argument of Report 201 with its constants, so the present correction theorems have a complete portable foundation. The parameter $t$ is suppressed; all bounds depend only on the common power class.
For the rest of this section assume \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:powerclass}. Write
\[
 u_n(h)=\E_{n,\lambda}N_h,\qquad
 v_n(h)=\Prob_{n,\lambda}(\max D\ge h),
\]
and set $u_N(j)=0$ when $j>N$.

\begin{lemma}[An all-length ratio bound]\label{pwd:rc:lem:allk}
For every $n\ge1$ and $0\le k\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:allk}
 Z_n(\lambda)\ge c^n(n!)^p\ge(cn^p/\ee^p)^n,
 \qquad
 \frac{Z_{n-k}(\lambda)}{Z_n(\lambda)}
 \le\left(\frac{\ee^p}{cn^p}\right)^k.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take the finite symmetric Jacobi matrix indexed by $0,\ldots,n$, with zero diagonal and entries $J_{h-1,h}=J_{h,h-1}=\sqrt{\lambda_h}$. A closed nearest-neighbor walk from zero of length $2j$, $j\le n$, is a Dyck path and cannot leave this truncation. It traverses each edge equally often in the two directions. Hence
\[
 (J^{2j})_{00}=Z_j(\lambda),\qquad 0\le j\le n.
\]
The spectral theorem, applied to squared eigenvalues, makes these initial moments those of a positive measure on $[0,\infty)$ of mass one. H\"older's inequality gives $Z_{n-k}\le Z_n^{(n-k)/n}$. The same finite spectral representation, with truncation at $j+1$, gives $Z_j^2\le Z_{j-1}Z_{j+1}$ by Cauchy--Schwarz; hence $\log Z_j$ is discrete convex. The mountain $U^nD^n$ contributes at least $c^n(n!)^p$, and $n!\ge(n/\ee)^n$. Dividing the moment inequality by $Z_n$ proves the claim, including $k=0,n$.
\end{proof}
This fixed-$n$ chord bound controls every deletion length at once. No asymptotic one-step ratio is used.

Let $e_{h,k}$ be the total weight of primitive negative excursions of length $2k$ at height $h$: they start at $h$, immediately descend, remain in $\{0,\ldots,h-1\}$ until the last step, then rise to $h$. In particular these excursions remain nonnegative. Their $k$ downsteps have heights at most $h$, so
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:excursion-bound}
 0\le e_{h,k}\le4^kC^kh^{pk}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Exact deletion and slot identity]\label{pwd:rc:lem:deletion}
For $1\le h\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:deletion}
 u_n(h)-v_n(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-h}e_{h,k}\frac{Z_{n-k}}{Z_n}
 \bigl(u_{n-k}(h)+u_{n-k}(h+1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Mark a non-last descent across edge $h$. A later return to $h$ exists; cut out the path from this descent through its first such return. It is a unique primitive negative excursion. Deletion leaves a Dyck path of semilength $n-k$ with a distinguished vertex of height $h$.

Conversely insert any permitted negative excursion at any vertex of height $h$ in the shorter path. Since that path subsequently ends at zero, its later steps include a descent across $h$, so the inserted marked descent is non-last. The constructions are inverse, preserve all other descent heights, and multiply weights. The shorter path must reach $h$, which forces $k\le n-h$.

For $h>0$, a path has exactly $N_h+N_{h+1}$ vertices of height $h$: classify their arrivals as upsteps across edge $h$ or downsteps across edge $h+1$, using upward/downward balance on edge $h$. The number of non-last descents is $N_h-\mathbf1_{\{\max D\ge h\}}$. Weighted summation gives \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:deletion}.
\end{proof}
Put
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:A-a}
 A_0=\frac{4C\ee^p}{c},\qquad a(n,h)=A_0(h/n)^p.
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:allk}--\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:deletion} imply
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cone-recursion}
 u_n(h)\le1+\sum_{k=1}^{n-h}a(n,h)^k
 \bigl(u_{n-k}(h)+u_{n-k}(h+1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
All quantities in this recursion are nonnegative, a fact used in the deliberate overcounting below.

\subsection{A rough bound followed by the endpoint error}
Choose constants
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:cone-constants}
 \alpha=\min\left(\frac14,(12A_0)^{-1/p}\right),\quad
 \eta=A_0\alpha^p\le\frac1{12},\quad r_0=3\eta\le\frac14,\quad
 \kappa_0=\frac{\alpha}{2(1+\alpha)}.
\end{equation}
We first prove a rough bound in a wider cone and then extract the small error in a narrower one.

Start with $h\le\alpha n/2$. Expand \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:cone-recursion} recursively at every pair $(N,j)$ with $j\le\alpha N$; stop at the first exit from this outer cone. The expansion is finite because $N$ strictly decreases at every edge. Inside the cone the coefficient of a loss $k$ is at most $\eta^k$.

For total loss $s$ and $\ell$ recursive edges, the positive compositions of $s$ supply $\binom{s-1}{\ell-1}$ possibilities, and at each edge there are two height choices, preserving or increasing $j$. Thus the total coefficient of all chains with loss $s\ge1$ is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:chain-count}
 \sum_{\ell=1}^s2^\ell\binom{s-1}{\ell-1}\eta^s
 =2\,3^{s-1}\eta^s=\frac23r_0^s.
\end{equation}
This includes impossible chains and therefore only enlarges a nonnegative upper bound. The constants inserted at internal nodes sum to at most
$1+2\eta/(1-3\eta)$.

A terminal exit has coordinates $(n-s,h+t)$, where $0\le t\le s$. Since $h+t>\alpha(n-s)$ but $h\le\alpha n/2$, it must have $s>\kappa_0n$. Every terminal occupation is at most $n$; an impossible child has occupation zero. Therefore all exit terms together are at most
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:exit-bound}
 \frac{2n}{3(1-r_0)}r_0^{\kappa_0n}
 \le\frac{2}{3(1-r_0)\ee\kappa_0\log(1/r_0)}.
\end{equation}
The last inequality uses $\sup_{x\ge0}x\ee^{-bx}=1/(\ee b)$. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:B-bound}
 u_n(h)\le B_0:=1+\frac{2\eta}{1-3\eta}
 +\frac{2}{3(1-r_0)\ee\kappa_0\log(1/r_0)}
 \quad(1\le h\le\alpha n/2).
\end{equation}

Now take $n\ge8/\alpha$ and $h\le\alpha n/8$. If $k\le n/2$, then
\[
 h+1\le\alpha n/4\le\alpha(n-k)/2,
\]
so both child occupations in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:deletion} are at most $B_0$. For $k>n/2$ use $n$. With $a=a(n,h)\le\eta/8^p<1/4$, summing the geometric bounds gives
\begin{align}\label{pwd:rc:eq:nonlast}
 0\le u_n(h)-v_n(h)
 &\le\frac{2B_0a+2n a^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}}{1-a}\notag\\
 &\le\frac83(B_0+1)a.
\end{align}
Here $n4^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\le1$ for $n\ge2$. A path with maximum below $h$ has at most $4^n$ possible step patterns and weight at most $(Ch^p)^n$. The mountain bound yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:not-reaching}
 1-v_n(h)\le[A_0(h/n)^p]^n=a^n\le a.
\end{equation}
Finally the identity
\[
 |N_h-1|=(N_h-\mathbf1_{\{\max D\ge h\}})+\mathbf1_{\{\max D<h\}}
\]
holds pathwise. Equations~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:nonlast}--\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:not-reaching} prove the endpoint part of Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:foundations} with the explicit, nonoptimal choices
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:rho}
 \rho=\alpha/8,\qquad n_0=\lceil8/\alpha\rceil,\qquad
 C_1=A_0\left[1+\frac83(B_0+1)\right].
\end{equation}
All constants depend only on $p,c,C$. They can become large as $p\downarrow0$; the theorem fixes $p>0$ and asserts no uniformity in that limit.


\section{Uniform arch concentration and free energy}\label{pwd:rc:app:shape}
\begin{writenote}[Part~I's counterpart, 7 October 2026]
This appendix adapts Part~I's Section~\ref{pwd:hm:sec:shape} to the scaled
family $\kappa h^p\ee^{g_h(t)}$ with uniformly vanishing $g_h$, which
Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:foundations} needs, and its final subsection evaluates
the free energy $\Phi_p(y_*)=\log d_p-p$, which Part~I does not print. It is
kept in full for these two reasons; the closed-set bound, the calibration and
the lower bound are Part~I's arguments.
\end{writenote}
This appendix adapts the direct concentration proof of Report 201 to the family needed here. Suppose
\[
 \lambda_h(t)=\kappa h^p\ee^{g_h(t)},\qquad
 \sup_{h,t}|g_h(t)|<\infty,\qquad
 \sup_t|g_h(t)|\longrightarrow0\quad(h\to\infty).
\]
These conditions follow from the fixed power class and uniform asymptotic equivalence in Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:foundations}. No continuity in $t$ is needed. In particular geometric interpolation has $g_h(t)=\log(\lambda_h/(\kappa h^p))+tf_h$. A bounded pathwise density comparison with the pure-power measure would in general be false because finite-height changes can be repeated $n$ times; the argument therefore treats the family directly.

Interpolate the path heights linearly, with time divided by $n$ and height by $n$. Thus $y_n(s)$, $0\le s\le2$, belongs to the compact uniform-topology space
\[
 \K=\{y:[0,2]\to[0,\infty):y(0)=y(2)=0,\quad y\text{ is }1\text{-Lipschitz}\}.
\]
Every $y\in\K$ satisfies $y(s)\le\min(s,2-s)\le1$. Compactness follows from uniform boundedness, equicontinuity, and closure.

For $|v|\le1$ define the binary entropy
\[
 \mathcal E(v)=-\frac{1+v}{2}\log\frac{1+v}{2}
              -\frac{1-v}{2}\log\frac{1-v}{2},\qquad0\log0=0,
\]
and the extended-real functionals
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:variational}
 S(y)=\int_0^2\mathcal E(y'(s))\dd s,\quad
 V(y)=\frac p2\int_0^2\log y(s)\dd s,\quad
 \Phi_p(y)=S(y)+V(y).
\end{equation}
Lipschitz functions are differentiable almost everywhere. A divergent negative logarithmic integral means $V(y)=-\infty$.

If $H_j$ is the upper endpoint height of step $j$, upward/downward edge balance gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:step-weight}
 \log\frac{W_{\lambda(t)}(D)}{\kappa^n n^{pn}}
 =\frac12\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\left[p\log(H_j/n)+g_{H_j}(t)\right].
\end{equation}
The factor $1/2$ is essential: each weighted descent is paired with the corresponding number of ascents across its edge.

\subsection{A clipped closed-set upper bound}
Fix $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ and an integer $M\ge1$. Set
\[
 F=\max(0,\sup_{h,t}g_h(t)),\qquad\omega_M=\sup_{h>M,t}|g_h(t)|\longrightarrow0.
\]
For $n\ge(M/\varepsilon)\ee^{F/p}$, every $h\ge1$ and $0\le t\le1$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:clipping}
 p\log(h/n)+g_h(t)\le p\log\max(h/n,\varepsilon)+\omega_M.
\end{equation}
For $h>M$ the perturbation is bounded by $\omega_M$. For $h\le M$, the chosen $n$ gives $p\log(h/n)+F\le p\log\varepsilon$. Thus the finitely many large low-height values are absorbed into the clipping slack, rather than charged an uncontrolled $O(n)$ error.

For a finite partition $Q=(s_i)$ of $[0,2]$, define
\[
 S_Q(y)=\sum_i(s_{i+1}-s_i)\mathcal E\left(\frac{y(s_{i+1})-y(s_i)}{s_{i+1}-s_i}\right),\qquad
 V_\varepsilon(y)=\frac p2\int_0^2\log\max(y(s),\varepsilon)\dd s.
\]
Both are continuous on $\K$. Nested dyadic partitions give $S_Q\downarrow S$: Jensen's inequality gives monotonicity, and differentiation of Lipschitz functions with bounded convergence identifies the limit. Likewise $V_\varepsilon\downarrow V$ as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, by monotone convergence applied to the nonnegative function $-\log\max(y,\varepsilon)$.

Let $B\subseteq\K$ be closed. Divide the $2n$ steps into blocks at rounded times $ns_i$. There are only polynomially many vectors of block endpoint heights. For a block of length $L$ and displacement $d$, the bridge count is at most
\[
 \binom{L}{(L+d)/2}\le\exp\{L\mathcal E(d/L)\};
\]
impossible parities give no bridge, and nonnegativity can only decrease the count. For fixed $Q$, rounding errors in normalized entropy are $o(1)$ uniformly over paths, by continuity of $\mathcal E$. The step upper height divided by $n$ differs from $y_n(s)$ by at most $1/n$ within its interval. Since the clipped logarithm is $1/\varepsilon$-Lipschitz, \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:step-weight}--\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:clipping} give
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:clipped-upper}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\frac1n
 \log\left(\kappa^{-n}n^{-pn}\sum_{D:y_n\in B}W_{\lambda(t)}(D)\right)
 \le\sup_{y\in B}(S_Q(y)+V_\varepsilon(y))+\omega_M.
\end{equation}
The conventions are $\log0=-\infty$ and $\sup\varnothing=-\infty$.

First let $M\to\infty$. Then take nested dyadic partitions and decreasing $\varepsilon\to0$. For a decreasing sequence of continuous functions on a compact set, the maxima converge to the supremum of their pointwise upper-semicontinuous limit. Indeed choose maximizing points, take a convergent subsequence, and bound its limiting values by each fixed earlier function; the reverse inequality is immediate. Applying this to the compact set $B$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:closed-upper}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_t\frac1n
 \log\left(\kappa^{-n}n^{-pn}\sum_{D:y_n\in B}W_{\lambda(t)}(D)\right)
 \le\sup_{y\in B}\Phi_p(y).
\end{equation}
It also shows that $\Phi_p$ is upper semicontinuous. This is the closed-set large-deviation upper estimate actually needed below.

\subsection{The unique calibrated arch}
Define $y_*$ increasingly on $[0,1]$ by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:arch-definition}
 s=\int_0^{y_*(s)}\frac{\dd v}{\sqrt{1-(v/H_p)^p}},
\end{equation}
and reflect about $s=1$. Since $H_pI_p=1$, its maximum is $H_p$, and $y_*\in\K$. In the interior,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:arch-equations}
 (y_*')^2=1-(y_*/H_p)^p,\qquad
 y_*''=-\frac p{2H_p^p}y_*^{p-1}.
\end{equation}
The second identity extends through the top by continuation. At either endpoint, height divided by the distance to that endpoint tends to one.

\begin{lemma}[Calibration and uniqueness]\label{pwd:rc:lem:calibration}
The functional $\Phi_p$ has the unique maximizer $y_*$ on $\K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 A_*(s)=\mathcal E'(y_*'(s))
 =\frac12\log\frac{1-y_*'(s)}{1+y_*'(s)}.
\]
Equations~\eqref{pwd:rc:eq:arch-equations} give
\[
 A_*'(s)=\frac{p}{2y_*(s)},\qquad
 A_*(s)=O(1+|\log s|+|\log(2-s)|).
\]
The entropy and logarithm tangent inequalities are
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal E(y')-\mathcal E(y_*')&\le A_*(y'-y_*'),\\
 \frac p2(\log y-\log y_*)&\le\frac p2\frac{y-y_*}{y_*}.
\end{align*}
Integrate on $[d,2-d]$ and integrate the first right side by parts. The interior terms cancel. The remaining boundary term
$[A_*(y-y_*)]_d^{2-d}$ is $O(d|\log d|)$ because both heights are $O(d)$ at the endpoints.

The tangent terms are integrable: $A_*$ is integrable and $(y-y_*)/y_*$ is bounded near the endpoints. If $V(y)=-\infty$ there is nothing to prove; otherwise taking $d\downarrow0$ proves $\Phi_p(y)\le\Phi_p(y_*)$. If $y\ne y_*$, continuity makes them different on a set of positive measure, and the logarithmic tangent deficit has strictly positive integral,
\[
 \frac p2\int_0^2\left[\frac y{y_*}-1-\log\frac y{y_*}\right]\dd s>0,
\]
possibly infinite. The strict inequality proves uniqueness, including nonsmooth competitors, zero plateaus, and multiple arches.
\end{proof}
For every fixed $\eta_0>0$, compactness and upper semicontinuity therefore give a strict gap
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:gap}
 \sup_{\|y-y_*\|_\infty\ge\eta_0}\Phi_p(y)<\Phi_p(y_*).
\end{equation}

\subsection{A matching lower bound with controlled endpoints}
We provide the lower bound explicitly because the logarithm is singular at zero. Approximate $y_*$ by positive polygons $z\in\K$ that equal $s$ on $[0,\varepsilon]$ and $2-s$ on $[2-\varepsilon,2]$, and have all interior slopes strictly between $-1$ and $1$. For example join $(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$ to $(2\varepsilon,y_*(2\varepsilon))$, use fine chords of $y_*$ on the remaining interior, and reflect at the end. The joining slope is greater than $-1$, less than $1$, and tends to $1$ as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.

These approximants converge uniformly to $y_*$, their derivatives converge almost everywhere, and they satisfy $z(s)\ge c_0\min(s,2-s)$ with a common $c_0>0$ for sufficiently advanced approximants. The bounded entropy and an integrable constant plus $|\log\min(s,2-s)|$ dominate the two integrands. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:polygon-approx}
 \Phi_p(z)\longrightarrow\Phi_p(y_*).
\end{equation}
Fix one polygon. Refine its linear interior pieces into blocks of maximal length $d$ without changing $z$. Choose lattice times and parity-compatible lattice heights within $O(1)$ of their prescribed values $ns_i,nz(s_i)$. Take deterministic all-up and all-down endpoint pieces of rounded lengths. For large $n$, the fixed positive slack of every interior slope from $\pm1$ guarantees feasible bridges.

Let $b_z=\min_{[\varepsilon,2-\varepsilon]}z>0$. Every unrestricted bridge in an interior block stays within $2d+O(1/n)$ of $z$, since both its slopes and those of $z$ have absolute value at most one.
