Choosing $d$ sufficiently small relative to $b_z$ makes \emph{every} such bridge positive. Their concatenations are consequently admissible Dyck paths. Distinct bridge choices give distinct paths. Stirling's formula for the finitely many binomial factors gives
\[
 \log\#\{\text{constructed paths}\}=nS(z)+o(n).
\]
The endpoint pieces have zero entropy.

On the interior, the logarithm is uniformly Lipschitz and the normalized pure-power log weights approach the \emph{interior contribution} to $nV(z)$, with error $nO_z(d)+o(n)$. The deterministic endpoints contribute separately
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:endpoint-potential}
 p\sum_{h=1}^{\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor}\log(h/n)+O(\log n),
\end{equation}
whose quotient by $n$ tends to $p\int_0^\varepsilon\log s\dd s$. This is exactly the two-endpoint contribution to $V(z)$, including the factor $1/2$ in \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:step-weight}.

The perturbation is uniformly $o(n)$ over all these paths and all $t$. On the deterministic endpoints its absolute contribution is at most
$\sum_{h\le\varepsilon n+O(1)}\sup_t|g_h(t)|=o(n)$ by Ces\`aro convergence. On the interior all heights are at least $c_zn$, so the absolute contribution is at most
$n\sup_{h\ge c_zn,t}|g_h(t)|=o(n)$. It follows that
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_t\frac1n\log[\kappa^{-n}n^{-pn}Z_n(\lambda(t))]
 \ge\Phi_p(z)-O_z(d).
\]
The limits are taken in order: first $n\to\infty$ at fixed polygon and refinement, then $d\downarrow0$, then improve the polygon. Combining with \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:closed-upper} for $B=\K$ proves the uniform variational limit
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:free-energy}
 \sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left|\frac1n\log[\kappa^{-n}n^{-pn}Z_n(\lambda(t))]-\Phi_p(y_*)\right|
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:closed-upper} on the closed complement of an $\eta_0$-ball and the gap \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:gap} yields the needed conclusion.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform fixed-neighborhood concentration]\label{pwd:rc:prop:concentration}
For every fixed $p>0$, family $g_h(t)$ satisfying the displayed uniform hypotheses, and fixed $\eta_0>0$, there are $c_{\eta_0}>0$ and $n_{\eta_0}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:rc:eq:concentration}
 \sup_{0\le t\le1}\Prob_{n,t}(\|y_n-y_*\|_\infty\ge\eta_0)
 \le\ee^{-c_{\eta_0}n}\qquad(n\ge n_{\eta_0}).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
Only fixed-neighborhood concentration and the displayed closed-set upper estimate have been proved here. No rate for shrinking neighborhoods, fluctuation theorem, or full lower large-deviation principle on arbitrary open sets is asserted.


\subsection{Evaluation of the free energy constant}
Set $v=y_*'$ and use $A_*=\mathcal E'(v)$ from the calibration proof. Since $1-v^2=(y_*/H_p)^p$,
\[
 \mathcal E(v)+\frac p2\log y_*
 =\log2+\frac p2\log H_p+v A_*.
\]
Also $A_*'=p/(2y_*)$, and $y_* A_*$ tends to zero at both endpoints. Integrating by parts,
\[
 \int_0^2 y_*' A_*\dd s=-\int_0^2 y_* A_*'\dd s=-p.
\]
Consequently $\Phi_p(y_*)=\log4+p\log H_p-p=\log d_p-p$. The uniform variational limit therefore becomes exactly \eqref{pwd:rc:eq:free-energy-main}, including the factor $\kappa^n$. This completes the proof of every assertion of Lemma~\ref{pwd:rc:lem:foundations}.


\part{Diagonal power weighted Dyck paths: all orders asymptotics and integer safe inversion for OEIS A291333}\label{pwd:dg:part}
\setcounter{section}{\value{pwdsavesec}}\renewcommand{\thesection}{\arabic{section}}\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\arabic{section}}\setlength{\parindent}{17pt}\setlength{\parskip}{0pt plus 1pt}
\partsource{Bundle Report~204, archive \file{Report204.zip}, delivered as \file{Report204.tex} (16 pages). Delivered title block ``Diagonal power weighted Dyck paths / All orders asymptotics and integer safe inversion for OEIS A291333 / Report 204 / 4 October 2026''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} remarks and notes; Report~204's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+43$ here, with its statements and equations. Its labels carry \file{pwd:dg:}; its two unlabelled sections were labelled; its citation key \file{AD} is \file{avdoshkin}. This Part is self-contained.}
\begin{partabstract}
Let $Z_n(p)$ be the sum, over Dyck paths of semilength $n$, of the product of the $p$th powers of the descent heights. We give an exact staircase-partition representation and prove a full expansion for $Z_n(\tau n+\sigma)/(n!)^{\tau n+\sigma}$, uniform for $\tau$ in a fixed compact positive interval and bounded $\sigma$. The expansion holds in an exponentially weighted $\ell^1$ norm at every fixed order. Its coefficients are Boltzmann partition moments and can be computed by finite mixed-moment or row-box algorithms. A separate scalar estimate is uniform in linearly growing truncation order and proves an exponentially accurate specified truncation. We also obtain the first total-variation correction, analytic coefficient-cutoff bounds, and an all-fixed-order Lambert $W$ inverse with an integer-safe two-ceiling enclosure. The diagonal $Z_n(n)$ is OEIS A291333. Its leading equivalent, with constant $\prod_{j\ge1}(1-e^{-j})^{-1}$, was recorded by V\'aclav Kot\v e\v sovec in the OEIS in 2017 and updated in 2018; it is re-proved here, not claimed as new. Exact checks and high-precision diagnostics accompany the proofs in a reproducible source archive.
\end{partabstract}

\section{Model and scope}\label{pwd:dg:sec:model}
A Dyck path in $\D_n$ starts at height zero, has $n$ upsteps and $n$ downsteps, never goes below zero, and ends at zero. If $h_i$ is the height immediately before its $i$th downstep, set
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:model}
 Z_n(p)=\sum_{P\in\D_n}\prod_{i=1}^n h_i(P)^p,\qquad p\in\mathbb R.
\end{equation}
All heights in this product are positive integers, so real powers cause no ambiguity. Empty products give $Z_0(p)=1$. The formal continued fraction is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:sf}
 \sum_{n\ge0}Z_n(p)x^n
 =\cfrac{1}{1-\cfrac{1^p x}{1-\cfrac{2^p x}{1-\cfrac{3^p x}{1-\cdots}}}}.
\end{equation}
To see this, let $F_h$ count excursions above height $h$. Decomposition into a sequence of arches gives $F_h=(1-(h+1)^p xF_{h+1})^{-1}$. Each coefficient uses only finitely many heights. Thus \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:sf} is a formal identity, with no analytic convergence assertion.

The array A290569 has entries $Z_n(k)$, and its main diagonal A291333 is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:diagonal}
 a_n=Z_n(n).
\end{equation}
Both identifications are recorded in the OEIS \cite{oeis291333,oeis290569}. For example, the five paths of semilength three have descent-height products $6,4,2,2,1$, giving $a_3=6^3+4^3+2^3+2^3+1=297$.

The regime studied here is $p=\tau n+\sigma$. The fixed-column regime $p=k$ does not supply uniform estimates in this regime. Throughout, unless stated otherwise,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:compact}
 0<a\le\tau\le b<\infty,\qquad |\sigma|\le M,\qquad 0\le M<\infty.
\end{equation}
The constants $a,b,M$ are fixed. An expansion to every fixed order means a Poincar\'e expansion: for each chosen order there is a corresponding remainder bound. Section~\ref{pwd:dg:sec:gevrey} separately proves a scalar bound uniform in a growing order. It does not establish convergence, divergence, Borel summability, an optimal truncation rule, a true exponential scale, or a full transseries. No assertion here covers $\tau\to0$ or $\tau\to\infty$.

\paragraph{Prior leading equivalent.}
Write
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Q}
 Q(\tau)=\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}(1-e^{-\tau j})^{-1}
       =\sum_{\lambda\in\Part}e^{-\tau|\lambda|},
\end{equation}
where $\Part$ includes the empty partition. The OEIS attributes $a_n\sim Q(1)(n!)^n$ to Kot\v e\v sovec, 26 August 2017, updated 21 July 2018 \cite{oeis291333}. The constant also occurs in a different tree/partition sequence, A317103 \cite{oeis317103}. Our purpose is a proof with uniform corrections, quantitative partition-law refinements, explicit computation and inversion. This article makes no global priority claim.

\begin{remark}[{[write]} The OEIS entries, quoted, 7~October 2026]\label{pwd:dg:rem:oeis}
OEIS A291333, read on 7~October 2026 in the internal format, is at revision
\#12 (21~July 2018), author Ilya Gutkovskiy (22~August 2017), offset~0; its
name is ``a(n) = [x\^{}n] 1/(1 - x/(1 - 2\^{}n*x/(1 - 3\^{}n*x/(1 - 4\^{}n*x/(1 -
5\^{}n*x/(1 - ...)))))), a continued fraction.'', its formula lines are ``a(n) =
A290569(n,n).'' and Kot\v e\v sovec's ``a(n) \textasciitilde{} c * (n!)\^{}n
\textasciitilde{} c * 2\^{}(n/2) * Pi\^{}(n/2) * n\^{}(n*(2*n+1)/2) /
exp(n\^{}2-1/12), where c = 1/QPochhammer(exp(-1)) =
1.9824409074128737036856824655613120156828827...'' (26~August 2017, updated

\end{remark}

\section{An exact partition identity}\label{pwd:dg:sec:exact}
Let $\delta_n=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0)$ be the staircase. Partitions are weakly decreasing nonnegative row lengths with trailing zeros suppressed. Row and column indices start at one.

\begin{proposition}\label{pwd:dg:prop:bijection}
There is a bijection between $\D_n$ and the partitions $\lambda\subseteq\delta_n$ such that
\begin{align}
 R_n(\tau,\sigma)&:=\frac{Z_n(\tau n+\sigma)}{(n!)^{\tau n+\sigma}}
 =\sum_{\lambda\subseteq\delta_n}r_n(\lambda)^{\tau n+\sigma},\label{pwd:dg:eq:partition}\\
 r_n(\lambda)&=\prod_{i=1}^n\left(1-\frac{\lambda_i}{n-i+1}\right)
 =\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\frac{n-i-j+1}{n-i-j+2}.\label{pwd:dg:eq:cell}
\end{align}
Every factor is positive. Every partition with $|\lambda|<n$ lies in $\delta_n$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $u_i$ be the number of upsteps before downstep $i$ and put $\lambda_i=n-u_i$. The numbers $u_i$ are nondecreasing, $i\le u_i\le n$, and $u_n=n$. These are precisely the conditions that $\lambda\subseteq\delta_n$. Conversely, before each successive downstep insert enough upsteps to reach $u_i=n-\lambda_i$. The conditions above ensure a unique Dyck path. Its $i$th descent height is $u_i-i+1=n-i+1-\lambda_i$. Division by $n!$ gives the first product in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:cell}; telescoping each row gives the second.

If a partition has size $m$, then $\lambda_i\le m-i+1$ whenever row $i$ is present, since the preceding $i-1$ rows are nonempty. For $m<n$ this is at most $n-i$, proving the last claim.
\end{proof}

Define the statistics
\begin{align}
 D_k(\lambda)&=\frac1k\sum_i\bigl((i-1+\lambda_i)^k-(i-1)^k\bigr)\label{pwd:dg:eq:Drow}\\
 &=\frac1k\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\bigl((i+j-1)^k-(i+j-2)^k\bigr),\qquad k\ge1.\label{pwd:dg:eq:Dcell}
\end{align}
Telescoping proves the second formula. It is symmetric in row and column indices, so every $D_k$ is invariant under partition conjugation. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Dbound}
 D_1(\lambda)=m:=|\lambda|,\qquad 0\le D_k(\lambda)\le m^k.
\end{equation}
Indeed, a cell $(i,j)$ forces at least $i+j-1$ cells in its row and column together. Thus $i+j-1\le m$, and the mean value theorem bounds its divided difference in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Dcell} by $m^{k-1}$.

Set $\rho(\lambda)=\max_{i:\lambda_i>0}(i-1+\lambda_i)$, with $\rho(\varnothing)=0$. For $|z|\rho(\lambda)<1$ define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:g}
 g_\lambda(z;\tau,\sigma)=
 \exp\left(-\tau\sum_{k\ge1}D_k z^{k-1}-\sigma\sum_{k\ge1}D_k z^k\right).
\end{equation}
These series converge in the stated disk by \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Drow}. Since $\rho(\lambda)\le n-1$ in the staircase, the convergent expansion of each row logarithm gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:gphysical}
 g_\lambda(1/n;\tau,\sigma)=r_n(\lambda)^{\tau n+\sigma}
 \quad (\lambda\subseteq\delta_n).
\end{equation}
This remains valid for staircase shapes with $m>n$. The smaller disk $|z|m<1$ used for estimates below is a sufficient estimation domain, not the full domain of the identity.

Put $A_r=\tau D_{r+1}+\sigma D_r$ and define $B_r=B_r(\lambda;\tau,\sigma)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Bdef}
 g_\lambda(z)=e^{-\tau m}\sum_{r\ge0}B_rz^r,
 \qquad B_0=1,\qquad rB_r=-\sum_{j=1}^rjA_jB_{r-j}.
\end{equation}
The recurrence follows by differentiating the formal exponential $\exp(-\sum_{j\ge1}A_jz^j)$. For later use,
\begin{align}
 B_1&=-A_1,& B_2&=\tfrac12A_1^2-A_2,\nonumber\\
 B_3&=-A_3+A_1A_2-\tfrac16A_1^3,&
 B_4&=-A_4+A_1A_3+\tfrac12A_2^2-\tfrac12A_1^2A_2+\tfrac1{24}A_1^4.\label{pwd:dg:eq:firstB}
\end{align}

\section{Uniform expansion in weighted summation norm}\label{pwd:dg:sec:uniform}
The identity \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Q} follows by expanding independent geometric series. It is finite for every $\tau>0$: for example, $-\log(1-e^{-\tau j})\le e^{-\tau j}/(1-e^{-\tau})$, a summable majorant.

\begin{theorem}\label{pwd:dg:thm:uniform}
Assume \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:compact}. Fix an integer $K\ge0$ and $0\le\eta<a/2$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:coeff}
 c_r(\tau,\sigma)=\frac1{Q(\tau)}\sum_{\lambda\in\Part}
      e^{-\tau|\lambda|}B_r(\lambda;\tau,\sigma).
\end{equation}
These sums are absolutely convergent, $c_0=1$, and uniformly in $\tau,\sigma$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:weighted}
 \sum_{\lambda\in\Part}e^{\eta|\lambda|}
 \left|\ind_{\lambda\subseteq\delta_n}g_\lambda(1/n)
 -e^{-\tau|\lambda|}\sum_{r=0}^KB_r(\lambda)n^{-r}\right|
 =O(n^{-K-1}).
\end{equation}
The first term is defined to be zero outside the staircase. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:main}
 R_n(\tau,\sigma)=Q(\tau)\left(\sum_{r=0}^Kc_r(\tau,\sigma)n^{-r}
                                      +O(n^{-K-1})\right).
\end{equation}
The constants depend only on $a,b,M,K$ and, in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:weighted}, the fixed $\eta$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose $0<\delta<1/2$ with $b\delta/(1-\delta)\le a/4$, and let
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Cdelta}
 C=\exp\left(\frac{M\delta}{1-\delta}\right).
\end{equation}
For $m\ge1$ and $|z|\le\delta/m$, \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Dbound} gives
\begin{align*}
 \left|\tau\sum_{k\ge2}D_kz^{k-1}\right|
  &\le \frac{bm\delta}{1-\delta}\le\frac{am}4,\\
 \left|\sigma\sum_{k\ge1}D_kz^k\right|
  &\le\frac{M\delta}{1-\delta}.
\end{align*}
Hence $|g_\lambda(z)|\le Ce^{-3am/4}$. Cauchy's estimate gives the uniform bound
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:cauchy}
 |e^{-\tau m}B_r(\lambda)|\le Ce^{-3am/4}(m/\delta)^r.
\end{equation}
For $m\le\delta n/2$, summing the coefficient majorants after order $K$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:smallremainder}
 \left|g_\lambda(1/n)-e^{-\tau m}\sum_{r=0}^KB_rn^{-r}\right|
 \le 2Ce^{-3am/4}\left(\frac{m}{\delta n}\right)^{K+1}.
\end{equation}
All these partitions fit the staircase by Proposition~\ref{pwd:dg:prop:bijection}. After multiplication by $e^{\eta m}$, the sum of \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:smallremainder} is $O(n^{-K-1})$, since $3a/4-\eta>0$ and every fixed polynomial moment of the partition series converges.

Here are explicit tail justifications needing no asymptotic formula for the partition numbers $p(m)$. For $c>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:tail}
 \sum_{m>N}p(m)e^{-cm}\le e^{-cN/2}Q(c/2).
\end{equation}
Polynomial factors can be absorbed into a smaller exponential. For $n\ge2M/a$, the exponent $\tau n+\sigma$ is positive. From the row product, $-\log(1-x)\ge x$ and $n-i+1\le n$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:actualtail}
 0\le\ind_{\lambda\subseteq\delta_n}g_\lambda(1/n)
 \le e^{-(\tau+\sigma/n)m}\le e^{-am/2}.
\end{equation}
The actual-weight tail $m>\delta n/2$ is exponentially small in the weighted norm because $a/2-\eta>0$. The finitely many coefficient tails are exponentially small by \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:cauchy}, since $3a/4-\eta>0$. This proves \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:weighted}; the empty partition contributes exactly $1$ and has no positive-order remainder. The same coefficient estimate proves absolute convergence in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:coeff}. Summing \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:weighted} at $\eta=0$ proves \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:main}; $1\le Q(\tau)\le Q(a)$ is uniform.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Observables and normalized laws}
Give a partition the probability
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:law}
 P_{n,\tau,\sigma}(\lambda)=
 \frac{\ind_{\lambda\subseteq\delta_n}g_\lambda(1/n)}{R_n(\tau,\sigma)},
 \qquad \nu_\tau(\lambda)=\frac{e^{-\tau|\lambda|}}{Q(\tau)}.
\end{equation}
This is the weighted path law transported by the bijection. If an observable $f$ satisfies $|f(\lambda)|\le C_f e^{\eta|\lambda|}$ with a fixed $\eta<a/2$, then \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:weighted} can be multiplied and summed against $f$. All fixed polynomials in $m,D_2,D_3,\ldots$ satisfy such a bound with any fixed positive $\eta$.

For a fully explicit normalized expansion, define $T_0=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:T}
 T_r(\lambda)=B_r(\lambda)-\sum_{j=1}^rc_jT_{r-j}(\lambda).
\end{equation}
Finite series division in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:weighted}, using \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:main} and $Q\ge1$, yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:normalized}
 P_{n,\tau,\sigma}=\nu_\tau\sum_{r=0}^KT_rn^{-r}
                 +O_{\ell^1_\eta}(n^{-K-1}).
\end{equation}
Here $\norm{v}_{\ell^1_\eta}=\sum_\lambda e^{\eta|\lambda|}|v(\lambda)|$. The coefficient sequences have finite weighted norm by the preceding bounds, so all products in this finite division are legitimate. This proves total-variation convergence and convergence of every fixed polynomial moment.

Under $\nu_\tau$, the multiplicities $M_j$ of parts of size $j$ are independent and
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:geometric}
 \Prob(M_j=s)=(1-q^j)q^{js},\qquad q=e^{-\tau},\quad s=0,1,\ldots.
\end{equation}
Indeed the product of these probabilities is $Q(\tau)^{-1}q^{\sum jM_j}$. The sum of $\Prob(M_j>0)$ is finite, so the product law is supported on partitions. Formula \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:normalized} quantifies the approximation to this product law.

\section{Coefficients and coefficient algorithms}\label{pwd:dg:sec:coefficients}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Lambert}
 L_s(q)=\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{j^s q^j}{1-q^j},\qquad q=e^{-\tau}.
\end{equation}
All expectations in this section are under $\nu_\tau$. If $n(\lambda)=\sum_i(i-1)\lambda_i$, then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:D2}
 D_2=n(\lambda)+n(\lambda')+\frac m2,
 \qquad \E D_2=L_2-\frac12L_1.
\end{equation}
For the expectation identity, $n(\lambda')=\frac12\sum_i\lambda_i(\lambda_i-1)$ and conjugation preserves $\nu_\tau$. Also $\E\sum_i\lambda_i^2=L_2$ and $\E m=L_1$, by \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:geometric}.

It follows that
\begin{align}
 c_1(\tau,\sigma)&=-\tau L_2+(\tau/2-\sigma)L_1,\label{pwd:dg:eq:c1}\\
 c_2(\tau,\sigma)&=\tfrac12\E(\tau D_2+\sigma m)^2-\tau\E D_3-\sigma\E D_2.\label{pwd:dg:eq:c2}
\end{align}
Define logarithmic coefficients by
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:ell}
 \log\left(1+\sum_{r\ge1}c_rz^r\right)=\sum_{r\ge1}\ell_rz^r,
 \qquad
 \ell_r=c_r-\frac1r\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}j\ell_jc_{r-j}.
\end{equation}
In particular $\ell_1=c_1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:ell2}
 \ell_2=\tfrac12\Var(\tau D_2+\sigma m)-\tau\E D_3-\sigma\E D_2.
\end{equation}
Taking the logarithm of the positive scalar expansion is justified uniformly: its leading normalized term is $1$, its correction is uniformly $O(n^{-1})$, and the Taylor remainder for $\log(1+w)$ is uniform for $|w|\le1/2$.

For the diagonal we abbreviate $c_r=c_r(1,0)$ and $\ell_r=\ell_r(1,0)$. Then
\begin{align}
 a_n&=(n!)^nQ(1)\left(1+\frac{c_1}{n}+\frac{c_2}{n^2}+\cdots+\frac{c_K}{n^K}
                              +O(n^{-K-1})\right),\label{pwd:dg:eq:diagseries}\\
 c_1&=-\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{j^2-j/2}{e^j-1},\qquad
 c_2=\tfrac12\E D_2^2-\E D_3,\label{pwd:dg:eq:diagcoeff}\\
 c_3&=-\E D_4+\E(D_2D_3)-\tfrac16\E D_2^3.\nonumber
\end{align}
These are fixed, $n$-independent constants, not finite-$n$ fits.

\subsection{Finite mixed moments of multiplicities}\label{pwd:dg:sec:momentengine}
Fix a largest permitted part $J$. Process $j=J,J-1,\ldots,1$. Suppose $S$ rows have already been inserted and $M$ new rows of length $j$ are to be appended. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:update}
 S_{\mathrm{new}}=S+M,\qquad
 (D_k)_{\mathrm{new}}=D_k+\frac1k\sum_{s=0}^{M-1}\bigl((S+s+j)^k-(S+s)^k\bigr).
\end{equation}
For fixed $j$ the summand is a polynomial of total degree at most $k-1$ in $S,s$; summation makes the added term a polynomial of degree at most $k$ in $S,M$. Give $S,M$ weight one and $D_k$ weight $k$. Therefore the vector of mixed moments of weighted degree at most $2K$ is closed under \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:update}. Only the finitely many $D_k$ needed through $D_{K+1}$ enter the coefficients.

The newly drawn $M$ is independent of earlier rows. Its raw moments are explicit:
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:rawmoments}
 \E M^r=\sum_{\ell=0}^r\left\{\begin{matrix}r\\\ell\end{matrix}\right\}
      \ell!\left(\frac{q^j}{1-q^j}\right)^\ell.
\end{equation}
This follows from $M^r=\sum_\ell\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}r\\\ell\end{smallmatrix}\right\}(M)_\ell$ and the geometric generating function; the zeroth moment is one. Expand each updated monomial, take the moments in $M$ by \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:rawmoments}, and use the stored old moments in $S,D_k$. This is a finite exact algebraic algorithm at every chosen $J,K$, with rational functions of the $q^j$ as inputs.

Every term of $B_r$ has weighted degree at most $2r$: a factor $A_j$ has weight at most $j+1\le2j$, and its formal order is $j$. Thus the same closed vector evaluates all $c_r^{(J)}$ for $r\le K$. These are expectations conditional on the largest part being at most $J$; their normalization is
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:QJ}
 Q_J(\tau)=\prod_{j=1}^J(1-e^{-\tau j})^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The moment bounds in Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:uniform} justify convergence as $J\to\infty$. Section~\ref{pwd:dg:sec:cutoff} supplies a diagonal cutoff bound.

\subsection{A separate row-box algorithm}\label{pwd:dg:sec:rowbox}
An independent way to compute the coefficients is to sum shapes contained in an $L\times L$ square. Work with polynomials in $z$ modulo $z^{K+1}$. Define
\begin{align}
 D_{k,i}(j)&=\frac{(i-1+j)^k-(i-1)^k}{k},\nonumber\\
 w_i(j)&=e^{-\tau j}\exp\left[-\sum_{r=1}^K
      \bigl(\tau D_{r+1,i}(j)+\sigma D_{r,i}(j)\bigr)z^r\right]\pmod {z^{K+1}}.
 \label{pwd:dg:eq:rowweight}
\end{align}
Use the boundaries $F(i,0)=1$ and $F(L+1,j)=1$, and compute
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:rowbox}
 F(i,j)=F(i,j-1)+w_i(j)F(i+1,j),
 \quad i=L,L-1,\ldots,1,\quad j=1,\ldots,L.
\end{equation}
The first branch restricts row $i$ to length below $j$; the second fixes its length to $j$ and caps the next row by $j$. The boundaries count the all-zero trailing rows once. Induction proves
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:rowboxsum}
 [z^r]F(1,L)=\sum_{\lambda\subseteq L\times L}e^{-\tau|\lambda|}B_r(\lambda).
\end{equation}
After division by $Q(\tau)$ this is an unnormalized restriction of the full-law coefficient. It is not the conditional multiplicity moment $c_r^{(J)}$ and must not be divided by $Q_J$ by mistake. Naive truncated polynomial multiplication costs $O(L^2K^2)$ arithmetic operations. The two algorithms give genuinely different finite truncations and an effective cross-check.

\section{A uniform scalar Gevrey remainder}\label{pwd:dg:sec:gevrey}
The next theorem keeps track of the order dependence rather than extrapolating a fixed-order big-$O$ statement.

\begin{theorem}\label{pwd:dg:thm:gevrey}
Under \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:compact}, choose $\delta,C$ as in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Cdelta} and set
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:gevconstants}
 B=\frac4{a\delta},\qquad
 A=2CQ(a/2),\qquad A'=(1+2C)Q(a/4).
\end{equation}
For every $r\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:gevcoeff}
 |c_r(\tau,\sigma)|\le CQ(a/2)B^r r!.
\end{equation}
For positive integers $n\ge2M/a$ and every integer $0\le K\le n/(2B)$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:gevrem}
 \left|R_n-Q(\tau)\sum_{r=0}^Kc_rn^{-r}\right|
 \le A B^{K+1}(K+1)!n^{-K-1}+A'e^{-a\delta n/8}.
\end{equation}
All constants are independent of $K,n,\tau,\sigma$ within these ranges.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The elementary inequality
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:absorb}
 m^r\le r!(4/a)^r e^{am/4}
\end{equation}
comes from retaining one term of the exponential series. Combine it with \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:cauchy}, sum over nonempty partitions, and use $Q(\tau)\ge1$ to get \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:gevcoeff}. The empty partition is handled directly, with $c_0=1$ and $C,Q\ge1$.

For $m\le\delta n/2$, apply \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:absorb} with $r=K+1$ to \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:smallremainder}. Summation gives the first term of \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:gevrem} with the displayed $A$.

For $m>\delta n/2$, the actual weights are bounded by $e^{-am/2}$, hence their sum is at most $Q(a/4)e^{-a\delta n/8}$. For each coefficient use \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:cauchy} and \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:absorb} to obtain
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{m>\delta n/2}\sum_{|\lambda|=m}|e^{-\tau m}B_r|n^{-r}
 &\le C r!(B/n)^r\sum_{m>\delta n/2}p(m)e^{-am/2}\\
 &\le C Q(a/4)e^{-a\delta n/8}r!(B/n)^r.
\end{align*}
The ratio of successive terms in the finite sum from $r=0$ to $K$ is $(r+1)B/n\le KB/n\le1/2$. Its sum is at most two, including $K=0$. Adding the actual-weight tail gives $A'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{pwd:dg:cor:exptrunc}
For all sufficiently large $n$, choose
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Klinear}
 K+1=\floor{\frac{n}{4B}}.
\end{equation}
Then the error on the left of \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:gevrem} is at most
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:exptrunc}
 A4^{-\floor{n/(4B)}}+A'e^{-a\delta n/8}.
\end{equation}
Thus this specified linear truncation is exponentially accurate, uniformly on the compact parameter set.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Write $s=K+1\ge1$. The choice is in the theorem's range, and $s!\le s^s$ implies $(B/n)^ss!\le(Bs/n)^s\le4^{-s}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The coefficient upper bound has Gevrey order one. It is an upper bound and need not describe the true coefficient growth. The scalar bound \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:gevrem} is uniform in $K$; the weighted-$\ell^1$ theorem was stated at fixed $K$. No uniform-in-$K$ weighted-$\ell^1$ result is inferred here. The explicit exponential rate in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:exptrunc} is a proved rate from conservative majorants, not an optimality statement or an identification of separate exponential sectors.
\end{remark}

\section{Total variation and the first correction}\label{pwd:dg:sec:TV}
For probabilities on partitions use $\TV(P,\nu)=\frac12\sum_\lambda|P(\lambda)-\nu(\lambda)|$.

\begin{proposition}\label{pwd:dg:prop:TV}
Uniformly under \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:compact},
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:TVgeneral}
 \TV(P_{n,\tau,\sigma},\nu_\tau)
 =\frac1{2n}\E_{\nu_\tau}|B_1-c_1|+O(n^{-2}).
\end{equation}
On the diagonal, put $q=e^{-1}$ and $\mu=\E_{\nu_1}D_2=-c_1$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:TVdiag}
 \frac12<\mu<2,\qquad
 \TV(P_{n,1,0},\nu_1)
 =\frac{\mu(1+q)-q/2}{Q(1)n}+O(n^{-2}).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
At order one \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:normalized} says
$P_n-\nu_\tau=n^{-1}\nu_\tau(B_1-c_1)+O_{\ell^1}(n^{-2})$.
The inequality $\bigl||x+y|-|x|\bigr|\le|y|$ proves \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:TVgeneral}, including the factor $1/2$.

For the diagonal, the first five terms of the series for $e$ give $e>8/3$, while comparison of the remaining factorial tail with a geometric series gives $e<3$. Thus $1/3<q<3/8$. The first two terms of the positive series for $\mu$ at $q=1/3$ give
\[
 \mu>\frac{q}{2(1-q)}+\frac{3q^2}{1-q^2}\bigg|_{q=1/3}=\frac58>\frac12.
\]
For the upper bound, separate $j=1$ and replace $1-q^j$ by $1-q^2$ for $j\ge2$:
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:muupper}
 \mu\le\frac{q}{2(1-q)}+
 \frac{q(1+q)/(1-q)^3-q/[2(1-q)^2]-q/2}{1-q^2}
 <\frac{27237}{13750}<2.
\end{equation}
The penultimate value is obtained at $q=3/8$. Monotonicity holds because before summation the majorant is a sum of positive increasing terms.

Now $D_2(\varnothing)=0$, $D_2((1))=1/2$, and $D_2\ge2$ on every other diagram: a diagram of size at least two contains $(2)$ or $(1,1)$, each with $D_2=2$, and every added cell has positive contribution. Since $\E(\mu-D_2)=0$, half its absolute expectation is its positive-part expectation, supported just on $\varnothing$ and $(1)$. Their Boltzmann probabilities are $1/Q$ and $q/Q$, so the result is $[\mu(1+q)-q/2]/Q$.
\end{proof}

\section{Analytic bounds for coefficient cutoffs}\label{pwd:dg:sec:cutoff}
In this section only, $\tau=1,\sigma=0$. Let $c_r^{(J)}$ be the multiplicity coefficient with parts at most $J$, as in Section~\ref{pwd:dg:sec:momentengine}. Define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:beta}
 \beta_r=[z^r]\exp\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right).
\end{equation}
In particular $\beta_1=1$, $\beta_2=3/2$, $\beta_3=13/6$ and $\beta_4=73/24$. We use $\beta_r$ here to keep these majorants distinct from the inverse coefficients $b_r$ below.

\begin{proposition}\label{pwd:dg:prop:cutoff}
For $r\ge1$, $0<\theta<1$, and $J+1\ge2r/(1-\theta)$, set
\begin{align}
 U_r(J,\theta)&=\beta_r\exp\left(\frac{\pi^2}{6\theta}\right)
 (J+1)^{2r}e^{-(1-\theta)(J+1)},\label{pwd:dg:eq:U}\\
 V_r(J)&=\beta_r e^{\pi^2/3}(4r/e)^{2r}
             \frac{e^{-(J+1)}}{1-e^{-1}}.\label{pwd:dg:eq:V}
\end{align}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:cutoff}
 |c_r-c_r^{(J)}|\le U_r(J,\theta)+V_r(J).
\end{equation}
For $r=0$, $c_0=c_0^{(J)}=1$, so its error is exactly zero.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a nonempty partition of size $m$, $D_{j+1}\le m^{j+1}\le m^{2j}$. Taking absolute coefficients of the exponential yields
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Bmajorant}
 |B_r(\lambda)|\le\beta_rm^{2r}.
\end{equation}
The Euler product obeys
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:Qbound}
 \log Q(\theta)=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac1{k(e^{\theta k}-1)}
 \le\frac1\theta\sum_{k\ge1}k^{-2}=\frac{\pi^2}{6\theta}.
\end{equation}
If a part exceeds $J$, then $m\ge J+1$. The function $m^{2r}e^{-(1-\theta)m}$ decreases for $m\ge2r/(1-\theta)$. Multiply its maximum on this tail by $\sum_mp(m)e^{-\theta m}=Q(\theta)$ and use \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Qbound}; the unnormalized missing coefficient sum is at most $U_r$.

Let $E_J$ be the event that every part is at most $J$. The finite multiplicity law is $\nu_1(\cdot\mid E_J)$ and $\Prob(E_J)=Q_J(1)/Q(1)$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:conditioning}
 c_r-c_r^{(J)}=E[B_r\ind_{E_J^c}]-\Prob(E_J^c)c_r^{(J)}.
\end{equation}
The first term is bounded by $U_r$, using $Q(1)\ge1$. By independence, or just a union bound,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:probmissing}
 \Prob(E_J^c)\le\sum_{j>J}\Prob(M_j>0)
 =\frac{e^{-(J+1)}}{1-e^{-1}}.
\end{equation}
For $r\ge1$, maximizing $m^{2r}e^{-m/2}$ over $m\ge0$ gives $(4r/e)^{2r}$. Divide the restricted sum by $Q_J\ge1$, split $e^{-m}=e^{-m/2}e^{-m/2}$, and apply \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Bmajorant}:
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:conditionalbound}
 |c_r^{(J)}|\le\beta_rQ(1/2)(4r/e)^{2r}
 \le\beta_re^{\pi^2/3}(4r/e)^{2r}.
\end{equation}
Equations \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:conditioning}--\eqref{pwd:dg:eq:conditionalbound} prove \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:cutoff}. The constant coefficient is handled separately as stated.
\end{proof}

The row-box method admits a closely related bound. A diagram outside $L\times L$ has size at least $L+1$, so for $r\ge1$ and the same condition with $L$ replacing $J$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:boxcutoff}
 \left|c_r-\frac{[z^r]F(1,L)}{Q(1)}\right|\le U_r(L,\theta).
\end{equation}
There is no $V_r$ term because no conditional renormalization is performed. For $r=0$, the error is at most $e^{\pi^2/(6\theta)}e^{-(1-\theta)(L+1)}$. These estimates control the exact finite-cutoff error, not floating-point roundoff.

For $J=200$ and $\theta=0.09$, ordinary high-precision evaluation of $U_r+V_r$ gives approximately
\[
 1.28\cdot10^{-67},\quad 7.76\cdot10^{-63},\quad
 4.53\cdot10^{-58},\quad 2.57\cdot10^{-53}
 \quad (r=1,2,3,4).
\]
The formulas are rigorous analytic bounds. These printed evaluations use ordinary high-precision arithmetic and are not directed-rounding enclosures; neither they nor the computed coefficient decimals certify every displayed digit.

\section{Smooth inversion and integer thresholds}\label{pwd:dg:sec:inverse}
Define, for $Y>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:N}
 N(Y)=\min\{n\ge1:a_n\ge Y\},\qquad L=\log Y.
\end{equation}
The use of $Y>1$ avoids the initial equality $a_0=a_1=1$.

\begin{lemma}\label{pwd:dg:lem:monotone}
The sequence $a_n$ is strictly increasing for $n\ge1$ and unbounded.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Wrap a size-$n$ Dyck path in an outer upstep and downstep. Each old descent height $h$ becomes $h+1$; its factor at exponent $n+1$ is $(h+1)^{n+1}>h^n$. The final descent has height one. This is an injection, so summing proves strict increase. The single path consisting of all upsteps followed by all downsteps has weight $(n!)^n$, proving unboundedness.
\end{proof}

For fixed $K\ge0$ define the smooth approximation
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:HK}
 H_K(x)=x\log\Gamma(x+1)+\log Q(1)+\sum_{r=1}^K\ell_rx^{-r}.
\end{equation}
Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:uniform} and \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:ell} imply
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:integerresidual}
 \log a_n=H_K(n)+O(n^{-K-1}).
\end{equation}
Let $\psi$ be the logarithmic derivative of $\Gamma$. Convexity of $\log\Gamma$ and $\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)$ imply $\log x\le\psi(x+1)\le\log(x+1)$. Stirling's formula \cite{dlmfgamma} therefore gives directly
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:derivative}
 H_K'(x)=2x\log x-x+O(\log x)>0
\end{equation}
for sufficiently large $x$. This argument differentiates the exact Gamma expression, not an uncontrolled remainder. Denote by $x_K(L)$ the unique sufficiently large solution of $H_K(x)=L$.

\begin{theorem}\label{pwd:dg:thm:ceil}
For each fixed $K\ge0$ there is a constant $C_K>0$ such that for all sufficiently large $Y$, writing $x=x_K(\log Y)$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:ceiling}
 \ceil{x-C_Kx^{-K-2}/\log x}\le N(Y)
 \le\ceil{x+C_Kx^{-K-2}/\log x}.
\end{equation}
The constants and lower threshold on $Y$ are existential asymptotic constants, not effective numerical certificates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
On $[x-2,x+2]$, \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:derivative} gives $H_K'\ge c x\log x$, while the integer residual in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:integerresidual} is bounded by $C x^{-K-1}$. Choose a fixed $C_K$ strictly larger than $C/c$ after enlarging the constants uniformly on this interval, and put $\eps=C_Kx^{-K-2}/\log x$. Consider precisely the integers
\[
 m_- =\ceil{x-\eps}-1,\qquad m_+=\ceil{x+\eps}.
\]
For large $x$ they lie in $[x-2,x+2]$, with $m_-<x-\eps$ and $m_+\ge x+\eps$. The derivative lower bound exceeds the possible integer residual at each, so $\log a_{m_-}<L<\log a_{m_+}$. Lemma~\ref{pwd:dg:lem:monotone} gives $m_-+1\le N(Y)\le m_+$, which is \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:ceiling}, including integer endpoints.
\end{proof}

The two ceilings may differ near an integer, even when the radius tends to zero. Thus the theorem does not license the unconditional formula $N(Y)=\ceil{x_K(\log Y)}$. If both ceilings coincide, their common value is the threshold. At a finite input, resolving an ambiguous rounding decision requires a certified residual bound or direct exact comparison with neighboring $a_n$.

\subsection{The Lambert scale and the first three corrections}
Let $W_0$ be the real principal Lambert function, defined by $W_0(z)e^{W_0(z)}=z$ for $z>0$ \cite{dlmfW}. Set
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:t}
 t=\sqrt{\frac{2L}{W_0(2L/e^2)}},\qquad
 u=\log t,\qquad H=2u-1,\qquad A=\frac{u+\log(2\pi)}2,
\end{equation}
so that $t^2(\log t-1)=L$ exactly. Also put $d=1/12+\log Q(1)$. Multiplying Stirling's expansion by $n$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:logdiag}
 \log a_n=n^2(\log n-1)+\frac n2\log(2\pi n)+d
       +\frac{\ell_1}{n}+\frac{\ell_2-1/360}{n^2}+O(n^{-3}).
\end{equation}
The constant $1/12$ and the absence of a Stirling $n^{-1}$ term after multiplication are important for inversion.

For each fixed $K\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:firstinverse}
 x_K(L)=t+\alpha_0(u)+\frac{\alpha_1(u)}t+\frac{\alpha_2(u)}{t^2}+O(t^{-3}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 \alpha_0&=-A/H,\label{pwd:dg:eq:alpha0}\\
 \alpha_1&=-\frac{(H+2)\alpha_0^2/2+(A+1/2)\alpha_0+d}{H},\label{pwd:dg:eq:alpha1}\\
 \alpha_2&=-\frac{(H+2)\alpha_0\alpha_1+\alpha_0^3/3
              +(A+1/2)\alpha_1+\alpha_0^2/4+\ell_1}{H}.\label{pwd:dg:eq:alpha2}
\end{align}
The rational function $\alpha_0(u)$ tends to $-1/4$. Replacing it by that limit loses a correction of order $1/\log t$, larger than every inverse power of $t$. The logarithmic dependence must therefore be retained in a power-scale inverse.

\subsection{An arbitrary fixed-order recurrence}\label{pwd:dg:sec:inverserecurrence}
Let $\mathcal B_j$ denote Bernoulli numbers, to distinguish them from $B_r(\lambda)$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:br}
 b_r=\ell_r+s_r,\quad s_r=0\ (r\ \hbox{odd}),\qquad
 s_{2k-2}=\frac{\mathcal B_{2k}}{2k(2k-1)}\quad(k\ge2).
\end{equation}
Thus $b_1=\ell_1$ and $b_2=\ell_2-1/360$. Hold $u$ fixed and use a new formal variable $\eps$. Define
\begin{align}
 \Psi(\eps,v;u)={}&\eps^{-1}\left\{(1+\eps v)^2
          [u-1+\log(1+\eps v)]-(u-1)\right\}\nonumber\\
 &+\tfrac12(1+\eps v)[u+\log(2\pi)+\log(1+\eps v)]\nonumber\\
 &+d\eps+\sum_{r\ge1}b_r\eps^{r+1}(1+\eps v)^{-r}.\label{pwd:dg:eq:Psi}
\end{align}
The apparent pole is removable and the constant term is $Hv+A$. Starting with \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:alpha0}, put
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:alpharecurrence}
 \alpha_j(u)=-\frac1H[\eps^j]\Psi\left(\eps,
             \sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\alpha_i(u)\eps^i;u\right),\qquad j\ge1.
\end{equation}
Only $b_1,\ldots,b_{j-1}$ enter. The required list is empty when $j=1$; the initial coefficient $j=0$ requires no $b_r$. The coefficient of a newly added $\alpha_j$ at order $\eps^j$ is exactly $H$; every other use of it has higher order. This proves that the recurrence is triangular. Expanding its first terms gives \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:alpha1} and \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:alpha2}.

\begin{theorem}\label{pwd:dg:thm:inverseorders}
Fix integers $J\ge0$ and $K\ge\max(0,J-1)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pwd:dg:eq:inverseorders}
 x_K(L)=t+\sum_{j=0}^J\alpha_j(\log t)t^{-j}+O(t^{-J-1}),
 \qquad L\to\infty.
\end{equation}
Every $\alpha_j$ is a rational function of $u$, bounded as $u\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Induct on $j$ in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:alpharecurrence}. Each numerator is a polynomial in finitely many previous $\alpha_i$ and $b_r$, with at most affine dependence on $u$. If the preceding coefficients are bounded, the numerator is $O(u)$; division by $H=2u-1$ preserves boundedness. Rationality is preserved as well. After choosing a sufficiently large $u_0$, all these fixed rational functions and all their fixed derivatives are bounded on $u\ge u_0$; the only introduced denominators are powers of $H$.

Write $\widetilde x_J=t+\sum_{j=0}^J\alpha_j(u)t^{-j}$. The leading expansion of $H_K(t+v)-L$, uniformly for bounded $v$, is $t(Hv+A)+O(u)$. Taking two fixed sufficiently large displacements of opposite sign proves $x_K=t+O(1)$ by eventual monotonicity. The same bound holds for $\widetilde x_J$.

Use Stirling's formula to an order leaving $O(x^{-J})$ in $H_K(x)$, retaining $b_1,\ldots,b_{J-1}$. The list is empty for $J=0,1$; at $J=0$, the constant $d$ may still be retained with smaller error. Omitted logarithmic correction terms are $O(x^{-J})$ because $K\ge J-1$ when $J\ge1$. Ordinary Taylor remainders in $\log(1+\eps v)$ and $(1+\eps v)^{-r}$ are uniform for bounded $v$ and $\eps=1/t$ small. Their prefactors grow at most as $O(u)$. The recurrence cancels coefficients through $\eps^J$ in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:Psi}, which is the residual divided by $t$, hence
\[
 H_K(\widetilde x_J)-L=O(ut^{-J}).
\]
On the intervening bounded interval about $t$, \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:derivative} gives $H_K'(x)\ge ctu$. The mean value theorem proves the error $O(t^{-J-1})$ in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:inverseorders}.
\end{proof}

This fixed-order expansion approximates a defined smooth inverse. It introduces no unproved interpolation of the discrete sequence. To recover an integer threshold one must use Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:ceil} or exact comparisons; a floating-point root followed by a ceiling is not an integer certificate.

\begin{remark}[{[write]} The inverse and the transseries volume, 7~October 2026]\label{pwd:dg:rem:transseries}
Statement by statement, against the volume named in the Guide (its symbols
carry the subscript ``vol'').
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Instance.} By Lemma~\ref{pwd:dg:lem:monotone}, $a_n$ increases
strictly for $n\ge1$, so $N(Y)$ is the integer staircase of
\file{p0:def:three-inverses} with $n_1=1$ and \file{p0:thm:staircase}(1)
applies.
\item \emph{Analogue.} Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:ceil} is, as stated and
proved, an analogue of \file{p0:thm:staircase}(2): a two-ceiling bracket
proved directly at the integers from the smooth model $H_K$.
\item \emph{Instance.} The scale $t$ of~\eqref{pwd:dg:eq:t}, with
$t^2(\log t-1)=L$, is \file{p0:prop:factorial-core} after the substitution
$X_\vol=t^2$: $X(\tfrac12\log X-1)=L$ has $\kappa_\vol=\tfrac12$,
$d_\vol=-1$, and the proposition's $X=L/(\kappa_\vol v)$ with
$v=W_0(2L\ee^{-2})$ is $2L/W_0(2L/\ee^2)=t^2$. This is the double-factorial
normalization of \file{p0:rem:factorial-core} up to the scale of $L$.
\item \emph{Formal instance, first coefficient checked.} With $x=t(1+E)$ the
core $x^2(\log x-1)$ gives $t^2[HE+h(E)]$ with $H=2u-1$, $u=\log t$, and
$h(E)=(u-1)E^2+(1+E)^2\log(1+E)-E$, so the recurrence~\eqref{pwd:dg:eq:alpharecurrence}
is the triangular recurrence of \file{p0:thm:core-reversion} written for the
additive displacement $v=tE$, with $\Lambda_\vol=H$ and coefficients in a
ring containing $u$; its first coefficient $\alpha_0=-A/H$
of~\eqref{pwd:dg:eq:alpha0} is the volume's $e_1$. The write did not compare
$\alpha_1,\alpha_2$. Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:inverseorders} has its own
residual-over-slope proof, whose last step is \file{p0:thm:backward-error}.
\end{enumerate}
No novelty is claimed for any of these inversions.
\end{remark}

\section{Computational verification and diagnostics}\label{pwd:dg:sec:computations}
The proofs above are analytic and combinatorial. The accompanying programs test identities and normalization, compute finite-cutoff coefficients, and evaluate asymptotic diagnostics. They are not premises for the theorems.

\subsection{Exact checks}
The exact verification suite uses integers, rational numbers and symbolic polynomial operations. It compares three constructions of $Z_n(p)$: direct path enumeration, a height-transfer dynamic program and a finite formal continued fraction. It checks the staircase bijection and row/cell products path by path, the conjugation identities for $D_k$, their size bounds, coefficient polynomials, geometric moments, finite row-box enumeration, and the triangular inverse recurrence. It also compares the first eleven diagonal values with the OEIS entry. The largest initial terms are stored in the machine-readable output rather than typeset as a long digit table.

All checks use explicit conditional failures. They remain active under \texttt{python -O}; the suite does not rely on removable Python \texttt{assert} statements. The canonical result files deliberately omit the interpreter optimization flag so successful normal and optimized runs can be byte-compared. The README specifies the complete build and archive manifest.

\subsection{High-precision diagnostics}
The following values are ordinary high-precision numerical diagnostics, rounded for readability:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}\toprule
Quantity & Approximate value\\\midrule
$Q(1)$ & $1.98244090741287370369$\\
$c_1$ & $-1.72818020667799661965$\\
$c_2$ & $4.37507557565279903972$\\
$c_3$ & $-10.48810716563746719452$\\
$c_4$ & $24.67555987770631379244$\\
$\ell_2$ & $2.88177216227599748368$\\
$[\mu(1+e^{-1})-e^{-1}/2]/Q(1)$ & $1.09965570555819126120$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These coefficients come from the algorithms in Section~\ref{pwd:dg:sec:coefficients}, not from fitting finite-$n$ data. Finite-part and finite-square computations have different normalization and are compared accordingly. Analytic truncation errors and numerical roundoff are distinct: ordinary \texttt{mpmath} arithmetic has not been replaced here by directed interval arithmetic.

For a normalized forward check let
\[
 E_K(n)=n^{K+1}\left(\frac{R_n(1,0)}{Q(1)}-\sum_{r=0}^Kc_rn^{-r}\right).
\]
The theorem predicts $E_K(n)\to c_{K+1}$. A transfer computation without enormous factorial-sized intermediates gives:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrr@{}}\toprule
$n$ & $E_0(n)$ & $E_1(n)$ & $E_2(n)$ & $E_3(n)$\\\midrule
10 & $-1.375520$ & $3.526598$ & $-8.484773$ & $20.033338$\\
20 & $-1.532882$ & $3.905960$ & $-9.382304$ & $22.116058$\\
40 & $-1.624994$ & $4.127452$ & $-9.904941$ & $23.326636$\\
80 & $-1.675084$ & $4.247721$ & $-10.188327$ & $23.982379$\\
160 & $-1.701240$ & $4.310475$ & $-10.336082$ & $24.324094$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For each downstep numbered $d+1$, the transfer divides its height factor by $(n-d)^{p}$. The product of the denominators is $(n!)^p$ for every path, so this computes $R_n$ directly. This normalization is also tested against exact small cases.

For inverse diagnostics choose $L=\log a_n$ and compute the large root of $H_4(x)=L$. These are real-root errors, not threshold errors:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}\toprule
$n$ & $x_4(\log a_n)-n$ & $(x_4-n)n^6\log n$ & $n\TV(P_{n,1,0},\nu_1)$\\\midrule
10 & $-2.95433\cdot10^{-6}$ & $-6.802602$ & $0.988176$\\
20 & $-3.75043\cdot10^{-8}$ & $-7.190586$ & $1.042846$\\
40 & $-4.85625\cdot10^{-10}$ & $-7.337623$ & $1.070987$\\
80 & $-6.41083\cdot10^{-12}$ & $-7.364266$ & $1.085257$\\
160 & $-8.61809\cdot10^{-14}$ & $-7.338070$ & $1.092440$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The TV column approaches the value $1.0996557\ldots$ from Proposition~\ref{pwd:dg:prop:TV}. Its finite-$n$ evaluation checks that only the empty and singleton partitions have positive probability difference in these cases. To justify this shortcut, adding a cell $(i,j)$ multiplies the likelihood ratio $P_n/\nu_1$ by $e(1-1/(n-i-j+2))^n<1$ inside the staircase. Every diagram of size at least two contains $(2)$ or $(1,1)$, which have the same likelihood ratio. Thus checking the singleton and this size-two maximum covers all larger diagrams; excluded diagrams have ratio zero. The general asymptotic theorem does not require that extra finite-$n$ observation. The smooth-root residual scale tests the indexing in \eqref{pwd:dg:eq:integerresidual}. None of these numbers supplies the unknown effective $C_K$ in Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:ceil}.

General-parameter diagnostics also include $(\tau,\sigma)=(0.5,-0.7),(1.3,0.4),(2,1)$, with finite boxes and their sizes stated in the result file. At smaller $\tau$ a fixed box cuts off more mass; its output is not relabeled an exact infinite coefficient. This is why the error analysis is stated separately from numerical agreement.

\subsection{Reproduction contract}
The public archive contains this TeX source and PDF, a build script, pinned Python dependencies, exact and numerical source code, canonical JSON results and a SHA-256 manifest. From its extracted root, \texttt{python build.py} runs the checks, regenerates results, compiles the PDF and recreates the archive. Running \texttt{python -O build.py} performs the same complete work with optimization enabled. Consult \texttt{README.md} for the concrete toolchain and file list. Fixed PDF metadata, sorted archive members and fixed ZIP metadata make outputs deterministic in the recorded environment. Cross-platform byte identity across different TeX engines or dependency versions is not promised.

The archive contains the public mathematics and reproducible computational evidence. Its results distinguish exact checks from numerical tolerance checks, and no successful tolerance test is represented as an interval certificate. No network access is required by the build once the stated dependencies are installed.

\section{Sources and limits of the source screen}\label{pwd:dg:sec:sources}
The source check was bounded and read-only. The OEIS-hosted indexed A291333 record retrieved on 4 October 2026 supplies the definition, diagonal identification and Kot\v e\v sovec attribution. Direct opening of that entry returned a cache miss, so the indexed record is not represented as a fresh uncached revision check. A site-wide footer date is not an entry revision date. The opened A290569 entry confirms the fixed-column continued fraction and the main diagonal; the opened A317103 entry confirms the same product constant in a different model. Attempts to retrieve the neighboring A291332 entry did not yield a usable definition, so no claim about its detailed overlap is made.

Avdoshkin and Dymarsky's weighted-Dyck discussion \cite{avdoshkin}, Appendix C, is relevant prior context for macroscopic weighted-path methods. It studies fixed power-law parameters and selected modifications. It is not used to transfer a fixed-power asymptotic to $p=\tau n+\sigma$; the proofs here instead use the exact partition identity and small-diagram concentration.

A bounded screen of a public report repository \cite{proveit} included identifier searches, cached path listings and a limited local text corpus. Selected nearby weighted-Dyck reports were distinguished by their peak-weight or fixed-exponent models. Neither a negative identifier result nor this limited overlap screen constitutes exhaustive literature or repository clearance. Accordingly, this report re-proves the already recorded leading equivalent and presents the further theorems with explicit hypotheses, without asserting a first proof worldwide or absence of earlier work.

\clearpage
\section{Further questions}\label{pwd:dg:sec:questions}
The proofs leave several concrete problems open.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Approach to the small-$\tau$ boundary.} When $\tau$ decreases with $n$, typical partition size grows and the present compact-parameter bounds deteriorate. Identifying the crossover to a macroscopic path regime requires estimates beyond Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:uniform}.
\item \emph{True high-order growth.} The bound $|c_r|\le CB^rr!$ does not show that factorial growth occurs. Determining late coefficients could identify sharper truncation scales. No conclusion about divergence or Borel summability follows from the current upper bound.
\item \emph{Effective threshold certificates.} Explicit finite-$n$ logarithmic remainders combined with validated Gamma and Lambert computations could turn Theorem~\ref{pwd:dg:thm:ceil} into a numerical certification algorithm with a computable threshold.
\item \emph{Validated coefficient arithmetic.} The analytic cutoff estimates already isolate truncation error. Directed-rounding implementations of the finite moment recurrences could add rigorous arithmetic enclosures and certify selected digits.
\item \emph{Higher distributional terms.} The polynomials $T_r$ give all fixed-order signed-law corrections. Higher TV terms involve sign boundaries of these corrections; the diagonal first-order split is unusually simple because $1/2<\mu<2$.
\end{enumerate}
